Strong Algebras and Radical Sylvester-Gallai Configurations
Rafael Oliveira, Akash Kumar Sengupta

Abstract
In this paper, we prove the following non-linear generalization of the classical Sylvester-Gallai theorem. Let 𝕂 {\mathbb{K}} be an algebraically closed field of characteristic 0 0 and ℱ = { F 1 , ⋯ , F m } ⊂ 𝕂 ⁡ [ x 1 , ⋯ , x N ] \mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}] be a set of irreducible homogeneous polynomials of degree at most d d such that F i F_{i} is not a scalar multiple of F j F_{j} for i ≠ j i\neq j . Suppose that for any two distinct F i , F j ∈ ℱ F_{i},F_{j}\in\mathcal{F} , there is k ≠ i , j k\neq i,j such that F k ∈ rad ⁡ ( F i , F j ) F_{k}\in\rad(F_{i},F_{j}) . We prove that such radical SG configurations must be low dimensional. More precisely, we show that there exists a function λ : ℕ → ℕ \lambda:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}} , independent of 𝕂 , N {\mathbb{K}},N and m m , such that any such configuration ℱ \mathcal{F} must satisfy dim ( span 𝕂 ​ { ℱ } ) ≤ λ ⁡ ( d ) . \dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq\lambda(d). Our result confirms a conjecture of Gupta [ 35 , Conjecture 2] and generalizes the quadratic and cubic Sylvester-Gallai theorems of [ 56 , 49 ] . Our result takes us one step closer towards the first deterministic polynomial time algorithm for the Polynomial Identity Testing (PIT) problem for depth-4 circuits of bounded top and bottom fanins. Our result, when combined with the Stillman uniformity type results of [ 3 , 15 , 23 ] , yields uniform bounds for several algebraic invariants such as projective dimension, Betti numbers and Castelnuovo-Mumford regularity of ideals generated by radical SG configurations.



1
Introduction

In 1893, Sylvester posed a fundamental question in combinatorial geometry (
[
61
]
): given a finite set of distinct points
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that the line defined by any pair of distinct points
v_{i},v_{j}
contains a third point
v_{k}
in the set, must all points in the set be collinear?
This question was answered positively and independently by Melchior and Gallai
[
47
,
29
]
, and is now known as the Sylvester-Gallai (SG) theorem.

Since then, mathematicians and computer scientists have given considerable attention to generalizations of Sylvester’s question
[
19
,
36
,
55
,
39
,
41
,
20
,
50
,
8
,
33
,
17
]
, and results such as the above are known as Sylvester-Gallai (SG) type theorems.
In its most general form, a Sylvester-Gallai (SG) configuration is a finite set of geometric objects which satisfy certain local conditions or dependencies.
For instance, in the original question, the geometric objects are points and the local dependencies are collinear dependencies amongst triples from the set.
The main theme underlying Sylvester-Gallai type problems is the following local-to-global phenomenon: must these local constraints on the geometric objects imply a global constraint on such configurations?
More specifically, the main question of concern is:

Are Sylvester-Gallai type configurations always low-dimensional?

The answer to this question has turned out to be affirmative for various Sylvester-Gallai type problems with linear algebraic constraints.
The above works prove beautiful generalizations of the classical SG theorem such as the colored, robust and higher dimensional linear SG theorems.
For a comprehensive survey on earlier works, we refer the reader to
[
9
]
.

Linear Sylvester-Gallai type theorems have been successfully applied to obtain important consequences in diverse subfields of theoretical computer science.
In algebraic complexity theory, linear Sylvester-Gallai theorems were used in algorithms for Polynomial Identity Testing (PIT) and reconstruction of depth-3 circuits of bounded top fanin
[
16
,
44
,
58
,
57
]
.
In coding theory, linear Sylvester-Gallai theorems were used to prove non-existence of 2-query Locally Correctable Codes over fields of characteristic zero
[
8
]
.

In this paper we prove a non-linear generalization of the SG theorem, which is at the intersection of algebraic geometry, commutative algebra, algebraic complexity theory and combinatorics.
Motivated by the PIT problem for depth-4 circuits, we consider Sylvester-Gallai configurations where the geometric objects are hypersurfaces in a projective space, or equivalently homogeneous polynomials in a polynomial ring
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.
The local constraint is given by radical dependence of triples of polynomials, which implies the following geometric constraint:
for any two distinct hypersurfaces
Y_{i},Y_{j}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{N-1}
in the configuration, there is a third hypersurface
Y_{k}
in the configuration such that
Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\subset Y_{k}
.

We now formally describe such SG configurations.
Henceforth, we adopt the standard notation and use the term
form
to refer to a homogeneous polynomial.
We also let
{\mathbb{K}}
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
and denote our polynomial ring by
S:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
.

Definition 1.1

(Radical Sylvester-Gallai configuration)
.

Let
d
be a positive integer.
We say that a finite set
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\}\subset S
of irreducible forms of degree
\leq d
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration if the following conditions hold:

1.

F_{i}\not\in(F_{j})
for any
i\neq j\in[m]
, (pairwise non-associate forms)

2.

for every pair
F_{i},F_{j}
, there is
k\neq i,j
such that
F_{k}\in\rad(F_{i},F_{j})
. (radical SG condition)

Note that if
d=1
, the definition above specializes to the dual of the linear Sylvester-Gallai condition for points in
{\mathbb{P}}^{N-1}
.
In this case, condition (1) is equivalent to the linear forms being pairwise linearly independent.
For examples of linear and higher degree radical Sylvester-Gallai configurations, we refer to
Section

3.3
.

Our main result is to prove that such SG configurations must be “low dimensional.”
More formally, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2

(Radical Sylvester-Gallai Theorem)
.

There exists an increasing function
\lambda:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
such that
if
\mathcal{F}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, then we have

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq\lambda(d).

That is, the dimension of the
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear span of
\mathcal{F}
is bounded above by a function only of
d
, independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
,
N
and the number of polynomials
m
.

Theorem

1.2
settles a conjecture of Gupta
[
35
, Conjecture 2]
, which is a fundamental first step towards completing Gupta’s program to solve the PIT problem for depth-4 circuits with bounded top and bottom fanins.
Our result generalizes the results of
[
56
,
49
]
, which proved Gupta’s conjecture for
d=2,3
respectively.
For
d=1
, the statement is equivalent to the linear SG theorem over the field
{\mathbb{K}}
, which is one of the ingredients of our proof.

Theorem

1.2
is a consequence of our main technical result (see
Theorem

1.8
), which works in a more general setting of radical SG-configurations of reducible forms in graded unique factorization domains which are quotients of polynomial rings by
strong
sequences.

Our perspective highlights the
geometric
aspects of Sylvester-Gallai problems.
When viewed through the
algebraic and computational
lens (via the algebra-geometry dictionary), one realizes that these problems are intrinsically related to the study of
cancellations or syzygies
in algebraic problems.
In algebraic and computational terms, the Sylvester-Gallai question can be rephrased as:

Must Sylvester-Gallai configurations depend only on “few variables”?

Thus, from an algebraic point of view, the radical Sylvester-Gallai problem is a question about bounding the syzygies of the configuration of polynomials.
A key observation is that Sylvester-Gallai problems can be viewed in light of the commutative algebraic principle of
Stillman uniformity
.
One way to quantify the complexity of a set of polynomials
\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
, is to measure various commutative algebraic invariants of the ideal
I=(F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})
, such as the degree, projective dimension and Betti numbers, among others.
In general, polynomials in
N
variables will have greater complexity as
N
increases.
However, the following principle of so-called Stillman uniformity
[
21
]
emerges from several recent developments in commutative algebra.
If the degree and the number of polynomials is held fixed, then the complexity of polynomials remains bounded even if
N
increases, because they depend on few “variables”
[
3
,
22
,
15
]
.
A model case of this phenomenon is the breakthrough work of
[
3
]
, which proves Stillman’s conjecture by constructing
strong subalgebras
of polynomial rings.

Even though the Sylvester-Gallai question above has a striking similarity with Stillman uniformity, a major difference is that in the commutative algebraic works mentioned above, the bounds necessarily depend on the number of polynomials defining the ideal.
On the other hand, in Sylvester-Gallai type problems the bounds must be independent of the number of polynomials.
Also, note that
Theorem

1.2
provides an uniform bound (independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
,
N
and
m
) on the dimension of the
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear span of
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
, which is much stronger than any Stillman uniformity type bound on other algebraic invariants.
Indeed, we obtain the following consequence of
Theorem

1.2
, which provides uniform bounds for several algebraic invariants of a radical Sylvester-Gallai configuration, via Stillman uniformity.

Corollary 1.3

(Stillman Bounds for Radical Sylvester-Gallai Configurations)
.

There exist functions
\lambda_{\deg},\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}},\lambda_{pdim},\lambda_{\beta_{i,j}}:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, such that the following holds.

If
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, and
I=(F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})
, then the following invariants of
I
are uniformly bounded by functions independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
,
N
and
m
.

1.

degree of
I
, i.e.
\deg(I)\leq\lambda_{\deg}(d)
.

2.

projective dimension of
I
, i.e.
\mathrm{pdim}(I)\leq\lambda_{pdim}(d)
,

3.

Castelnuovo-Mumford regularity, i.e.
\mathrm{reg}(I)\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}(d)
,

4.

the
(i,j)
-th Betti numbers of
I
, i.e.
\beta_{ij}(I)\leq\lambda_{\beta_{i,j}}(d)
.

In fact, several other invariants of
I
such as the number of minimal or associated primes, degrees and number of generators of minimal primes will also be uniformly bounded. We refer to
[
3
,
23
]
for a list of such invariants.

On one hand, Sylvester-Gallai problems demand a much stronger uniform bounds than Stillman’s uniformity.
On the other hand, SG configurations possess many combinatorial and geometric properties.
Hence the challenges posed by the Sylvester-Gallai problem are novel in nature and require new techniques to overcome them.
To that end, we build upon the work of
[
3
]
and generalize their methods to work over quotients of polynomial rings.
We also develop several algebraic geometric tools to prove effective quantitative bounds for commutative algebraic questions appearing naturally in SG type problems.
Our results enable us to carry out an inductive argument by leveraging the combinatorics of the configuration and eventually we reduce the radical SG problem to the robust linear SG theorem.
We expect these techniques to be fundamental for the full resolution of Gupta’s program to obtain a polynomial-time deterministic black-box PIT algorithm for dept-4 circuits with bounded top and bottom fanin.
In the following subsections we discuss how SG problems are motivated from PIT, our results, technical contributions, key ideas and an overview of the proof, some of which are interesting in their own right.

1.1
Polynomial Identity Testing and Gupta’s conjecture

Given an algebraic circuit, the Polynomial Identity Testing (PIT) problem asks to determine whether the circuit computes the identically zero polynomial.
Even though there are efficient randomized algorithms for PIT
[
64
,
54
]
, devising an efficient deterministic algorithm for this problem is one of the main challenges in theoretical computer science.
The PIT problem is also intimately connected to other fundamental questions such as lower bounds for arithmetic circuits
[
40
,
2
,
42
,
16
,
26
,
11
]
and derandomization problems
[
25
,
45
,
48
,
34
,
24
]
.
We refer the reader to the excellent surveys
[
52
,
60
,
53
]
for more on the PIT problem and its applications.
The results of
[
6
,
30
]
show that in order to solve the PIT problem in general, it is enough to solve the problem for low depth circuits such as unrestricted depth-
3
circuits or homogeneous depth-
4
circuits.
Therefore, these classes have received considerable attention in recent years.
In particular, we have seen significant progress towards PIT for depth-
3
circuits via rank bounds for linear SG-configurations
[
16
,
44
,
58
]
.

Sylvester-Gallai configurations are intrinsic to the PIT problem as demonstrated by the following example.
Suppose a polynomial
P\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
has the expression
P=a_{1}\cdots a_{d}+b_{1}\cdots b_{d}+c_{1}\cdots c_{d}
where
a_{i},b_{j},c_{k}
are pairwise non-associate linear forms (in particular,
P
is represented by a depth-
3
circuit of the form
\Sigma^{[3]}\Pi^{[d]}\Sigma
).
If
P\equiv 0
, then whenever
a_{i},b_{j}
vanish there exists a
c_{k}
which also vanishes, in particular
c_{k}\in\mathrm{rad}(a_{i},b_{j})=(a_{i},b_{j})
.
Hence, the sets
R=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{d}\}
,
B=\{b_{1},\cdots,b_{d}\}
,
G=\{c_{1},\cdots,c_{d}\}
form a colored Sylvester-Gallai configuration and by the Edelstein-Kelly theorem
[
19
]
, we have
\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{K}}(R\cup B\cup G))\leq 3
.
Therefore, if
P\equiv 0
, then we may assume that all the linear forms
a_{i},b_{j},c_{k}
depend on three variables after a change of coordinates and reduce the problem to
{\mathbb{C}}[x,y,z]
.
Thus the rank bounds in linear SG theorems imply that the constituent linear polynomials of the circuit depend on few variables, and more sophisticated applications of this idea yielded the PIT algorithms for special depth-
3
circuits.
However, we are still far from obtaining polynomial time deterministic PIT algorithms for unrestricted depth-
3
circuits.

For depth-
4
circuits, the Sylvester-Gallai configurations appearing in a polynomial identity are no longer linear, due to the presence of higher degree polynomials.
Hence the case of depth-
4
circuits is even more challenging.
In
[
10
,
35
]
, it was conjectured that such non-linear SG-configurations should also depend on few variables or have low algebraic rank.
In particular, Gupta
[
35
]
proposed far-reaching non-linear generalizations of the known Sylvester-Gallai type problems and showed that if the posed conjectures are true, then we will have a deterministic polynomial-time black-box PIT algorithm for the model of depth 4 algebraic circuits with constant top and bottom fan-in.
Such a circuit is denoted by
\Sigma^{k}\Pi\Sigma\Pi^{d}
(where
d,k
should be thought of as constants) and it computes a polynomial
P
of the form

P=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}Q_{ij}

(1)

where
\deg(Q_{ij})\leq d
for each
i,j
.

Gupta conjectured that the non-linear Sylvester-Gallai configuration of polynomials appearing in a depth-
4
polynomial identity must also be depend on few variables.
More precisely, if a polynomial
P
, evaluated by a depth-
4
circuit
1
1

1

One needs to assume that the circuit is simple and minimal. For the precise definitions, we refer the reader to
[
58
,
35
]
.
as above, is identically
0
, then the transcendence degree of the set
\{Q_{i,j}\}
is conjectured to be bounded by a function depending only on
k
and
d
.

The first challenge in Gupta’s series of conjectures is the following non-linear version of the linear Sylvester-Gallai problem,
[
35
, Conjecture 2]
:

Conjecture 1.4

(Radical Sylvester-Gallai Conjecture)
.

There is a function
\lambda:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
such that the transcendence degree of any
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}
, is upper bounded by
\lambda(d)
.

Since the transcendence degree of
\mathcal{F}
is upper bounded by the dimension of the
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear span of
\mathcal{F}
, the following stronger version of the conjecture has been considered.

Conjecture 1.5

(Strong Radical Sylvester-Gallai Conjecture)
.

There is a function
\lambda:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}
, we have
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq\lambda(d)
.

In other words, for any
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}
, we must have that
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})
is upper bounded by a function independent of the polynomial ring
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
and the cardinality
|\mathcal{F}|
. In
[
56
]
, Shpilka broke ground on
1.5
, solving it for
d=2
.
After that,
[
49
]
solved
1.5
for
d=3
. In this work, we positively settle
1.5
for any value of
d
, which in turn positively resolves
1.4
.
In this work, our
Theorem

1.2
completely settles this question in the affirmative.

In this paper, we restrict to the case of characteristic
0
, since the conjecture is false in characteristic
p>0
.
In fact, even the linear Sylvester-Gallai theorem is not true over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, as one may take the finite set of linear forms to be all the linear forms in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.
Our
Theorem

1.2
is a consequence of our main technical theorem which holds in the more general setting of reducible forms in
strong
quotients of polynomial rings.
In the following subsection we will describe our main technical theorem and the key ideas behind the proof.

1.2
Main technical results & overview of proofs

We develop an inductive framework for solving
1.5
.
We induct on the degree of the forms in the configuration, eventually reducing the d-radical-SG problem to the robust linear Sylvester-Gallai theorem (see
Theorem

3.2
).
An important issue that arises in the process of reducing degree is that radical SG configurations in polynomial rings do not seem to behave well when we try to reduce the degree.
For this reason, as is often the case in inductive arguments, one needs to start with a stronger hypothesis, which may be harder to prove but amenable to induction.

We will now describe the ideas for reducing the degree of the configuration, along with our generalized definition of radical SG configurations and a high-level overview of our inductive approach.
We will also see how our generalized definition arises naturally in the process.

I. Degree reduction and general radical SG configurations

In the previous works on low-degree SG-configurations
[
56
,
49
]
, the idea to reduce the degree was employed in a special case as follows.

Special case in low degree.
Let
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\}
be a radical SG configuration of degree at most
3
in a polynomial ring
S
. Suppose that there is a small set of linear forms
W=\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\}\subset S_{1}
, such that the ideal
(W)
contains
\mathcal{F}
. Then one proceeds as follows.

•

General projection.

Apply a “general projection” of
W
, sending all forms in
W
generically to a new variable
z
. In other words, consider the composition
S\hookrightarrow S[z]\xrightarrow{\varphi}S[z]
defined by
\varphi(\ell_{i})=\alpha_{i}z
, where
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
are general scalars . Since
\mathcal{F}\subset(W)
, after the projection all forms in
\varphi(\mathcal{F})
are divisible by
z
. Hence for all
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
, one obtains a residual form
G_{i}\in S[z]
where
\varphi(F_{i})=zG_{i}
.

•

Degree reduction.
Show that the set
\{G_{i}|i\in[m]\}\cup\{z\}
forms a SG-type configuration and thereby reduce the degree of the configuration.

•

Lifting.
The general choice of the scalar multiples of
z
allows one to lift the SG-type bound back to the configuration
\mathcal{F}
.

Note that the process above yields a set of forms divisible by
z
, which we will allow in our generalized definition. Furthermore, the residual forms
G_{i}
as above, are not necessarily irreducible. For example, let
F=xu^{2}-yv^{2}\in S:={\mathbb{K}}[x,y,u,v]
, where
W=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{x,y\right\}
.
Consider the projection
x\mapsto\alpha^{2}z
,
y\mapsto\beta^{2}z
, where
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{K}}
. Then we have
F\mapsto z(\alpha u-\beta v)(\alpha u+\beta v)
in the quotient ring
S/(x-\alpha^{2}z,y-\beta^{2}z)
. As this behaviour is unavoidable, one must consider
reducible forms
in the definition of Sylvester-Gallai configurations if one is to use such projections or quotients inductively.

The reason why we would like to work over a larger class of rings (rather than just polynomial rings) is more subtle, and has not appeared in previous works.
In general, it is not necessary that a there is a small set of linear forms
W
such that
\mathcal{F}\subset(W)
, even in the low-degree case.
For example, if
\mathcal{F}
contains a high-rank quadric then
W
must be large.
The previous works navigate this issue by heavily using structure theorems for ideals generated by two quadrics or cubics.
In general, one cannot hope for such a classification or structure theorem for codimension
2
complete intersection ideals of arbitrary degree.

We shift perspective here and employ a new strategy to overcome this challenge.
Given a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}
in
S
, we will
directly
construct a small set of possibly
non-linear
forms
W
such that the ideal
(W)
contains all the highest degree forms in
\mathcal{F}
.
Then we will take a general quotient using
W
and reduce
\mathcal{F}
to a lower degree SG-configuration in a graded quotient of
S
.
This quotient ring will be a UFD, but not necessarily a polynomial ring.
Thus we are led to generalizing the definition of radical SG-configurations in UFDs.

We generalize the definition of radical SG configurations in two key ways. Firstly, we allow the ambient ring to be a finitely generated,
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
\mathbb{K}
-algebra which is a unique factorization domain (UFD). Secondly, we allow the forms to be reducible. However, we require that there is a special linear form
z
such that for any two forms in the configuration, the only common factor is
z
.

Definition 1.6

(Radical Sylvester-Gallai Configuration in a UFD)
.

Let
d
be a positive integer. Let
R=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}R_{i}
be a finitely generated,
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
\mathbb{K}
-algebra with
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
,
R
is generated by
R_{1}
. Suppose that
R
is a UFD.
Let
z\in R_{1}
be a linear form and
\mathcal{F}:=\{z,zF_{1},\ldots,zF_{m}\}\subset R
be a finite set of square-free forms of degree at most
d+1
.
We say that
\mathcal{F}
is a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration if for every
i\neq j\in[m]
:

\gcd(F_{i},F_{j})=1\text{ and }|\rad(zF_{i},zF_{j})\cap\mathcal{F}|\geq 3.

With this definition at hand, we are ready to outline the key steps of our inductive approach.

II. Our inductive approach.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration as above, and
\mathcal{F}_{d}:=\{F_{i}\ \mid\ zF_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\text{ and }\deg(F_{i})=d\}
.
We would like to construct a small set of forms
W
containing
\mathcal{F}_{d}
and
z
.
Once we construct
W
we will apply general quotients to reduce the degree of the configuration, thereby obtaining a
({d-1},y,R^{\prime})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
We outline the steps below.

•

Small set of forms.
We construct a small set of forms
W:=\{z,H_{1},\cdots,H_{r}\}
of degree at most
d
, such that
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
.
Note that the forms in
W
are no longer required to be linear.

•

General quotient.
Let
y
be a new variable. We apply a general quotient given by
z\mapsto\alpha y
and
H_{i}\mapsto\alpha_{i}y^{\deg(H_{i})}
where
\alpha,\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
are sufficiently general.
More precisely, consider the image configuration
\varphi(\mathcal{F})
under the quotient morphism
\varphi:R[y]\rightarrow R^{\prime}:=R[y]/J
, where
J=(z-\alpha y,H_{i}-\alpha_{i}y^{\deg(H_{i})}|i\in[r])
.
Note that
R^{\prime}
is a
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, as
J
is a homogeneous ideal in
R[y]
.

•

Degree reduction.
After taking the reduced parts of the forms in
\varphi(\mathcal{F})
, we obtain a radical SG-configuration of square-free forms
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\varphi(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
in the quotient ring
R^{\prime}
.
Now all the forms in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
are divisible by
y
and the maximum degree of the residual forms in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is
(d-1)
.

•

Lifting.
We show that sufficiently general choice of the scalars
\alpha_{i}
allows us to lift the inductive bounds back to
\mathcal{F}
in
R
.
Furthermore, the cardinality of
W
being small implies that the lifted bound depends only on
d
.

All of the steps above present us with distinct technical challenges, which were not encountered in previous works on low-degree configurations. The first step is crucial and the most challenging one due to following reasons.

First, the cardinality of
W
should be small. More precisely,
|W|
should be bounded above by a function of
d
only, independent of the field
{\mathbb{K}}
, the number of variables
N
and
|\mathcal{F}|
or
|\mathcal{F}_{d}|
.

Second, the radical SG theorem does not hold in arbitrary quotients of polynomial rings even if all the forms in the SG-configuration are of degree
1
(see
Example

3.14
).
Therefore, even if we apply successive general quotients and reduce to a radical SG configuration of degree
1
in a quotient ring
R
, we cannot ensure that such a 1-radical-SG configuration is low-dimensional in general.
The failure of the radical SG-theorem in an arbitrary quotient ring, is due to the fact that an ideal
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
generated by two linear forms
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in R
may not be a prime ideal.
Hence, the condition
\ell_{3}\in\rad(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is not equivalent to the linear SG-condition
\ell_{3}\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\right\}
, unlike the case of linear forms in a polynomial ring.
In fact, one cannot ensure primality of
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
, even if
R
is a UFD, as shown in the following example.

Example 1.7
.

Let
F=x_{1}x_{2}+x_{3}x_{4}+x_{5}^{2}\in S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{7}]
and
R=S/(F)
. Then
R
is a UFD. Consider the images
x_{1},x_{3},x_{5}\in R
. Then we have
x_{5}\in\rad(x_{1},x_{3})
in
R
, but
x_{5}\not\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{x_{1},x_{3}\right\}
. Note that this issue does not arise if the rank of the quadric is high enough. Indeed, let
G=x_{1}x_{2}+x_{3}x_{4}+x_{5}^{2}+x_{6}^{2}+x_{7}^{2}
. Then
S/(G)
is a UFD, and the ideal generated by any two linear forms
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is a prime ideal in
S/(G)
.

Therefore, we need to identify a subclass of UFDs, such that the radical SG theorem holds over rings in this subclass.
Furthermore, in order to strike a balance between the two requirements above, we need a method for constructing a small set of forms
W
with
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
, such that even after a general quotient we obtain a UFD in this subclass.

One major challenge is that
W
needs to be
small
, hence the requirements above are in tension with each other.
For example, if we started with a polynomial ring
R
, we can ensure that the quotient ring
R^{\prime}
is a polynomial ring, by choosing
W
to be a set of linear forms.
But, in that case
W
will not always be small as observed earlier.
Therefore we cannot restrict to work only with polynomial rings.
As hinted in
Example

1.7
above, if the forms in
W
are “high rank” or
strong
enough, then we can expect the quotient to be a UFD where the 1-radical-SG theorem holds.
In this work, we identify the correct subclass to be quotients of polynomial rings by
strong
sequences and our main technical result
Theorem

1.8
proves that the
d
-radical SG theorem holds over such strong quotients.

Now we will discuss the precise notion of strength, our main technical theorem and the ideas for constructing the desired small set of forms.

III. Strength of forms, strong vector spaces & algebras

Let us note two simple yet extremely useful properties of linear forms in a polynomial ring
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.
First, a set of linearly independent linear forms
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\in S
, is also algebraically independent.
Therefore the
{\mathbb{K}}
-subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}]
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring and
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}
can be thought of as variables in this polynomial ring.
Second, the quotient
S/(\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r})
is again a polynomial ring, and hence a UFD.

In general, both the properties above fail for higher degree forms, even for quadrics.
However, we know that if a quadric
Q
is of rank at least
3
, then
S/(Q)
is a UFD
[
37
, Chapter II.6 Exercise 6.5]
.
The notion of strength
2
2

2

Classically known as Schmidt rank.
of a form generalizes the notion of rank for quadratic forms.
In the course of proving Stillman’s conjecture
[
3
]
, Ananyan and Hochster defined strength of forms and proved thoroughgoing generalizations of the properties of linear and quadratic forms above to higher degree forms. We will now discuss the notion of strength and their results below.

Collapse and strength.
Let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
be a polynomial ring and
S_{d}
denote the space of degree
d
forms.
We say that a form
F\in S_{d}
has an
r
-collapse if
F
can be written as
F=G_{1}H_{1}+\cdots+G_{r}H_{r}
where
1<\deg G_{i}<d
for all
i\in[r]
.
The strength of a form
F
, denoted
s(F)
, is the maximum integer
s
such that
F
has no
s
-collapse (hence
F
has a
s+1
-collapse). Linear forms are defined to have infinite strength. We say that a form
F
is
k
-strong if
s(F)\geq k
.

With the above definition,
[
3
, Theorem A]
generalizes the above classical result as follows.
There is an increasing function
A:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
and the number of variables
N
, such that for any form
F\in S_{d}
with
s(F)\geq A(d)
, it holds that
S/(F)
is a UFD.

In order to extend this result to homogeneous ideals generated by more than one form, one needs to quantify the collective strength of a set of forms or a vector space.
Define the
minimum strength
of a vector space
V\subset S_{d}
, denoted by
s_{\min}(V)
, to be the minimum strength of
any nonzero
element of
V
.
Since the generators of a homogeneous ideal can be of different degrees, we also need to define collective/minimum strength of graded vector spaces.
Let
V=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}\subset S
be a graded vector space with
\dim(V_{i})=\delta_{i}
.
Given any function
C:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, we say that
V
is
C
-strong if
s_{\min}(V_{i})\geq C_{i}(\delta)
, where
\delta=(\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{d})
is the dimension sequence of
V
and
C=(C_{1},\cdots,C_{d})
.
We say that a
{\mathbb{K}}
-subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subset S
is a
C
-strong algebra if
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathbb{K}}[V]
, where
V
is a
C
-strong vector space.
Now
[
3
, Theorem A]
shows that there exists an ascending function
A:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
and
N
, such that if
V\subset S
is a
A
-strong vector space, then any basis of
V
is algebraically independent.
Thus the
A
-strong algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring.
Furthermore, we have that
S/(V)
is a UFD.

Now that we have defined strong vector spaces and discussed some of their important properties, we can discuss how we build on them to solve our SG problem.

IV. Main technical theorem

In subsection II, we saw that one can only hope to prove SG type theorems over certain subclasses of UFDs.
In subsection III we saw how strong spaces give rise to special UFDs.
In this section, we consider the subclass of such UFDs given by quotient rings
R=S/(U)
, where
S
is any polynomial ring and
U\subset S
is a sufficiently strong vector space.
Our main technical theorem below shows that general radical Sylvester-Gallai configurations over such strong quotients are low dimensional.

Theorem 1.8

(General Radical Sylvester-Gallai Theorem)
.

For any two positive integers
d,e
such that
e\geq 2
and
d\leq e
, there exist ascending functions
\Lambda_{d,e}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
and
\lambda_{d,e}:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, both independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
and
N
, such that the following holds.

Let
{\mathbb{K}}
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
and
S:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.
Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a
\Lambda_{d,e}
-strong graded vector space and
R=S/(U)
.
If
\mathcal{F}
is a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration for some
z\in R_{1}
, then we have

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq\lambda_{d,e}(\dim(U)).

i.e. the dimension of the
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear span of
\mathcal{F}
is upper bounded by a function of
d,e
and
\dim(U)
, which is independent of the field
{\mathbb{K}}
, the number of variables
N
and the cardinality of
\mathcal{F}
.

The functions
\Lambda_{d,e}
will be larger than the Ananyan-Hochster function
A
described above.
Hence the quotient ring
R=S/(U)
will be a UFD.
Theorem

1.8
shows that if
U
is generated in degree at most
e
, and it is
\Lambda_{d,e}
-strong, then the dimension of the span of any
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration will be uniformly bounded by
\lambda_{d,e}(\dim(U))
for all
d\leq e
.
We note that the condition
d\leq e
is to make the inductive definition of the functions
\Lambda_{d,e},\lambda_{d,e}
simpler and it is not restrictive in practice, as one can replace
e
by
\max{\{d,e\}}
.
Now given any polynomial ring
S
, we may write
S=S[u]/(u)
, where
u
is a new variable.
Thus we recover
Theorem

1.2
with the bound
\lambda(d)=\lambda_{d,d}(1)
.

The proof of
Theorem

1.8
is much more involved than previous proofs of the low-degree cases
d=2,3
. Thus it requires significantly more techniques from commutative algebra and algebraic geometry. We generalize the methods of
[
3
]
to work over strong quotients of polynomial rings. In particular, we generalize the notion of strength to graded algebras and using a notion of
lifted strength
we show that an analogue of
[
3
, Theorem A]
works for strong quotients of polynomial rings (see
Section

5
). However, the construction of the small set
W
in the inductive step is the most involved part of the argument. It is also arguably the most interesting part, because of the interplay of algebraic geometry and combinatorics. We describe some of the ideas for this construction below and refer to
Section

7
for details.

V. Construction of a small set of strong forms.

Let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
. Given a (d, z, R)-radical-SG configuration in a strong quotient ring
R=S/(U)
, recall that our goal is to construct a
small
set of
strong
forms
W
such that
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
, where
|W|
depends only on
d
, independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
,
N
and
|\mathcal{F}|
.
In fact, the
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear span of
W
needs to be sufficiently strong to ensure that successive general quotients are still in the subclass of strong quotient UFDs.

In
[
3
, Theorem B]
, the authors proved the following statement and confirmed Stillman’s conjecture as a consequence.
Given forms
f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\in S_{\leq d}
there exist
s
number of sufficiently strong forms
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\in S_{\leq d}
, such that
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]
, where
s
depends only on
d
and
r
, independent of
N
and
{\mathbb{K}}
.
However, this does not solve our problem even if
R
is a polynomial ring, as the number of forms
s
necessarily depends on the initial number of forms
r
.
Therefore in addition to generalizing the construction above, we also need to exploit the geometric and combinatorial properties of SG configurations by developing algebraic-geometric tools.
We give an overview of some of these results below.

Construction of strong algebras.
The construction of
[
3
]
works with specific strength lower bounds given by the function
A:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
described earlier. We need to have the flexibility to make the forms as strong as we want, due to our successive quotients. We formalize their construction as an iterative process and generalize it to a robust version (see
Lemma

5.15
). In particular, we show that given a vector space
U\subset S
and any function
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, we can construct a
B
-strong vector space
V
with
U\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
, where
\dim(V)
is bounded by a function depending on
B
,
\dim(U)
and
d
. Furthermore, we have the robustness property that any sufficiently strong form in
U
is preserved during the construction and is contained in
V
.

A small set of strong forms.
We will construct the small set of strong forms
W
by iteratively applying a sequence of general quotients as follows.
We start by choosing a small number of forms
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
.
Here
r
is some function of
d
only, and in fact it turns out to be enough to take
r=O(d^{3})
.
Now we apply the construction above to obtain a small, sufficiently strong algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
containing
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
.
Let us say that a form
F\in\mathcal{F}
of degree
d
is good if
F\in(V)
, otherwise we call
F
a bad form.
If
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(V)
then we are done.
Otherwise, we will apply a general quotient using
V
to obtain a SG-configuration
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in a strong quotient ring
R^{\prime}
.
Then we apply the process iteratively on
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in
R^{\prime}
and obtain a strong algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V^{\prime}]\subset R^{\prime}
.
We repeat the process above as long as there exist bad forms in our strong quotient ring.
By our robustness property, these successive general quotients can be realized as a strong general quotient of the polynomial ring
S
.

Note that at each step of the process, the construction reduces the number of bad forms by at least
r
, which was chosen to be a small number. However, using our algebraic geometric results and quantitative bounds described below, we are able to leverage the combinatorics of the SG configuration to ensure that the number of bad forms actually reduces significantly (see
Section

7.1
). More precisely, we show that if a sufficiently strong algebra contains a large number of forms, i.e.
{\mathbb{K}}[V]\cap\mathcal{F}
is large, then we must have that
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(V)
, as desired (see
Lemma

7.4
). Otherwise, in
Lemma

7.5
, we show that we can indeed increase the algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
to another small strong algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V^{\prime}]
, such that the ideal
(V^{\prime})
contains a large fraction of
\mathcal{F}_{d}
.
Therefore, the number of bad forms reduces significantly.
Finally, in
Section

7.2.2
, we show that after
O(d)
number of iterations of the step above, we will end up with all highest degree forms in a ideal generated by a small dimensional strong vector space
V^{\prime}
in a general quotient
S^{\prime}
. Then we can obtain
W
by lifting the generators to
S
.

VI. Algebraic-geometric tools and effective bounds.

Lastly, our strategy naturally leads us to study several interesting algebraic-geometric questions.
We will now describe some of the algebraic-geometric results that we prove, along with our motivation in the context of SG configurations. Our results work over strong quotients of polynomial rings (see
Section

4
), however here we state the version for polynomial rings for simplicity.

Given a sufficiently strong vector space
V
in a polynomial ring
S
and a d-radical-SG configuration
\mathcal{F}\subset S
, we would like to bound the number of bad forms, i.e. the forms
P\in(\mathcal{F}\cap S_{d})\setminus(V)
. In general, there might be many such bad forms. However, a key observation is that if the
{\mathbb{K}}
-span of degree
d
forms in
\mathcal{F}
is high dimensional, then many of the ideals
(P,Q)
tend to be non-radical, where
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}
are both degree
d
forms. Indeed, since
P,Q\in S_{d}
, if
(P,Q)
is radical, then by the SG condition there exists a third form
F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\rad(P,Q)=(P,Q)
. As
\deg(F)\leq d
, we have that
F
must be of degree
d
and
F\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{P,Q\right\}
. Therefore, if most of the ideals
(P,Q)
are radical, then we can expect that a large fraction of
\mathcal{F}\cap S_{d}
is a robust linear SG-configuration. Hence it must have low dimensional
{\mathbb{K}}
-span. We refer the reader to
Section

7
for the precise statements and arguments.

Thus we are naturally led to the following question. Given a form
P\not\in(V)
, how many degree
d
forms
Q\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]\cap\mathcal{F}
can be there such that
(P,Q)
not radical? We first prove the following criterion for radical ideals using discriminants.

Lemma 1.9
.

(see
Lemma

4.27
)
Let
S:={\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
be a polynomial ring. Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
. Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in S
be forms of positive degree such that
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a reduced complete intersection,
P
is reduced and
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
. Suppose that, for all
i\in[r]
such that
P
depends on the variable
z_{i}
, we have
\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P)\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot S
for any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
. Then the ideal
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in
S
.

The result above is a generalization of the fact that a polynomial in one variable is non-reduced iff its discriminant is zero. It also generalizes
[
49
, Lemma 3.21]
, where a similar result was proved for ideals
(P,Q)
with
P,Q
irreducible. As a corollary we obtain the following effective quantitative bound which answers our question above and generalizes
[
49
, Corollary 3.24]
.

Corollary 1.10

(see
Corollary

4.31
)
.

Let
S:={\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
be a polynomial ring. Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
. Let
P\in S
be reduced form of degree
d\geq 1
such that
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
. Then there exist at most
d^{2}(2d-1)
number of reduced forms
Q_{i}\in A
such that
Q_{i},Q_{j}
do not have common factors for
i\neq j
and
(P,Q_{i})
is not radical for all
i
.

We remark that the bound
d^{2}(2d-1)
helps us ensure that a choice of
r=O(d^{3})
works in the construction of small set of forms described earlier. We refer to
Section

7
for precise statements and proofs. Our methods also yield the following primality criterion.

Lemma 1.11

(see
Lemma

4.24
)
.

Let
P,Q
be irreducible forms in
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s},y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
. Suppose that
Q\in{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
and
P
is irreducible in
S/(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
. Then the ideal
(P,Q)
is prime.

In fact,
Lemma

4.24
works with the subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
replaced by any sufficiently strong subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
. In practice, we can apply the criterion as follows. Given irreducible forms
P,Q\in S
, apply
Lemma

5.15
to construct a sufficiently strong algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]\subset S
such that
Q\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
. If
P
is irreducible in
S/(V)
, then the ideal
(P,Q)
is prime.

The results above are stated for a polynomial ring. However, using our construction and properties of strong algebras, we are able to prove the results for strong quotient rings (see
Section

4
and
Section

5
). We achieve this by using a transfer principle which allows us to transfer the results from polynomial rings to strong quotients.

A transfer principle.
Let
R=S/(U)
be a quotient of a polynomial ring
S
, where
U
is sufficiently strong. Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\in R
be a small number of forms. Since
U
is sufficiently strong, then we can construct a strong algebra
A:={\mathbb{K}}[V]\subset R
, such that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\in A
and the algebra
A
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring. Furthermore, the extension
A\subset R
is intersection flat and has several useful properties. For example, for any prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset A
, the extension
{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot R
is a prime ideal in
R
(see
Proposition

4.18
). Now the transfer principle can be stated as follows. In order to prove commutative algebraic statements for the ideal
I=(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
in
R
such as primality, reducedness, number of minimal primes or to bound the number of non-radical pairs in
\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\}
, it is enough to prove the corresponding statement in the polynomial ring
A
(see
Proposition

4.20
).

We employ this transfer principle throughout our work. The requirement of
U
being sufficiently strong can be quantified depending on the number
r
. The larger the number of forms
r
, the stronger
U
needs to be. The precise quantitative requirements for strength of
U
depends on the context of the application. We refer the reader to
Section

4
and
Section

5
for precise statements and details of applications of this transfer principle.

1.3
Related Work

Radical Sylvester-Gallai problems:

The works
[
56
,
49
]
proved radical SG theorems in low degree
d=2,3
. These works heavily relied on structure theorems for complete intersection ideals generated by pairs of quadrics or cubics, and the arguments crucially used that the degree of the forms is
2
or
3
. In general one can not hope for such characterizations for ideals of arbitrary degree. We vastly generalize the methods and ideas of
[
56
,
49
]
to work over strong quotient rings and our proof is completely new as we do not rely on such structure theorems. Some of the results in
Section

4
of this paper are similar to the results in
[
49
]
, where the authors proved weaker versions for irreducible forms in polynomial rings. We generalize these results to reducible forms in strong quotient UFDs. Furthermore, our definition of strong algebras
Section

5
is a robust generalization of wide vector spaces in
[
49
]
. The notion of wide vector spaces worked in degree at most
2
and were specifically tailored to work with SG configurations. Our general definition of strong algebras is more conceptual and the results generalizing
[
3
]
in this paper are of independent interest.

Progress on PIT:

In recent years, we have seen remarkable progress on the PIT problem for constant depth circuits.
In
[
13
]
, the authors give a deterministic
quasi-polynomial
time algorithm for blackbox PIT for depth 4 circuits with bounded top and bottom fanins.
Their approach is analytic in nature, allowing them to bypass the need of Sylvester-Gallai configurations.
In
[
46
]
, the authors give a deterministic
weakly-exponential
algorithm for PIT for these circuits via the hardness vs randomness paradigm for constant depth circuits
[
12
]
.
The hardness result for the determinant, proved by
[
46
]
, coupled with techniques from
[
1
]
, also yields another weakly-exponential time black-box PIT for constant-depth circuits, as is shown in
[
1
, Section 6]
.

Despite all the formidable progress above mentioned, the Sylvester-Gallai type approach of
[
35
]
is the only one so far which could yield deterministic
polynomial-time
black-box PIT algorithms for depth-4 circuits with constant top and bottom fanins.
In
[
51
]
, this Sylvester-Gallai type approach was carried out for depth-4 circuits with top fanin 3 and bottom fanin 2.

1.4
Organization

In
Section

2
, we establish the notation and conventions that will be used throughout the paper.
In
Section

3
, we formally define all variants of Sylvester-Gallai configurations and previous results that we will need in this paper. We also provide examples of Sylvester-Gallai configurations.
In
Section

4
, we establish the results from commutative algebra and algebraic geometry that we will need.
In
Section

5
, we formally define strength of forms and one of our main tools to solve the Sylvester-Gallai problem: strong Ananyan-Hochster vector spaces and algebras. We prove useful properties of these algebras generalizing the results of
[
3
]
.
In
Section

6
, we formally define general quotients, and prove their technical properties. We also study how Sylvester-Gallai configurations behave under general quotients and how we can lift dimension bounds from general quotients to the original configurations.
In
Section

7
we combine all the results from the previous sections to prove our main technical result and its consequences and thereby resolve
1.4
.

2
Notations and Conventions

In this section, we establish the notation which will be used throughout the
paper and some important background which we shall need to prove our claims
in the next sections.

We will work over an algebraically closed field
{\mathbb{K}}
with
\textnormal{char}({\mathbb{K}})=0
. We will denote by
{\mathbb{N}}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
, the set of natural numbers including
0
. For any positive integer
n
, we let
[n]=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
. Throughout the paper, we will have
S:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{0},\cdots,x_{N}]
a polynomial ring for some positive integer
N
. Note that,
S
is a
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra with the standard grading by degree
\deg(x_{i})=1
for all
i\in[N]
.
That is,
\displaystyle S=\oplus_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}}S_{d}
, where
S_{d}
is the
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space of homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
.
As it is standard, we will use
form
when we refer to a homogeneous polynomial. Let
R=\oplus_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}}R_{d}
be a graded ring and
I\subset R
an ideal. We say that
I
is a homogeneous ideal iff
I
is a graded submodule of
R
. If
I\subset R
is a homogeneous ideal, then the quotient ring
R/I
is also a graded ring where
(R/I)_{d}=R_{d}/I_{d}
.
A graded vector space of homogeneous elements is a subspace
V\subset R
generated by homogeneous elements from
R
, thereby inheriting the grading from
S
, i.e.
V=\oplus_{d\geq 0}V_{d}
, where
V_{d}=V\cap S_{d}
.

Recall that
S
is a unique factorization domain (UFD).
Given a unique factorization domain
R
and
F,G\in R
, we denote by
\gcd(F,G)
a generator of the unique minimal principal ideal containing
F,G
. Note that
\gcd(F,G)
is unique up to a scalar multiple.

For any two functions
f,g:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, we write that
f=O(g)
if there exists some constant
C
and
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
f(n)\leq Cg(n)
for
n\geq n_{0}
. Similarly, we write
f=\Omega(g)
if if there exists some constant
C
and
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
f(n)\geq Cg(n)
for
n\geq n_{0}
. Furthermore, we say that
f=\Theta(g)
if there exists constants
C_{1},C_{2}
and
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that we have
C_{1}g(n)\leq f(n)\leq C_{2}g(n)
for all
n\geq n_{0}
.

3
Sylvester-Gallai Configurations

In this section we formally describe all the generalizations of Sylvester-Gallai configurations that we will need for the rest of the paper, and state the main results that we need from previous works.

To simplify the definitions of our configurations, we say that two forms
F,G\in S
are
non-associate forms
iff
F
is not a scalar multiple of
G
(and vice-versa).
In this section (and in general in all the sets of forms we study), our SG configurations will be made of sets of pairwise non-associate forms.

3.1
Linear Sylvester-Gallai Configurations

Definition 3.1

(Robust linear SG configuration)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{m}\}\subset S_{1}
be a finite set of pairwise non-associate linear forms in
S
and let
\delta\in(0,1]
.
We say that
\mathcal{F}
is a
{\delta}\textnormal{-linear-SG}
configuration if the following condition holds:

1.

for every
i\in[m]
, there exist at least
\delta(m-1)
values of
j\neq i
such that
|\mathcal{F}\cap\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\right\}|\geq 3
.

If
\delta=1
, then we simply call it a linear SG configuration.

It was proved in
[
8
,
17
,
14
]
that the dimension of the span of a
{\delta}\textnormal{-linear-SG}
configuration is bounded by a function depending only on the robustness parameter
\delta
.
We now state the sharpest known result, from
[
14
, Theorem 1.6]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a
{\delta}\textnormal{-linear-SG}
configuration, then
\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq\left\lceil\frac{4}{\delta}\right\rceil-1.

We will need the following strengthening of the result above, which comes from considering a slightly more general type of linear SG configurations, where we also allow certain SG pairs
(\ell_{i},\ell_{j})
to intersect non-trivially a small dimensional vector space, instead of spanning a third element of the configuration.

Definition 3.3

(Robust linear Sylvester-Gallai configurations over a vector space)
.

Let
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
0<\delta\leq 1
and
\mathcal{F}:=\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m}\}\subset S_{1}
be a set of pairwise non-associate linear forms.
We say that
\mathcal{F}
is a
({c},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration if there exists a vector space
W\subset S_{1}
of dimension at most
c
such that the following condition holds:

•

for any
\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\setminus W
, there exist at least
\delta(m-1)
indices
j\in[m]\setminus\{i\}
such that
\ell_{j}\not\in W
and

|\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}|\geq 3\ \ \ \text{ or }\ \ \ \textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\right\}\cap W\neq 0.

It was noted in
[
56
, Corollary 16]
, that a
(1,\delta)
-linear SG-configuration has dimension bounded by
50/\delta
.
The proof of
[
56
, Corollary 16]
uses
[
17
, Theorem 1.9]
which states that a
\delta
-linear SG-configuration has dimension bounded by
12/\delta
.
However, using the sharper bound of
Theorem

3.2
, the same argument yields the following version of
[
56
, Corollary 16]
.

Proposition 3.4
.

A
({1},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration has dimension at most
1+8/\delta
.

The following proposition generalizes
[
56
, Corollary 16]
and
Proposition

3.4
to show that the dimension of a
(c,\delta)
-linear SG configuration is also bounded.

Proposition 3.5

(Robust Linear SG Configurations)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
({c},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration.
Then
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq c+1+8/\delta
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\}
, and
W\subset S_{1}
be the vector space given by
Definition

3.3
.
Let
\varphi:=\varphi_{W,\alpha}:S\rightarrow S[z]
be a general quotient, and let
y_{i}=\varphi(\ell_{i})
.
Hence, we have
\varphi(W)=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{z\right\}
, and by item 3 of
Proposition

6.5
,
\ell_{i}\not\in W\Rightarrow y_{i}\not\in(z)
.
Moreover, by
Proposition

6.6
, for any pair
\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\in\mathcal{F}\setminus W
, we have
y_{i}\not\in(y_{j})
.
In particular, this implies that
\varphi(\mathcal{F})
is a
({1},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration, which by
Proposition

3.4
implies
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi(\mathcal{F})\right\}\leq 1+8/\delta
.
Since
\mathcal{F}\subset\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi(\mathcal{F}),z,W\right\}
, we have
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq c+1+8/\delta
.
∎

The next proposition proves that if one has a set of pairwise non-associate forms which is almost a
{\delta}\textnormal{-linear-SG}
configuration, then it must be a
({c},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration for some small
c
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\mathcal{F}\subset S_{1}
be a finite set of pairwise non-associate linear forms with
|\mathcal{F}|:=m
.
Suppose that there exist
r
forms
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\in\mathcal{F}
such that for any
F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\}
, there exists at least
2\delta m
forms
G_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
|\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,G_{i}\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}|\geq 3
.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is an
({r},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration.

Proof.

Let
W=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\right\}
.
As the elements of
\mathcal{F}
are pairwise non-associate, no two elements of
\mathcal{F}
are scalar multiples of each other.
For each element
F\in\mathcal{F}
, let

\mathcal{F}_{span}(F):=\{G\in\mathcal{F}\ \mid\ |\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,G\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}|\geq 3\}.

If
F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus W
then
F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\}
.
Hence
|\mathcal{F}_{span}(F)|\geq 2\delta m
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{span}(F)=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\}\sqcup\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{t}\}
, where
G_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\setminus W
and
H_{j}\in W
for all
i\in[s],j\in[t]
.

To prove that
\mathcal{F}
is an
({r},{\delta})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration, it is enough to show that
s\geq t
, as this implies
2s\geq s+t\geq 2\delta m\Rightarrow s\geq\delta m
.
Note that for any
H_{j}
, there must exist
G_{i_{j}}\in\mathcal{F}_{span}(F)\setminus W
such that
G_{i_{j}}\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,H_{j}\right\}
.
Additionally, we have
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,H_{j}\right\}\cap\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,H_{k}\right\}=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F\right\}
, otherwise we would have
F\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{H_{j},H_{k}\right\}\subset W
, which is a contradiction.
Hence, the above proves that
G_{i_{j}}\neq G_{i_{k}}
for every
j\neq k\in[t]
, which implies that
s\geq t
, as we wanted.
∎

3.2
Radical Sylvester-Gallai configurations over quotient rings

We now define generalizations of the previous definitions of SG configurations to the setting of finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras which are UFD.
To motivate our general definition, we restate Gupta’s original generalization, from
[
35
, Conjecture 2]
.
3
3

3

Definition

3.7
is a restatement of
Definition

1.1
with the new notation.

Definition 3.7

((Basic) Radical Sylvester-Gallai Configuration)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\}\subset S
be a subset of irreducible, pairwise non-associate forms of degree at most
d
.
We say that
\mathcal{F}
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration if for every
i,j\in[m]
, we have that
|\rad(F_{i},F_{j})\cap\mathcal{F}|\geq 3.

Note that a
{1}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration is the classical linear SG configuration.

See
1.6

Note that in the definition above we require that the linear form
z
is in the set
\mathcal{F}
.
This is simply a minor convention which will allow these configurations to be preserved under special quotients that will be defined in
Section

6
.
We remark that a
({1},z,S[z])\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration recovers the
({1},{1})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration, which was first defined in
[
56
, Corollary 16]
.

Remark 3.8
.

Since each form in a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}
is divisible by
z
, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{e}
the set of forms
F\in R_{e}
such that
zF\in\mathcal{F}
.
With this convention and the previous remark, we define
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\{1\}
.

The next proposition shows that
Definition

1.6
recovers

Definition

3.7
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Note that if we consider the ring
S[z]
, where
z
is free over
S
,
U=(0)
, and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}\in S
are irreducible forms of degree
\leq d
, then the set
\mathcal{F}:=\{z,zG_{1},\ldots,zG_{m}\}
is a
({d},z,S[z])\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration if, and only if,
\mathcal{G}:=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}\}
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, then condition 1 of
Definition

1.6
is satisfied since
\gcd(G_{i},G_{j})=1
for any
i\neq j
.
The SG condition on
\mathcal{G}
implies that there is
G_{k}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
G_{k}\in\rad(G_{i},G_{j})
, which implies
zG_{k}\in\rad(zG_{i},zG_{j})
.
Thus,
\mathcal{F}
is a
({d},z,S)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.

Conversely, if
\mathcal{F}
is a
({d},z,S)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, then the gcd condition of
Definition

1.6
implies
\gcd(G_{i},G_{j})=1
for
i\neq j
, which in particular implies
G_{i},G_{j}
are non-associate.
Since
z
is free over
S
and
G_{i}\in S
, we have that
z,G_{i},G_{j}
is a regular sequence for any
i\neq j
, as
G_{i},G_{j}
are non-associate.
Then, the radical condition of
Definition

1.6
implies there exists
zG_{k}\in\rad(zG_{i},zG_{j})\subset\rad(G_{i},G_{j})
, and since
z,G_{i},G_{j}
is a regular sequence, we must have
G_{k}\in\rad(G_{i},G_{j})
, which implies
\mathcal{G}
is a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
∎

As we will see in
Section

7
, for special choices of vector spaces
U
, i.e. the strong vector spaces that we define in
Section

5
, we are able to prove that these more general SG configurations must span a small dimensional vector space.

3.3
Examples of Sylvester-Gallai configurations

In this section we discuss some examples of Sylvester-Gallai configurations. Note that due to the classical Sylvester-Gallai theorem over
{\mathbb{C}}
or by
Theorem

3.2
, we know that any linear SG-configuration or a
1
-linear SG-configuration has
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq 3
. Therefore, if we projectivize
S_{1}
and consider linear forms up to scalar multiples as points in the projective space
{\mathbb{P}}^{N-1}
, then all linear SG-configurations must be planar. We recall a few examples of such configurations below.

Example 3.10

(Hesse configuration)
.

Let
C\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a non-singular cubic curve.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of inflection points of
C
.
Then
|\mathcal{H}|=9
and the line joining any two inflection points passes through another inflection point. Therefore
\mathcal{H}
is a linear SG-configuration.

More generally, we have the Fermat configuration of cardinality
3n
for
n\geq 3
.

Example 3.11

(Fermat configuration)
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
C=V(x^{n}+y^{n}+z^{n})\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of inflection points of
C
. Let
\omega
be a primitive
n
-th root of
-1
.
Then we have
\mathcal{F}=\{[0:1:\omega^{j}],[\omega^{j}:0:1],[1:\omega^{j}:0]|j\in[n]\}
. Hence the line joining any two points in
\mathcal{F}
contains a third point from
\mathcal{F}
(see
[
7
, Example 1]
).

The following example of cardinality
12
was constructed in
[
43
]
.

Example 3.12

(Kelly-Nwankpa configuration)
.

Let
i=\sqrt{-1}
.
Let
a=\frac{1+i}{2}
and
b=\frac{1-i}{2}
. Furthermore, let
p_{1}=[0:0:1]
,
p_{2}=[0:1:1]
,
p_{3}=[1:0:1]
,
p_{4}=[1:1:1]
,
p_{5}=[a:a:1]
,
p_{6}=[a:b:1]
,
p_{7}=[b:a:1]
and
p_{8}=[b:b:1]
.
Let
p_{9},p_{10},p_{11},p_{12}
be intersection points of the line at infinity and the lines joining
p_{1}
with
p_{2},p_{3},[1:i:1],[1:-i:1]
respectively.
Then the line joining any
p_{i},p_{j}
contains a third point
p_{k}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{12}\}
forms a SG configuration (see
[
43
, Theorem 3.10]
)

Now we consider examples of radical SG configurations of polynomials. Recall that for linear forms a
1
-radical SG configuration is a classical linear SG-configuration. Therefore, the previous examples are also examples of
1
-radical SG configurations in a polynomial ring
S
. Note that, if
\mathcal{F}\subset S
is a
1
-radical SG-configuration, then
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq 3
.

The following is an example of a non-linear
d
-radical-SG configuration for higher degree forms in
{\mathbb{K}}[x,y,z]
.

Example 3.13
.

Fix an integer
m\geq 6
. Let
F_{1}=x
,
F_{2}=y
,
F_{3}=xz+y^{2}
and
F_{4}=x(xz+y^{2})+y^{3}
. For
5\leq k\leq m
, we define
F_{k}
recursively as
F_{k}=F_{3}\cdots F_{k-1}+y^{\sum_{i=3}^{k-1}\deg(F_{i})}
. Note that
F_{i}
is homogeneous for
i\in[m]
and
\deg(F_{m})=\Theta(2^{m})
.

We have that
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\}
is a
d
-radical SG-configuration in
{\mathbb{K}}[x,y,z]
where
d\Theta(2^{m})
.
Indeed, note that
F_{i}\in(x,y)\cap(y,z)
for all
i\in[3,m]
.
In particular,
F_{3}\in\rad(F_{1},F_{2})=(x,y)
. Also, by induction we see that for all
i\in[3,m]
, we have
F_{i}
is congruent to a non-zero scalar multiple of
y^{\deg(F_{i})}
modulo
(x)
. Therefore
\rad(F_{1},F_{i})=(x,y)
and
F_{2}=y\in\rad(F_{1},F_{i})
for all
i\in[3,m]
. Similarly, for all
i\in[3,m]
, we have
F_{i}
congruent to a non-zero scalar multiple of
x^{a_{i}}z^{b_{i}}
modulo
(y)
for some positive integers
a_{i},b_{i}
.
Hence
\rad(y,F_{i})=(x,y)\cap(y,z)
for all
i\in[3,m]
.
Hence, for all
j\in[3,m]
there exists
k\neq j
such that
F_{k}\in\rad(F_{2},F_{j})
. Now, we consider pairs
F_{i},F_{j}
where
i,j\in[3,m]
.
Suppose
i<j
, then
F_{j}
is congruent to
y^{\deg(F_{j})}
modulo
(F_{i})
. Hence we have that
y\in\rad(F_{i},F_{j})
.

Furthermore, note that
\deg(F_{i})\neq\deg(F_{j})
for any
i\neq j\in[2,m]
. Hence, we have that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
are linearly independent and
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})=\Omega(\mathrm{log}_{2}(d))
.

We note that the radical SG-theorem does not hold in arbitrary quotients of polynomial rings.

Example 3.14
.

Let
H_{i}=x_{i}x_{i+1}-x_{i+2}^{2}\in S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{2r}]
for
i\in[2r]
, where we denote
x_{2r+1}:=x_{1}
and
x_{2r+2}:=x_{2}
. Consider the ideal
J=(H_{1},\cdots,H_{2r})
and the quotient
R=S/J
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\{x_{1},x_{3},\cdots,x_{2r-1}\}\subset R
. Then
x_{i},x_{j}
are pairwise linearly independent for
i\neq j
. Furthermore,
x_{2i+1}\in\rad(x_{2j+1})
for all
i,j
. Hence, for all
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathcal{F}
there exists
k\neq i,j
such that
x_{k}\in\rad(x_{i},x_{j})\cap\mathcal{F}
. However,
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})=r-1
.

4
Algebraic geometry

In this section we establish the necessary definitions and develop our algebraic-geometric toolkit. We refer the reader to the standard sources of
[
5
,
18
,
37
,
59
]
for in-depth discussions of the notions in this section.

4.1
Regular, prime and
\mathcal{R}
\eta
-sequences

Definition 4.1

(Regular sequence)
.

Let
R
be ring and
M
an
R
-module. A sequence of elements
F_{1},F_{2},\cdots F_{n}\in R
is called an
M
-regular sequence if

(1)

(F_{1},F_{2},\cdots,F_{n})M\neq M
, and

(2)

for
i=1,\cdots,n
,
F_{i}
is a non-zerodivisor on
M/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{i-1})M
.

If
M=R
, then we simply call it a regular sequence.

If
P
and
Q
are two irreducible polynomials in the polynomial ring
S
such that
P
does not divide
Q
, then
P,Q
is an
S
-regular sequence.
Indeed, since
P
is irreducible, we know that
S/(P)
is a integral domain.
Therefore
Q
is a non-zero element in
S/(P)
, and hence a non-zero divisor.

If
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is a regular sequence of forms in
S
, then
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
are algebraically independent.
Thus the subalgebra generated by
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring.
In particular, the homomorphism
k[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]\rightarrow S
defined by
y_{i}\mapsto F_{i}
is an isomorphism onto its image.

Even though the
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}]\subset S
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring, its elements may not “behave well” when seen as elements of
S
.
We next present a sufficient condition which will ensure that the subalgebra is well behaved with respect to
S
, which we describe in
Section

4.3
.

Definition 4.2

(Serre’s
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-property)
.

Let
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We say that a Noetherian ring
R
satisfies the
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
property if the local ring
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a regular local ring for all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}\subset R
such that
\mathrm{height}(\mathfrak{p})\leq\eta
.

Definition 4.3

(Prime and
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequences)
.

Let
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
R
a Noetherian ring.
A sequence of elements
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}\in R
is called a prime sequence (respectively an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence) if

1.

F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is a regular sequence, and

2.

R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{i})
is an integral domain (respectively, satisfies the
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
property) for all
i\in[n]
.

Remark 4.4
.

Since prime sequences and
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequences are regular sequences, we know that if
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is a prime sequence or
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in the polynomial ring
S
, then
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
are algebraically independent.

We note the following simple statement about radicals and regular sequences.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let

R
be an integral domain. Then we have the following.

1.

If
F,P_{1},P_{2}
is a regular sequence in
R
and
G\in R
, then
FG\in\rad{(FP_{1},FP_{2})}
iff
G\in\rad{(P_{1},P_{2})}
.

2.

Suppose
R
is a UFD. Let
F,G\in R
be non-units such that
(F,G)\neq R
. Then,
F,G
is a regular sequence in
R
if and only if
F,G
do not have any common factors in
R
.

Proof.

1. If
G\in\rad(P_{1},P_{2})
, then
FG\in\rad(FP_{1},FP_{2})
.
Conversely, if
FG\in\rad(FP_{1},FP_{2})
, then we have
FG\in\rad(P_{1},P_{2})
, since
\rad(FP_{1},FP_{2})\subset\rad(P_{1},P_{2})
.
Hence there is
D\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
such that
F^{D}G^{D}\in(P_{1},P_{2})
.
Since
F
is a non-zero divisor in
R/(P_{1},P_{2})
, we conclude that
G^{D}\in(P_{1},P_{2})
.

2. Since
R
is a domain, we have that
F
is a non-zero divisor in
R
. If
FP=GQ
for some
P,Q\in R
. Then by unique factorization we must have
F\mid Q
, if
F,G
do not have any common factors. Therefore,
F,G
is a regular sequence in
R
. Conversely, if
F=PF_{1}
and
G=PG_{1}
for some irreducible factor
P
, then we have
GF_{1}=G_{1}F\in(F)
. However,
F\not\mid F_{1}
, as
P
is a non-unit.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
be a polynomial ring and
z
be a new variable. For any polynomial
F\in S
, let
F^{*}\in S[z]
denote the homogenization of
F
with respect to
z
. Then we have the following.

1.

If
F\in S
is irreducible in
S
, then
F^{*}
is irreducible in
S[z]
.

2.

Let
P,Q\in S
be non-constant polynomials. If
P,Q
do not have any common factors in
S
, then
P^{*},Q^{*}
do not have any common factors in
S[z]
.

3.

Let
P,Q\in S
such that
P,Q
is a regular sequence in
S
. Then the number of minimal primes over
(P,Q)
in
S
is at most
\deg(P)\deg(Q)
.

Proof.

1. For any homogeneous polynomial
G\in S[z]
, let
G_{*}\in S
be the dehomogenization of
G
, obtained by setting
z=1
. Note that
z
does not divide
F^{*}
. If
F^{*}=GH
in
S[z]
, then
G,H
are both homogeneous. By
[
28
, Section 2.6]
we obtain
F=(F^{*})_{*}=G_{*}H_{*}
. Since
F
is irreducible, we may assume that
G_{*}=F
and
H_{*}\in{\mathbb{K}}
. This is a contradiction, since
H=(H_{*})^{*}
, as
z
does not divide
H
.

2. Let
P=\Pi_{i}f_{i}
and
Q=\Pi_{j}g_{j}
be irreducible factorizations of
P,Q
in
S
. Then by
[
28
, Section 2.6]
, we have that
P^{*}=\Pi_{i}f_{i}^{*}
and
Q^{*}=\Pi_{j}g_{j}^{*}
. By part (1), we note that
f_{i}^{*},g_{j}^{*}
are irreducible in
S[z]
. If
P^{*},Q^{*}
have a common factor, say
f_{i}^{*}=g_{j}^{*}
for some
i,j
. Then we have
f_{i}=(f_{i}^{*})_{*}=(g_{j}^{*})_{*}=g_{j}
, which is a contradiction.

3. By
Lemma

4.5
, we know that
P,Q
do not have any common factors. Hence, by part (2), we conclude that
P^{*},Q^{*}
also do not have any common factors in
S[z]
. Since
P^{*},Q^{*}
are homogeneous of positive degree, we see that
P^{*},Q^{*}
is a regular sequence in
S[z]
by
Lemma

4.5
. Note that the minimal primes over
(P,Q)
in
S
are in one-to-one correspondence with the irreducible components of
V(P,Q)\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{N}
. Furthermore, the irreducible components of
V(P,Q)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the irreducible components of
V(P^{*},Q^{*})\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{\dim(W)}
which are not contained in the hyperplane
V(z)
. Now the total number of irreducible components of
V(P^{*},Q^{*})
is at most
\deg(V(P^{*},Q^{*}))
. Since
P^{*},Q^{*}
is a homogeneous regular sequence in
S[z]
, we know that
\deg(V(P^{*},Q^{*}))=\deg(P^{*})\deg(Q^{*})=d^{2}
, by Bezout’s theorem
[
27
, Proposition 8.4]
.
∎

4.2
Homogeneous system of parameters and Cohen-Macaulay rings

In this subsection, we recall the standard definitions and properties of homogeneous system of parameters and Cohen-Macaulay rings.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
R=\oplus_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}R_{i}
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra such that
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
.
A sequence of homogeneous elements
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}\in R
is a homogeneous system of parameters, denoted by
h.s.o.p.
​, if
n=\dim(R)
and
\dim(R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}))=0
.

If
R
such a graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, an
h.s.o.p.
always exists.
In particular, more is true:

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
R=\oplus_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}R_{i}
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra such that
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
.
Suppose the Krull dimension of
R
is
\dim(R)=n
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}=\oplus_{i\geq 1}R_{i}
be the homogeneous maximal ideal.
An
h.s.o.p.
always exists. Moreover, for any sequence of forms
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
of positive degree in
R
, the following are equivalent:

1.

F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is a homogeneous system of parameters.

2.

{\mathfrak{m}}
is nilpotent modulo
(F_{1},\cdots,F_{n})
.

3.

R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{n})
is finite dimensional as a
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space.

4.

R
is module-finite over the subring
A={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
, i.e.
R
is a finitely generated
A
-module.

Moreover, whenever these conditions hold,
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
are algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{K}}
, so that
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
is a polynomial ring.

Definition 4.9

(Cohen-Macaulay)
.

A local ring
(R,{\mathfrak{m}})
of
\dim(R)=n
, is Cohen-Macaulay if there exists a regular sequence
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}\in{\mathfrak{m}}
of length
n
. A Noetherian ring
R
is Cohen-Macaulay if
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a Cohen-Macaulay for all maximal (equivalently prime) ideals
{\mathfrak{p}}
in
R
.

We have the following characterization of Cohen-Macaulay graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
R=\oplus_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}R_{i}
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra such that
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
. Suppose
\dim(R)=n
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}=\oplus_{i\geq 1}R_{i}
be the homogeneous maximal ideal. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

Some homogeneous system of parameters is a regular sequence.

2.

Every homogeneous system of parameters is a regular sequence.

3.

For some
h.s.o.p.
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
, the ring
R
is a module-finite free-module over
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
.

4.

For every
h.s.o.p.
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
, the ring
R
is a module-finite free-module over
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
.

5.

R
is Cohen-Macaulay.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
R
be a Cohen-Macaulay
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra,
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
be an
h.s.o.p.
for
R
and
{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\subset R
.
Then there exist homogeneous elements
u_{1},\cdots,u_{r}\in R
that generate
R
as a free
{\mathcal{A}}
-module and we have an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules
R\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{\oplus r}
such that the composition
{\mathcal{A}}\subset R\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{\oplus r}
is an isomorphism onto one of the direct summands of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\oplus r}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
.
By
Theorem

4.8
,
V=R/{\mathfrak{m}}R
is a finite dimensional graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space.
Let
u_{1},\cdots,u_{r}\in R
be homogeneous lifts of a homogeneous basis of
V
.
Then,
u_{1},\cdots,u_{r}
form a free basis of
R
as an
{\mathcal{A}}
-module.
Hence we have an isomorphism
R\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{\oplus r}
given by
u_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
.
Furthermore, we may assume that
u_{1}=1\in R
, therefore the inclusion
{\mathcal{A}}\subset R
is an isomorphism onto a direct summand of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\oplus r}
.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
be a sequence of forms in
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.

1.

If
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
is a regular sequence in
S
, then
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is a Cohen-Macaulay ring.

2.

If
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is a prime sequence, then
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is a Cohen-Macaulay domain for all
r\in[n]
.
The images of
F_{r+1},\cdots,F_{n}
in
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
form a prime sequence.

3.

Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}\in S
be an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence of forms where
\eta\geq 3
.
Then
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD for all
r\in[n]
.
The images of
F_{r+1},\cdots,F_{n}
in
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence.

Proof.

1. Since
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
is a regular sequence in
S
, we have that
\mathrm{ht}(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})=N-r
. Since,
S
is Cohen-Macaulay, we conclude that
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is Cohen-Macaulay by
[
18
, Proposition 18.13]
.

2. Since primes sequences in
S
are regular sequences, we have that
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{i})
is Cohen-Macaulay. By definition of prime sequences, we have that
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{i})
is a domain for all
i\in[m]
. Therefore, the images of
F_{r+1},\cdots,F_{m}
in
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
form a prime sequence.

3. Similarly, if
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence, then the images of
F_{r+1},\cdots,F_{m}
in
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
form a
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence. If
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence, we note that the quotient
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})
satisfies the
R_{3}
-property. Therefore,
S/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})
is an UFD by
[
4
, Corollary 1.5]
.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
R=\oplus_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}R_{i}
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra such that
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
.
Suppose
R
is generated by
R_{1}
as a
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra and
R
is Cohen-Macaulay.
Let
\dim(R)=n
.
Then we have:

1.

Every regular sequence
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
of homogeneous elements can be extended to a
h.s.o.p.
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
where
\deg(F_{i})=1
for
r+1\leq i\leq n
.

2.

If
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is a
h.s.o.p.
in
R
, then for any
r\leq n-1
, we have
R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is Cohen-Macaulay and the images of
F_{r+1},\cdots,F_{n}
in
R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
form a
h.s.o.p.

3.

Suppose
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t}
is a regular sequence of forms in
S
and
R=S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t})
.
Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\in R
be a regular sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
, and
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}\in R
an extension to a
h.s.o.p.
such that
\deg(F_{i})=1
for
r+1\leq i\leq n
.
Then the rank of
R
as a free module over
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
is given by
\Pi_{j=1}^{t}\deg(G_{j})\Pi_{i=1}^{r}\deg(F_{i})
.

Proof.

1. Since
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
is a regular sequence, we have that
\mathrm{ht}(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})=n-r
. Therefore,
R^{\prime}=R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
is Cohen-Macaulay by
[
18
, Proposition 18.13]
.
If
r=n
, then we are done.
Otherwise we proceed by induction.
The homogeneous maximal ideal of
R
is given by
{\mathfrak{m}}=(R_{1})
, and hence the homogeneous maximal ideal of
R^{\prime}
is
(R^{\prime}_{1})
.
If
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}=(R^{\prime}_{1})
is a minimal prime of
R^{\prime}
, then
\dim(R^{\prime})=\mathrm{ht}({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime})=0
, and hence
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
is a homogeneous system of parameters in
R
. Otherwise, by homogeneous prime avoidance, there exists a homogeneous element
f_{r+1}\in{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
which is not contained in any minimal prime of
R^{\prime}
, and hence
f_{r+1}\in R^{\prime}_{1}
is a nonzero divisor. Let
F_{r+1}\in R_{1}
be a homogeneous lift of
f_{r+1}
. Then
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r+1}
is a regular sequence and by induction we can extend to a homogeneous system of parameters
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r+1},\cdots,F_{n}
such that
\deg(F_{i})=1
for
r+1\leq i\leq n
.

2. Let
R^{\prime}=R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{r})
. Since,
R
is Cohen-Macaulay, we know that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}
is a regular sequence. Hence
R^{\prime}
is Cohen-Macaulay and
\dim(R^{\prime})=n-r
. Also, we have
R^{\prime}/(\overline{F}_{r+1},\cdots,\overline{F}_{n})\simeq R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{n})
, thus the images
\overline{F}_{r+1},\cdots,\overline{F}_{n}
are a homogeneous system of parameters in
R^{\prime}
, by
Theorem

4.8
.

3. Note that we may lift
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}\in R
to
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}\in S
such that
\deg(\widetilde{F}_{i})=1
for
r+1\leq i\leq n
