and we have
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t},\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}
is a homogeneous system of parameters for
S
. Now rank of
R
as a free
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
-module is given by the
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space dimension of
R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{n})
. Now
R^{\prime}=R/(F_{1},\cdots,F_{n})\simeq S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t},\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n})
, which is of Krull dimension
0
. Now we have
\dim_{{\mathbb{K}}}(R^{\prime})=\Pi_{j=1}^{t}\deg(G_{j})\Pi_{i=1}^{r}\deg(F_{i})
.
∎

Suppose that
u,v,u_{1},\cdots,u_{m}\in S_{1}
are pairwise linearly independent linear forms such that
v\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{u,u_{i}\right\}
for all
i\in[m]
. Then we must have that
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{u,v,u_{1},\cdots,u_{m}\right\})=2
. In particular, if
m\geq 2
, then we have
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{u,u_{i}\right\}=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{u,u_{j}\right\}
for all
i,j
. If
P,Q,P_{1},\cdots,P_{m}\in S
are pairwise non-associate forms of higher degree such that
Q\in\rad(P,P_{i})
for all
i\in[m]
, then it is no longer necessary that
\rad(P,P_{i})=\rad(P,P_{j})
for all
i,j
. However, the following lemma shows that if
m
is sufficiently large, then there exist two distinct forms
P_{i},P_{j}
such that
\rad(P,P_{i})=\rad(P,P_{j})
. Note that, in
Lemma

4.14
, the bound on
m
depends on both the degree
d
of the forms and the parameter
t
, which determines the dimension of the ring
R
. In
Lemma

5.19
, we will prove a strengthening for strong quotients of polynomial rings, where the bound on
m
is independent of
t
, and depends only on the degree
d
of the forms.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t}
be a regular sequence of forms of degree at most
d
in
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
and
R=S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t})
. Suppose
R
is a UFD. Let
P,Q,P_{1},\cdots,P_{m}\in R_{\leq d}
be homogeneous elements of positive degree where
m\geq 2^{d^{t+2}}
and
\gcd(P,Q)=\gcd(P,P_{i})=\gcd(Q,P_{i})=1
for all
i\in[m]
. If
Q\in\rad(P,P_{i})
for all
i\in[m]
, then we must have
\rad{(P,P_{i})}=\rad(P,P_{k})
for two distinct
i,k\in[m]
.

Proof.

Note that
P,Q
and
P,P_{i}
are regular sequences in
R
.
We have
\rad(P,Q)\subset\rad(P,P_{i})
for all
i\in[m]
, since
Q\in\rad(P,P_{i})
. Let
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{1},\cdots\mathfrak{p}_{\ell}\}
and
\mathcal{S}_{i}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{i1},\cdots,\mathfrak{p}_{i\ell_{i}}\}
be the set of minimal primes over
(P,Q)
and
(P,P_{i})
in
R
, respectively. We have
\bigcap\mathfrak{p}_{j}=\rad(P,Q)\subset\rad(P,P_{i})=\bigcap_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{ij}
. Therefore, by
[
5
, Proposition 1.11]
, we must have that for all
i,j
, there exists
k
such that
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{ij}
. Since we know that
\mathrm{ht}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})=\mathrm{ht}(\mathfrak{p}_{ij})=2
for all
i,j
, we must have that for all
i,j
, there exists
k
such that
\mathfrak{p}_{k}=\mathfrak{p}_{ij}
, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\mathcal{S}
for all
i\in[m]
.

Note that
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t},P,Q
is a regular sequence in
S
. Furthermore, the minimal primes
{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}
over
(P,Q)
in
R
are in one-to-one correspondence with the minimal primes
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{k}
over
(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t},P,Q)
in
S
. We have

\sum_{j}m(\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{j})e(S/\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{j})=e(S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t},P,Q))=\deg(P)\deg(Q)\Pi_{i=1}^{t}\deg(G_{i})\leq d^{t+2}.

Since
m(\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{j})\geq 1
and
e(S/\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{j})\geq 1
, we conclude that
|\mathcal{S}|\leq d^{t+2}
, i.e. there exist at most
d^{t+2}
minimal primes of
(P,Q)
.
Therefore there are at most
2^{d^{t+2}}-1
number of distinct choices for the set of minimal primes
\mathcal{S}_{i}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{ij}\}
of the ideals
(P,P_{i})
.
By the pigeonhole principle, there exist distinct
i,k\in[m]
such that
\mathcal{S}_{i}=\mathcal{S}_{k}
.
Thus,
\rad(P,P_{i})=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\mathfrak{q}=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\mathfrak{p}=\rad(P,P_{k})
.
∎

4.3
Intersection flatness and prime ideals

Recall that if
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
is a flat ring homomorphism, then
IR^{\prime}\bigcap JR^{\prime}=(I\bigcap J)R^{\prime}
for any two ideals
I,J
in
R
.
The notion of intersection flatness generalizes this fact to arbitrary intersections.
A flat ring homomorphism
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
is called intersection flat if for every family
\mathcal{I}
of ideals in
R
, we have
\bigcap_{I\in\mathcal{I}}(IR^{\prime})=(\bigcap_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I)R^{\prime}
.
The following result is from the discussion on extensions of prime ideals in in
[
3
, Section 2]
.
We rephrase it here in the form of the following proposition for convenience.

Proposition 4.15

(Intersection flatness)
.

Let
R
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra with
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
. Suppose
R
is Cohen-Macaulay. Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
be a regular sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
.
Then
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]\subset R
is a free extension of rings. Furthermore,
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]\subset R
is an intersection flat extension of rings.

Proof.

Since
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is a regular sequence, we may extend to a homogeneous system of parameters
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
, by
Proposition

4.13
.
Now, as
R
is Cohen-Macaulay, we have that
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]\subset R
is a free extension, by
Theorem

4.10
.
Also,
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
is a free extension.
Therefore the composition
{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]\subset R
is a free extension.
By
[
38
, Page 41]
, free extensions are intersection flat.
∎

Hilbert Rings
. We say that
R
is a Hilbert ring if every prime ideal is an intersection of maximal ideals.
The polynomial ring
S
and any finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra
R=S/I
are Hilbert rings.

We recall the following result, from
[
3
, Theorem 2.7]
.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
A
be a Noetherian Hilbert ring and let
R\supseteq A
be a Noetherian
A
-algebra that is intersection flat over
A
. Suppose that for every maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
of
A
, we have
R/{\mathfrak{m}}R
is an integral domain. Then for every prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}
of
A
, we have that
R/{\mathfrak{p}}R
is an integral domain, i.e.
{\mathfrak{p}}R
is a prime ideal of
R
.

In particular, we have the following corollary (
[
3
, Corollary 2.9]
): if
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\in S
is a prime sequence, then prime ideals in
{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]
extend to prime ideals in
S
.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
S
be our polynomial ring and
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}
be a prime sequence of forms in
S
.
Then for any prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]
, the extension ideal
\mathfrak{p}S
is also prime.

We prove that this result can be generalized to certain quotients of
S
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t}
be a prime sequence of forms in
S
. Let
R=S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t})
. Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
be a prime sequence in
R
. Then for any prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]
, the extension ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}R
is a prime ideal in
R
.

Proof.

Note that,
R
is a finitely generated graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, with
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
.
Also,
R
is generated by
R_{1}
as a
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra.
By
Proposition

4.12
we have
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay integral domain.
By
Proposition

4.15
, we have that
A:={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]\subset R
is intersection flat.
Therefore, by
Theorem

4.16
, it is enough to show that
{\mathfrak{m}}R
is a prime ideal for every maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
of
A
.
Since,
A
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring, we know that
{\mathfrak{m}}=(F_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,F_{m}-c_{m})
for some
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{m})\in{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
.
Now,
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is a regular sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
and the ideal
(F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})
is a prime ideal in
R
.
Then, by
[
3
, Proposition 2.8]
, we conclude that
(F_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,F_{m}-c_{m})R
is a prime ideal in
R
for all
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{m})\in{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
.
Therefore
{\mathfrak{m}}R
is a prime ideal in
R
for all maximal ideals
{\mathfrak{m}}
of
A
, and we are done.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t}
be an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence of forms in
S
, such that
\eta\geq 3
. Let
R=S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t})
. Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
be a prime sequence in
R
.
Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]
.

1.

Let
Q\in A
. Then
Q
is irreducible in
A
iff
Q
is irreducible in
R
.

2.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\in R
be irreducible elements and
d_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
such that
\prod_{i=1}^{k}P_{i}^{d_{i}}\in A
. Then
P_{i}\in A
for
i\in[k]
.

Proof.

1. By
Proposition

4.12
, we know that
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.
Since a prime sequence is algebraically independent, we know that
A
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring, and hence an UFD. Note that the units in
A
and
R
are only elements of
{\mathbb{K}}
. Let
Q\in A
. If
Q=H_{1}H_{2}
is a factorization of
Q
into non-units in
A
, then it is also a factorization into non-units in
R
. Therefore, if
Q
is irreducible in
R
, then it is irreducible in
A
. Conversely, suppose
Q
is irreducible in
A
. Then
(Q)
is a prime ideal in
A
. By
Proposition

4.18
, we have that
(Q)\cdot R
is a prime ideal in
R
.
Therefore,
Q
is irreducible in
R
, as
R
is a UFD.

2. Let
F=\prod_{i=1}^{k}P_{i}^{d_{i}}
.
Since
A
is a UFD and
F\in A
, we have that
F=\prod_{i=1}^{t}Q_{i}^{e_{i}}
, where each
Q_{i}\in A
is irreducible.
Therefore, by part (1), we have that
Q_{i}
is irreducible in
R
for all
i\in[k]
.
Hence,
F=\prod_{i=1}^{t}Q_{i}^{e_{i}}
is an irreducible factorization of
F
in
R
.
Since
R
is a UFD, by uniqueness of irreducible factorization, we must have that
P_{i}=Q_{i}
and
d_{i}=e_{i}
(possibly after a permutation of the indices).
Hence
P_{i}\in A
for all
i\in[k]
.
∎

Note that, in
Lemma

4.19
, it is necessary to assume that
A
is generated by a prime sequence.
For example, consider the subalgebra
A=k[xy]\subset k[x,y]
.
Now
A
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring in one variable and hence an UFD. However
xy\in A
but
x,y\not\in A
.

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t}
be a prime sequence of forms in
S
and
R=S/(G_{1},\cdots,G_{t})
.
Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
be a prime sequence in
R
and
A:={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}]\subset R
.
Let
I\subset A
be an ideal.

1.

For any ideal
J\subset A
we have
JR\cap A=J
.

2.

If
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset A
is a minimal prime over
I
in
A
, then
{\mathfrak{p}}R
is a minimal prime over
IR
in
R
.
Conversely, for any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}\subset R
over
IR
in
R
, there is a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset A
over
I
in
A
such that
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}R
.

3.

We have

\rad_{R}(IR)\cap A=\rad_{A}(I)\ \text{ and }\ \rad_{A}(I)\cdot R=\rad_{R}(IR).

where
\rad_{R}(IR)
denotes the radical of the ideal
IR
in the ring
R
, and
\rad_{A}(I)
is the radical of the ideal
I
in the ring
A
.

4.

I
is radical in
A
iff
IR
is radical in
R
.

5.

We have that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{h}
is a strict chain of prime ideals in
A
iff the extension
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}S\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{h}S
is a strict chain of prime ideals in
S
.

Proof.

1. Note that
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay domain. Since
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is a regular sequence, we know that
A\subset R
is a free extension. Hence,
A\subset R
is a faithfully flat extension. Therefore, for any ideal
J
of the ring
A
, we have that the contraction of the ideal
JR
to the ring
A
is
I
itself. Thus
IR\cap A=I
.

2. Let
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset A
be a minimal prime over the ideal
I
in
A
. Then,
{\mathfrak{p}}R
is a prime ideal in
R
, by
Proposition

4.18
. Let
IR\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}R
be a minimal prime over
IR
in
R
. Then
I=IR\cap A\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}\cap A\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}R\cap A={\mathfrak{p}}
. Since
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a minimal prime, we see that
{\mathfrak{q}}\cap A={\mathfrak{p}}
and hence
{\mathfrak{p}}R\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Therefore
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}R
and
{\mathfrak{p}}R
is a minimal prime over
IR
in
R
.

Conversely, let
IR\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}\subset R
be a minimal prime over
IR
in
R
. Then
I=IR\cap A\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}\cap A
. Hence there exists a minimal prime
I\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}
in
A
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}\cap A
. Now,
{\mathfrak{p}}R
is a prime ideal in
R
. We have
IR\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}R\subseteq({\mathfrak{q}}\cap A)R\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
. As
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a minimal prime over
IR
in
R
, we conclude that
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}R
.

3. We have
\rad_{R}(IR)=\cap_{i=1}^{a}{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
, where
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
are the minimal primes over
IR
in
R
.
Similarly, we have
\rad_{A}(I)=\cap_{j=1}^{b}{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}
where
{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}
are the minimal primes over
I
in
A
.
Now, by part (2), we know that for each
i\in[a]
, there exists
j\in[b]
such that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}={\mathfrak{p}}_{j}R
.
We also have that for each
j\in[b]
, there exists
i\in[a]
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}R={\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
.
Hence we have
\{{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}|i\in[a]\}=\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}R|j\in[b]\}
.
Therefore,
\rad_{R}(IR)\cap A=(\cap_{i=1}^{a}{\mathfrak{q}}_{i})\cap A=(\cap_{j=1}^{b}{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}R)\cap A=\cap_{j=1}^{b}({\mathfrak{p}}_{j}R\cap A)=\cap_{j=1}^{b}{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}=\rad_{A}(I)
.
Conversely, we have
\rad_{A}(I)\cdot R=(\cap_{j=1}^{b}{\mathfrak{p}}_{j})\cdot R=\cap_{j=1}^{b}({\mathfrak{p}}_{j}R)=\cap_{i=1}^{a}{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}=\rad_{R}(IR)
, where the second equality holds as
A\subset R
is flat.

4. If
I
is radical in
A
, then
\rad_{R}(IR)=\rad_{A}(I)\cdot R=IR
. Conversely, if
IR
is radical in
R
, then
\rad_{A}(I)=\rad_{R}(IR)\cap A=IR\cap A=I
.

5. By part (1) we know that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}S\cap A={\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
. Thus, if
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}S
is a prime then
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
is a prime. Conversely, by
Corollary

4.17
, we know that if
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
is a prime in
A
then
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}S
is a prime in
S
for all
i
. If
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}S={\mathfrak{q}}_{i+1}S
for some
i
, then we have
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}=({\mathfrak{q}}_{i}S)\cap A=({\mathfrak{q}}_{i+1}S)\cap A={\mathfrak{q}}_{i+1}
which is a contradiction. Conversely, if
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}={\mathfrak{q}}_{i+1}
then
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}S={\mathfrak{q}}_{i+1}S
. Therefore, we have that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{h}
is a strict chain of prime ideals in
A
iff the extension
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}S\subseteq\cdots\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{h}S
is a strict chain of prime ideals in
S
.
∎

4.4
A primality criterion

Recall that an ideal
I\subset S
is prime iff
S/I
is an integral domain iff the affine scheme
\mathrm{Spec}(S/I)
is irreducible and reduced. Also,
I
is radical iff
\mathrm{Spec}(S/I)
is reduced. The following lemma provides a method of showing that a Cohen-Macaulay affine scheme
Y
is irreducible or reduced, if there is a suitable morphism
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
to an affine scheme
X
, such that
X
and general fibers of
\pi
are irreducible or reduced. We will use this to prove a primality criterion in
Lemma

4.24
and a critierion for reducedness in
Lemma

4.27
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
be a homomorphism of finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras. Let
Y=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{B}})
,
X=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
be the corresponding morphism of affine schemes. Suppose that every irreducible component of
Y
dominates some irreducible component of
X
.

1.

Suppose that
X
is irreducible and reduced, i.e.
{\mathcal{A}}
is an integral domain. If
\pi^{-1}(x)
is irreducible for a general closed point
x\in X
, then
Y
is irreducible.

2.

Suppose
X
is reduced and
Y
satisfies Serre’s property
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, i.e.
Y
does not have embedded primes. If for every irreducible component
W
of
X
, we have that
\pi^{-1}(x)
is reduced for a general closed point
x\in W
, then
Y
is reduced.

Proof.

(1) Since
{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
is a homomorphism of finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras, the morphsim
\pi
is of finite type. Therefore, by
[
59
,
Tag 0554
]
, the generic fiber of
\pi
is irreducible. Since there is bijection between the irreducible components of
Y
that dominate
X
and the irreducible components of the generic fiber
[
32
, Chapter 0, 2.1.8]
, we conclude that
Y
is irreducible.

(2) Since
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies the
\mathcal{S}_{1}
-property, in order to show that
{\mathcal{B}}
is reduced, it is enough to show that
{\mathcal{B}}
is generically reduced, i.e.
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is reduced for any minimal prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
{\mathcal{B}}
.

Note that the minimal primes
\mathfrak{p}
of
{\mathcal{B}}
correspond to the generic points of the irreducible components of
Y
. Similarly the generic points of irreducible components
W
of
\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{A}})
correspond to minimal primes
{\mathfrak{q}}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
and we have
W=\mathrm{Spec}(A/{\mathfrak{q}})
. Since every irreducible component of
Y
dominates some irreducible component of
X
, any such generic point
\mathfrak{p}
maps to a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
under
\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{A}})
. Therefore, for any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
of
{\mathcal{B}}
, there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p})={\mathfrak{q}}
. Note that we have
Y\times_{X}W=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}})
and we have the fiber product diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times_{X}W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is reduced and
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a minimal prime of
{\mathcal{A}}
, we know that the localization
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a reduced local ring of dimension zero. Hence
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a field and
{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}=(0)
. Therefore, we have
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}/{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq({\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}})_{(0)}\simeq K({\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}})
, where
K({\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}})
is the field of fractions of the integral domain
{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}}
. We have that the fiber of
\pi
over
{\mathfrak{q}}
is given by
Y_{\mathfrak{q}}=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}})
and the the generic fiber of
\pi_{W}
is given by
(Y\times_{X}W)_{\mathfrak{q}}=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}}}K({\mathcal{A}}/{\mathfrak{q}}))
. Note that, by the universal property of fiber products, we have
Y_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq(Y\times_{X}W)_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Now
\pi_{W}:Y\times_{X}W\rightarrow W
is a morphism of finite type and
W
is irreducible with generic point
{\mathfrak{q}}
. By assumption, a the fiber
\pi_{W}^{-1}(x)
is reduced for a general closed point
x\in W
. By
[
59
,
Tag 0575
]
we conclude that the generic fiber
(Y\times_{X}W)_{\mathfrak{q}}
is reduced. Therefore, the scheme
Y_{\mathfrak{q}}
is reduced and the ring

\mathrm{Spec}({\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}})
is reduced.

Let
\frac{f}{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
be a nilpotent element. Therefore
sf^{k}=0
in
{\mathcal{B}}
for some
s\not\in\mathfrak{p}
. So
(sf)^{k}=0
in
{\mathcal{B}}
and hence
(sf)^{k}\otimes 1=0
in
{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Therefore
sf\otimes 1=0
in
{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
by reducedness. Let
T\subset{\mathcal{A}}
be the multiplicatively closed set
{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathfrak{q}}
. Consider the
{\mathcal{A}}
-bilinear map
\psi:{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\rightarrow T^{-1}{\mathcal{B}}
given by
(s,\frac{a}{b})=\frac{a\cdot s}{b}
. If
sf\otimes 1=0
, then by the universal property of tensor products we must have
sf=\psi(sf,1)=0
in
T^{-1}{\mathcal{B}}
. Therefore there exists an
a\in T={\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathfrak{q}}
, such that
a\cdot sf=0
in
{\mathcal{B}}
. Note that
s\not\in\mathfrak{p}
. We also have
\phi(a)\not\in\mathfrak{p}
, as
\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p})={\mathfrak{q}}
and
a\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}
. So we have
\phi(a)sf=a\cdot sf=0\in\mathfrak{p}
, where
\phi(a)s\not\in\mathfrak{p}
. Therefore we conclude that
f=0
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Thus
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is reduced, since
\frac{f}{t}
was an arbitrary nilpotent element.
∎

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
S:={\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{m},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
be the graded polynomial ring with
\deg(z_{i})=d_{i}\geq 1
and
\deg(x_{i})=e_{i}\geq 1
. Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in
be homogeneous elements of positive degree. Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
and
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in A
. Suppose
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
and the ideal
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
does not have any embedded primes in
S
. Then the following holds:

1.

P
is a non-zero divisor in
S/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.

2.

For any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
over
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
S
, we have that
{\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
is a minimal prime over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
.

Proof.

1. Since
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
does not have any embedded primes in
S
, we know that all associated primes of
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
are minimal primes. If
P
is a zero-divisor in
S/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
, then there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
S
such that
P\in{\mathfrak{p}}
. By
Proposition

4.20
, we know that there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{q}}\cdot S
. Since
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a homogeneous ideal in
A
, the minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
is also homogeneous. Hence
{\mathfrak{q}}\subset(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
, which is a contradiction as
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
.

2. Since primary decomposition commutes with polynomial extensions, we know that
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
does not have any embedded primes in
A
as well. Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be a minimal prime over
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
S
. Since
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a homogeneous ideal, we know that
{\mathfrak{p}}
is homogeneous and
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset(z_{1},\cdots,z_{m},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
in
S
. Now
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\subset{\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
. Hence there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
such that
{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
. We will show that
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
.

By
Proposition

4.20
, we have that
{\mathfrak{q}}S
is a minimal prime over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{s})
in
S
. Suppose
\mathrm{ht}({\mathfrak{q}})=h
. Then
\mathrm{ht}({\mathfrak{q}}S)=h
, as
S/{\mathfrak{q}}S=(A/{\mathfrak{q}})[z_{1},\cdots,z_{m}]
. Note that
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a minimal prime over
({\mathfrak{q}}S,P)
in
S
. By part (1), we know that
P
is a non-zero divisor in
S/{\mathfrak{q}}S
. Therefore,
\mathrm{ht}({\mathfrak{p}})=h+1
. Suppose
{\mathfrak{p}}\cap A\neq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then
\mathrm{ht}({\mathfrak{p}}\cap A)\geq h+1
. Let
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}\cdots\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{h+1}={\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
be a strict chain of primes of length
h+1
in
A
. Then by
Proposition

4.20
, we have a chain
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}S\subsetneq{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}S\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq({\mathfrak{q}}_{h+1}S)=({\mathfrak{p}}\cap A)S\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}
of length at least
h+1
in
S
. Hence we must have
{\mathfrak{p}}=({\mathfrak{p}}\cap A)S
. Now
{\mathfrak{q}}\cap A\subset(z_{1},\cdots,z_{m},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})\cap A=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
in
A
. Therefore we have a contradiction, since
P\in{\mathfrak{p}}=({\mathfrak{p}}\cap A)S
and
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
.
∎

We note that the proof of part (1) of
Proposition

4.22
works for quotients of
S
by prime sequences. We provide a proof below for completeness.

Corollary 4.23
.

Let
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}\in S
be a prime sequence of forms in
S
. Let
R=S/(H_{1},\cdots,H_{t})
. Suppose
g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}
is a prime sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
. Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in R
be homogeneous elements of positive degree. Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
and
A^{\prime}={\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
. Suppose
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
and the ideal
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
does not have any embedded primes in
R
. Then
P
is a non-zero divisor in
R/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.

Proof.

1. Since
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
does not have any embedded primes in
R
, we know that all associated primes of
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
are minimal primes. If
P
is a zero-divisor in
R/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
, then there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
R
such that
P\in{\mathfrak{p}}
. By
Proposition

4.20
, we know that there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{q}}\cdot S
. Since
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a homogeneous ideal in
A
, the minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
is also homogeneous. Hence
{\mathfrak{q}}\subset(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
, which is a contradiction as
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
.
∎

The following lemma proves a criterion for an ideal
(P,Q)
to be prime.

Lemma 4.24

(Primality criterion)
.

Let
P,Q
be irreducible forms in
S={\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
. Let
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}
be an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
with
\eta\geq 3
. Suppose that
Q\in{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]
and
P
is irreducible in
S/\mathfrak{I}_{Q}
, where
\mathfrak{I}_{Q}=(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s})
. Then the ideal
(P,Q)
is prime.

Proof.

Since
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence, we know that the subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
. We identify
{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]
with
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
using this isomorphism and, by abuse of notation, the polynomial in
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
corresponding to
Q
is still denoted by
Q
. Let
Y\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
and
X\subset\mathbb{A}^{s}
be the closed subschemes defined by the ideals
(P,Q)
and
(Q)
respectively. Let
B=S/(P,Q)
and
A={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]/(Q)
. We have
\mathrm{Spec}(B)=Y
and
\mathrm{Spec}(A)=X
. We have an inclusion of rings
A\hookrightarrow B
, since
(P,Q)\cap{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}]=(Q)
by Proposition
4.22
. Let
\pi:\mathbb{A}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{s}
be the projection morphism defined by
\pi(y)=(g_{1}(y),\cdots,g_{s}(y))
. Note that
\pi(Y)\subset X
. We will show that
Y
is irreducible and reduced.

First we will show that
(\pi|_{Y})^{-1}(x)
is irreducible and reduced for any closed point
x\in X
. Let
x=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{s})
be a closed point in
X
and let
\mathfrak{m}_{x}=(x_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,x_{s}-c_{s})
the maximal ideal corresponding to
x
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{x}\cdot S
denote the ideal generated by
(g_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,g_{s}-c_{s})
in
S
. By Lemma
4.17
, we know that
\mathfrak{m}_{x}\cdot S
is a prime ideal in
S
. Note that we have

(\pi|_{Y})^{-1}(x)\simeq\mathrm{Spec}(S/(\mathfrak{m}_{x}\cdot S,P))

for
x\in X
. Since
\mathfrak{m}_{x}\cdot S=(g_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,g_{s}-c_{s})
, it is enough to show that the ideal
(g_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,g_{s}-c_{s},P)
is prime for any
x=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{s})\in X
. Since
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
sequence with
\eta\geq 3
, we know that
S/\mathfrak{J}_{Q}
is an UFD. As the image
\overline{P}
is irreducible in
S/\mathfrak{J}_{Q}
, we have that
(\overline{P})
is a prime ideal in
S/\mathfrak{J}_{Q}
and
(\mathfrak{J}_{Q},P)
is a prime ideal in
S
. Therefore
(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s},P)
is a prime ideal in
S
. Hence
(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s},P)
is a prime sequence in
S
, because
(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s})
was an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
sequence. By
[
3
, Proposition 2.8]
, we conclude that
(g_{1}-c_{1},\cdots,g_{s}-c_{s},P)
is a prime ideal for any
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{s})\in X
. Therefore
(\pi|_{Y})^{-1}(x)
is an integral scheme, in particular it is irreducible and reduced.

Note that by
Proposition

4.22
, every irreducible component of
Y
dominates
X
. We know that
X
is irreducible, since
Q
is irreducible. Since
P,Q
is a regular sequence, we know that
Y
is Cohen-Macaulay. We have shown that
(\pi|_{Y})^{-1}(x)
is irreducible for all
x\in X
.
Therefore
Y
is irreducible and reduced by Lemma
4.21
.
∎

4.5
Elimination, Resultants and Discriminant Lemma

We now prove some structural results on the elimination ideals of radical ideals.

Lemma 4.25

(Elimination of radical)
.

Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in S:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
be polynomials of positive degree. Let
S^{\prime}={\mathbb{K}}[x_{2},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
. Suppose the following conditions hold:

1.

The ideal
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a radical ideal in
S
, and
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
.

2.

The polynomial
P
depends on the variable
x_{1}
, i.e.
P=\sum_{j=0}^{e}p_{j}x_{1}^{j}
, where
e>0
,
p_{e}\neq 0
and
p_{j}\in S^{\prime}
for all
j
.

3.

We have that
p_{e}
is a non-zero divisor in
S/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.

Then the elimination ideal
\rad(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})_{x_{1}}=\rad(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap S^{\prime}
is generated by
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}
, i.e.
\rad(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})_{x_{1}}=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. In particular,
\rad{(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{s}]=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.

Proof.

By item (2), we have
P=\sum_{k=0}^{e}p_{j}x_{i}^{j}
, where
0<e
and
p_{j}\in S^{\prime}
, with
p_{e}\neq 0
.
Let
J=\rad(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap S^{\prime}
. Since
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in J
, we have that
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\subseteq J
.

To show the other inclusion, if
F\in J
, we have that

F^{D}=PA+Q_{1}B_{1}+\cdots+Q_{k}B_{k}

for some
A,B_{1},\cdots,B_{k}\in S
. We will now show that
A\in(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.
To prove this, it is enough to show that for any
A,B_{1},\cdots,B_{k}\in S
that satisfy the equation above, we must have that the leading term of
A
is in
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
, when
A
considered as a polynomial in
x_{1}
with coefficients in
S^{\prime}
.

We have
\deg_{x_{1}}(F^{D})=\deg_{x_{1}}(Q_{t})=0
for all
t\in[k]
.
Let
\ell:=\deg_{x_{1}}(PA)
. Note that
\ell\geq e
. Consider
A
as a polynomial in
x_{1}
with coefficients in
S^{\prime}
and then we may write
A=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-e}a_{j}x_{1}^{j}
where
a_{\ell-e}\neq 0
. Similarly, for all
t\in[k]
, we may write
B_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{t}}b_{tj}x_{1}^{j}
. Comparing the coefficients of
x_{1}^{\ell}
on both sides of the equation above, we obtain

p_{e}\cdot a_{\ell-e}+Q_{1}b_{1\ell}+\cdots+Q_{k}b_{k\ell}=0.

Therefore
p_{e}a_{\ell-e}\in(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Since
p_{e}
is a non-zero divisor in
S/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})

, we conclude that
a_{\ell-e}\in(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Thus, by induction, we must have that
A\in(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
, which proves that
F^{D}\in(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Since
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical, we have that
F\in(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Hence
\rad(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap S^{\prime}=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.
∎

The following result generalizes
Lemma

4.25
to the setting of quotients
S
by prime sequences.

Lemma 4.26

(Elimination of radical in quotient rings)
.

Let
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}\in S
be a prime sequence of forms in
S
. Let
R=S/(H_{1},\cdots,H_{t})
. Suppose
g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}
is a prime sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
. Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in R
be homogeneous elements of positive degree. Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
and
A^{\prime}={\mathbb{K}}[g_{2},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
. Suppose the following holds:

1.

We have
P\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in{\mathbb{K}}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}]
.

2.

The ideal
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a radical ideal in
R
.

3.

As an element of the algebra
A
, we have that
P
depends on
g_{1}
, i.e.
P=\sum_{j=0}^{e}p_{j}g_{1}^{j}
, where
e>0
,
p_{e}\neq 0
and
p_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
for all
j
.

4.

We have that
p_{e}
is a non-zero divisor in
R/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
.

Then the elimination ideal
\rad_{R}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap A^{\prime}
is generated by
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}
, i.e. we have

\rad_{R}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap A^{\prime}=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}).

Proof.

Since
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in
R
, we know that
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in the algebra
A
, by
Proposition

4.20
. Since
p_{e}
is a non-zero divisor in
R/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
, we know that
p_{e}\not\in{\mathfrak{p}}
for any minimal prime over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
R
. By
Proposition

4.20
, we know that any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}\subset A
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
is given by
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}\cap A
for some minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
R
. Therefore,
p_{e}\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}

for any any minimal prime over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
, and hence
p_{e}
is a non-zero divisor in
A/(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Therefore, by applying

Lemma

4.25
to
A
, we obtain
\rad_{A}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap A^{\prime}=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Now, by
Proposition

4.20
, we know that
\rad_{R}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap A=\rad_{A}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
. Therefore we have

\rad_{R}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap A^{\prime}=\rad_{A}(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})\cap A^{\prime}=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}).

∎

The following result proves a criterion for showing that an ideal is radical, based on discriminants of one of the generators.

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
S:={\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
be the graded polynomial ring with
\deg(z_{i})=d_{i}\geq 1
and
\deg(x_{i})=e_{i}\geq 1
. Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
as a graded subalgebra. Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in S
be homogeneous elements of positive degree. Suppose that the following conditions hold:

1.

We have
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in A
and the ideal
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a Cohen-Macaulay, radical ideal in
A
.

2.

P
does not have multiple factors and
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
in
S
.

3.

For all
i\in[r]
such that
P
depends on the variable
z_{i}
, we have
\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P)\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot S
for any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
.

Then the ideal
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in
S
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, suppose that
P
only depends
z_{1},\cdots,z_{t}
for some
t\leq r
. By applying
Proposition

4.20
, it is enough to prove that
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in
{\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{t},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we may assume that
P
depends on all the
z_{i}
variables in
S
.

Let
J=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
and
I=JS=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
S
. By
Proposition

4.20
, we have that
I
is radical in
S
, since
J=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in
A
. Furthermore, we have
I\cap A=J
. Since
A/J
is Cohen-Macaulay, we know that
S/IS\simeq(A/J)[z_{1},\cdots,z_{r}]
is also Cohen-Macaulay. By
Proposition

4.22
, we know that
P
is a non-zero divisor in
S/I
. Since
S/I
is Cohen-Macaulay by assumption, we conclude that
S/((P)+I)
is also Cohen-Macaulay and hence it has no embedded primes. Let
\pi:\mathbb{A}^{r+s}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{s}
be the morphism corresponding to
A\subset S
. Note that
\pi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
for closed points. Consider the affine schemes
Y=\mathrm{Spec}(S/((P)+I))
and
X=\mathrm{Spec}(A/J)
. Note that we have a homomorphism of finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras
A/J\rightarrow S/((P)+I)
, as
J\subset((P)+I)\cap A
. Note that the corresponding morphism of affine schemes is given by
\pi|_{Y}:Y\rightarrow X
and we have commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}}^{r+s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi|_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Since
J
is a radical ideal in
A
, we have that
X
is reduced. Since
S/((P)+I)
does not have any embedded primes, we know that
Y
is satisfies the
\mathcal{S}_{1}
-property. By
Proposition

4.22
, we know that for every minimal prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
over
(P)+I
in
S
, there exists a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
J
in
A
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}\cap A={\mathfrak{q}}
. In particular, every irreducible component of
Y
dominates some irreducible component of
X
. Therefore, by
Lemma

4.21
, it is enough to show that for every irreducible component
W
of
X
, we have that
\pi|_{Y}^{-1}(x)
is reduced for a general closed point
x\in W
.

Let
x=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{s})\in X
be a closed point. Let
P_{x}\in{\mathbb{K}}[z_{1}\cdots,z_{r}]
denote the polynomial
P(z_{1}\cdots,z_{r},c_{1},\cdots,c_{s})
. Note that
\pi|_{Y}^{-1}(x)=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{r}]/(P_{x}))
, as
x\in X
. Since
P
is homogeneous and
P\not\in(x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
, we conclude that
P_{x}\not\equiv 0
for any
x\in X
. Now
\pi|_{Y}^{-1}(x)
is not reduced iff the polynomial
P_{x}
has a multiple factor, i.e.
\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{k}}(P_{x})\equiv 0
for some variable
z_{k}
such that
P_{x}
depends on
z_{k}
. For
i\in[r]
, let
Z_{i}\subset\mathbb{A}^{r+s}
be the closed subscheme
\mathrm{Spec}(S/\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P))
defined by
\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P)
. Note that
\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P)\not\equiv 0
, since
P
does not have multiple factors. Now, for any variable
z_{i}
such that
P_{x}
depends on
z_{i}
, we have

\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P_{x})\equiv 0\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P)(z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},c_{1},\cdots,c_{s})=0\Leftrightarrow\pi^{-1}(x)\subset Z_{i}.

To summarize, for a closed point
x\in X
, we have that
\pi|_{Y}^{-1}(x)
is reduced if
\pi^{-1}(x)\not\subset Z_{i}
for all
i
such that
P_{x}
depends on
z_{i}
. Therefore it is enough to show that, for any irreducible component
W
of
X
and a general closed point
x\in W
, we have that
\pi^{-1}(x)\not\subset Z_{i}
for all
i\in[r]
.

Note that
\mathrm{Spec}(S/I)=\pi^{-1}(X)=X\times\mathbb{A}^{r}
. Fix
i\in[r]
. Let
W
be an irreducible component of
X
corresponding to a minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
J
in
A
. Let
\dim(W)=n
and we have
\pi^{-1}(W)=W\times{\mathbb{A}}^{r}
. By assumption (3), we have that
Z_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(W)
is of dimension at most
n+r-1
.
Let
T
be an irreducible component of
Z_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(W)
with the reduced scheme structure. Note that
\dim(T)\leq n+r-1
. If
T
does not dominate
W
, then for a general
x\in W
we have
\pi^{-1}(x)\cap T=\emptyset
. If
T
dominates
W
, then the composition morphism
T\rightarrow Z_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(W)\rightarrow\pi^{-1}(W)\xrightarrow{\pi_{W}}W
is a dominant morphism of integral separated schemes of finite type. Therefore, there exists an open subset
U\subset W
such that
\dim(\pi^{-1}(x)\cap T)=\dim(T)-\dim(W)\leq r-1
for all
x\in U
, by
[
18
, Corollary 14.5]
or
[
63
, Theorem 11.4.1]
. Since there are finitely many possibilites for the irreducible components
T
of
Z_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(W)
, we conclude that
\dim(\pi^{-1}(x)\cap(Z_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(W)))\leq r-1
for a general
x\in W
. Since
\dim(\pi^{-1}(x))=r
, we conclude that
\pi^{-1}(x)\not\subset Z_{i}
for a general
x\in W
. Since there are finitely many choices for
Z_{i}
, we conclude that for a general
x\in W
we have
\pi^{-1}(x)\not\subset Z_{i}
for all
i\in[r]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Example 4.28
.

Note that in
Lemma

4.27
, we need that
\mathrm{Disc}_{z_{i}}(P)\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}S
for all
z_{i}
such that
P
depends on
z_{i}
. It is not enough to assume that this property holds for just one such
z_{i}
. One can construct such an example as follows. Let
P=y^{3}+vy^{2}+(xu^{2}-z^{3})
and
Q=xu^{2}-z^{3}
in
{\mathbb{K}}[x,y,z,u,v]
. Then
Q\in{\mathbb{K}}[x,u,z]
and
\mathrm{Disc}_{v}(P)=y^{2}\not\in(Q)
. However, the ideal
(P,Q)
is not radical as
\sqrt{(P,Q)}=(y^{2}+yv,xu^{2}-z^{3})
. This occurs because we have
\mathrm{Disc}_{y}(P)\in(Q)
.

Definition 4.29
.

Let
R
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra and
g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}
a regular sequence in
R
. Let
\phi:{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{s}]\rightarrow A
be the isomorphism defined as
\phi(y_{i})=g_{i}
.
Let
P,Q\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Let
\widetilde{P}=\phi^{-1}(P)
and
\widetilde{Q}=\phi^{-1}(Q)
. We define the resultant of
P,Q
with respect to
g_{i}
in the subalgebra
\mathcal{A}
as

\mathrm{Res}_{g_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}}(P,Q)=\phi(\mathrm{Res}_{y_{i}}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})).

Similarly we define the discriminant of
P
with respect to
g_{i}
in the sublagebra
\mathcal{A}
as

\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}}(P)=\phi(\mathrm{Res}_{y_{i}}(\widetilde{P},\frac{\partial\widetilde{P}}{\partial y_{i}})).

Using the isomorphism
\phi
we can translate the usual properties of resultants in polynomial rings to similar properties in the subalgebra
\mathcal{A}
.
Note that
\mathrm{Res}_{g_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}}(P,Q)
is in the subalgebra generated by
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\}\setminus\{g_{i}\}
in
S
. Also, we have
\mathrm{Res}_{g_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}}(P,Q)\in(P,Q)
.

Corollary 4.30
.

Let
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}\in S
be a prime sequence of forms in
S
. Let
R=S/(H_{1},\cdots,H_{t})
. Suppose
g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}
is a prime sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
. Let
A^{\prime}={\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
and
A={\mathbb{K}}[f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
. Let
P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in R
be homogeneous elements of positive degree. Suppose the following conditions hold:

1.

We have
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\in A
and the ideal
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a Cohen-Macaulay, radical ideal in
A
.

2.

We have
P\in A^{\prime}
and
P
does not have multiple factors in
A^{\prime}
. Suppose
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
in
R
.

3.

For all
i\in[r]
such that
P
depends on
g_{i}
in
A^{\prime}
, we have
\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{i}}^{A^{\prime}}(P)\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot R
for any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
.

Then the ideal
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a radical ideal in
R
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

4.20
, it is enough to show that
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is a radical ideal in the
{\mathbb{K}}
-subalgebra
A^{\prime}
. Note that we have an isomorphism of graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras
A^{\prime}\simeq{\mathbb{K}}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}]
given by
g_{i}\mapsto z_{i}
and
f_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
where
\deg(z_{i})=\deg(g_{i})
and
\deg(x_{i})=\deg(f_{i})
. Therefore, condition (1) of
Lemma

4.27
holds. Note that
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})R\cap A^{\prime}=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
in
A^{\prime}
. Therefore condition (2) of
Lemma

4.27
holds. Now, we also note that for any minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
in
A
, we have
\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{i}}^{A^{\prime}}(P)\not\in{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot A^{\prime}
, by condition (3) above, Hence, we may apply
Lemma

4.27
to conclude that
(P,Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k})
is radical in
A^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.31
.

Let
\eta\geq 3
and
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}\in S
be an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence of forms in
S
. Consider the UFD
R=S/(H_{1},\cdots,H_{t})
. Suppose
g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}
is a prime sequence of homogeneous elements in
R
. Let
P\in R
be a square-free homogeneous element of degree
d\geq 1
. Suppose we have
P\in{\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
and
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
in
R
. Let
\mathcal{H}=\{Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{t}\}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
be such that:

1.

Q_{i}
is square-free homogeneous of positive degree in
R
for all
i\in[t]
.

2.

Q_{i},Q_{j}
do not have common factors in
R
for any
i\neq j
.

3.

(P,Q_{i})
is not radical in
R
for all
i
.

Then we have
|\mathcal{H}|=t\leq d^{2}(2d-1)
. In particular, there exist at most
d^{2}(2d-1)
number of distinct elements in
{\mathbb{K}}[f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
satisfying the conditions (1),(2) and (3) above.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{r}]
. Consider
P
as a polynomial in
{\mathcal{B}}[f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
. Since
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
, there exists a monomial
\alpha\prod_{j}g_{i_{j}}^{e_{j}}
in
P
, where
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}\setminus\{0\}
and
g_{i_{j}}\in\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{r}\}
. Without loss of generality, let
g_{1},\cdots,g_{k}
be the elements appearing in this monomial. Note that we must have
k\leq d
, since
P\in R_{d}
and
\deg(g_{i})\geq 1
for all
i\in[r]
. Now, we have that
P\not\in(g_{k+1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
.

Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[g_{k+1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
. Then
Q_{i}\in A
for all
i\in[t]
. Now,
Q_{i}
is non-zero and hence a non-zero divisor in
A
. Therefore we have that
(Q_{i})
is a Cohen-Macaulay ideal. Since
Q_{i}
is square-free in
R
, it is square-free in
A
and hence
(Q_{i})
is a radical ideal in
A
for all
i\in[t]
. Note that
P\in A^{\prime}:={\mathbb{K}}[g_{1},\cdots,g_{r},f_{1},\cdots,f_{s}]
and
P
is square-free in
R
by assumption. Therefore
P
is also square-free in
A^{\prime}
. We also have
P\not\in(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
in
R
, by assumption. Therefore, by
Corollary

4.30
, if
(P,Q_{i})
is not radical in
R
then there exists
\ell\leq k
and some minimal prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
(Q_{i})
in
A
such that
\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{\ell}}^{A^{\prime}}(P)\in{\mathfrak{q}}R
. Now the minimal primes of
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
(Q_{i})
in
A
are given by the irreducible factors of
Q_{i}
in
A
. Therefore, if
(P,Q_{i})
is not radical, then there exists some irreducible factor of
Q_{i}
in
A
that divides
\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{\ell}}^{A^{\prime}}(P)
. Now
\deg(\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{\ell}}^{A^{\prime}}(P))\leq d(2d-1)
for all
i\in[k]
, as
\deg(P)=d
. Therefore, we have
\deg(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{\ell}}^{A^{\prime}}(P))\leq d^{2}(2d-1)
as
k\leq d
. Therefore the number of irreducible factors of
\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathrm{Disc}_{g_{\ell}}^{A^{\prime}}(P)
in
R
is at most
d^{2}(2d-1)
. If
t>d^{2}(2d-1)
, then by the pigeonhole principle there exist
i\neq j
such that
Q_{i},Q_{j}
have a common irreducible factor in
A
. This is a contradiction since
Q_{i},Q_{j}
do not have common factors in
R
for any
i\neq j
.
∎

5
Strength of forms and Strong Ananyan-Hochster spaces

In this section we describe the main tool which we will use to prove our Sylvester-Gallai theorem – strong Ananyan-Hochster algebras.
As mentioned in
Section

1
, we would like to construct small algebras which behave as polynomial rings and contain many of the polynomials in our configuration.
As we saw in
Section

4
, for an algebra to “behave as a polynomial ring” it is enough to construct an algebra whose generators form a prime sequence.
Another property that we would like from our algebras, is that they are robust with regards to certain augmentations, as we will increase such algebras to contain more and more forms from our configuration.
Moreover, we would like to preserve the structure of the original algebra inside of the augmented one – a concept which we will make precise in
Lemma

5.15
.

In a recent breakthrough work by Ananyan-Hochster
[
3
]
, where they positively answer Stillman’s conjecture, they show that given a set of polynomials
\mathcal{H}:=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}\}
in a polynomial ring
S
, one can construct a sub-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subset S
such that
\mathcal{H}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
and the number of generators of
{\mathcal{A}}
is uniformly bounded
by a function of the size of
\mathcal{H}
and the degrees of the polynomials in
\mathcal{H}
.

Building on
[
3
]
, we define the notion of a strong Ananyan-Hochster algebra (which we denote henceforth by strong AH algebra).
We show that strong AH algebras have excellent algebraic-geometric properties and are particularly suitable for applications to Sylvester-Gallai problems as described above.
In order to construct such algebras, we need to define a notion of rank for a form in
S
.
The notion of rank that we describe below is called strength and was introduced in
[
3
,
4
]
.
This notion can be seen as a symmetric generalization of the notion of slice rank of a tensor
[
62
]
.

5.1
Strength

Let
R=\oplus_{d\geq 0}R_{d}
be a finitely generated graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, generated by
R_{1}
.
In
[
3
]
the notions of collapse and strength were defined for a polynomial ring.
We will extend those definitions to finitely generated graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras and prove the necessary properties below.
Henceforth, we refer to a homogeneous element of
R
as a
form
, adopting the same notation for polynomial rings.

Definition 5.1

(Collapse)
.

Given a non-zero form
F\in R_{d}
, we say that
F
has a
k
-collapse
if there exist
k
forms
G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}
such that
1\leq\deg(G_{i})<d
and
F\in(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
.

Definition 5.2

(Strength)
.

Given a non-zero form
F\in R_{d}
, the
strength
of
F
, denoted by
s(F)
, is the least positive integer such that
F
has a
(s(F)+1)
-collapse but it has no
s(F)
-collapse.
We say that
s(F)\geq t
whenever
F
does not have a
t
-collapse.

Remark 5.3
.

By the definitions above, a form
x\in R_{1}
does not have a
k
-collapse for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Thus, we say that for any
x\in R_{1}
,
s(x)=\infty
. In particular, linear forms in the polynomial ring
S
have infinite strength.

We will make the convention that
s(0)=-1
.

Definition 5.4

(Minimum collapse)
.

Given a non-zero form
F\in R_{d}
and
s\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
such that
s(F)=s-1
, a
minimum collapse
of
F
is any identity of the form
F=G_{1}H_{1}+\cdots+G_{s}H_{s}
, where
G_{i},H_{i}
are forms of degree in
[d-1]
.

It is useful to define the min and max strength of a linear system of forms of the same degree.

Definition 5.5

(Min and max strength)
.

Given a set of forms
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}\in R_{d}
, define

s_{\min}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
as the
minimum
strength of a
non-zero
form in
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}\right\}
and
s_{\max}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{r})
as the
maximum
strength of a form in
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}\right\}
.

In particular, given any non-zero finite dimensional vector space
V\subset R_{d}
, define
s_{\min}(V)
(
s_{\max}(V)
) as the minimum (maximum) strength of any non-zero form in
V
. If
V=(0)
, then there are no non-zero forms in
V
. In this case, by convention we define
s_{\min}((0))=s_{\max}((0))=\infty
.
We will say that a vector space
V
is
k
-strong if
s_{\min}(V)\geq k
. Note that the zero vector space is infinitely strong.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
z
be a new variable and consider the polynomial ring
S[z]
.

1.

Let
F\in S
be a form of degree
d
with
s(F)=s
. Then,
s(F)=s
as a form in
S[z]
, and for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}
, we have
s\leq s(F-\alpha z^{d})\leq s+1
in
S[z]
.

2.

Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\in S
be linearly independent forms. Let
G_{i}\in S[z]
be defined as
G_{i}=F_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{\deg(F_{i})}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
. Then
G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}
are linearly independent.

3.

Let
V\subset S_{d}
be a
k
-strong vector space with a basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
. Let
G_{i}\in S[z]
be defined as
G_{i}=F_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{d}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
. Then
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}\right\}
is a
k
-strong vector space in
S[z]
.

Proof.

1. If
F
has a
k
-collapse in
S
, then it is also a
k
-collapse in
S[z]
. Conversely, let
F=f_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+f_{k}g_{k}
be a
k
-collapse in
S[z]
. Then modulo
(z)
, we have
F=\overline{f}_{1}\overline{g}_{1}+\cdots+\overline{f}_{k}\overline{g}_{k}
, which is a
\ell
-collapse for some

\ell\leq k
, where
\overline{f}_{i},\overline{g}_{j}
are the images of
f_{i},g_{j}
modulo
(z)
. Thus we must have
k\geq\ell\geq s+1
in
S
. Therefore strength of
s(F)=s
in
S[z]
.

If
F
has a
k
-collapse in
S
, then
F-\alpha z^{d}
has a
(k+1)
-collapse in
S[z]
. Hence
s(F-\alpha z^{d})\leq s+1
. Now let
F-\alpha z^{d}=f_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+f_{k}g_{k}
be a
k
-collapse in
S[z]
. Then modulo
(z)
, we have
F=\overline{f}_{1}\overline{g}_{1}+\cdots+\overline{f}_{k}\overline{g}_{k}
, which is a
\ell
-collapse for some

\ell\leq k
, where
\overline{f}_{i},\overline{g}_{j}
are the images of
f_{i},g_{j}
modulo
(z)
. Thus we must have
k\geq s+1
. Hence
s(F-\alpha z^{d})\geq s
.

2. If
\sum\beta_{i}G_{i}=\sum\beta_{i}F_{i}-(\sum\beta_{i}\alpha_{i})z^{d}=0
for some choice of
\beta_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
, then we have
\sum\beta_{i}F_{i}=(\sum\beta_{i}\alpha_{i})z^{d}
.
Thus we must have
\sum\beta_{i}F_{i}=0
and hence
\beta_{i}=0
for all
i
.

3. Let
U=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}\right\}
.
Then for any
G\in U\setminus\{0\}
, we have
G=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(F_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{d})=F-\alpha z^{d}
for some
\lambda_{i},\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}
where
F=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}F_{i}
.
Note that
F\neq 0
as
G\neq 0
. Thus
s(G)=s(F-\alpha z^{d})\geq s(F)\geq k
, by part (1).
∎

5.2
Strong Ananyan-Hochster Vector Spaces

In
[
3
]
, the authors proved that if a form
F
is
sufficiently strong
then the singular locus of the hypersurface
V(F)
is small.
More generally they show that if
s_{\min}(F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})
is sufficiently high then
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
sequence for suitable
\eta\geq 1
. We recall their main theorem,
[
3
, Theorem A]
below.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
{\mathbb{K}}
be an algebraically closed field,
N
be a positive integer and
S:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.

1.

There exists an ascending function
A(\eta,d)\geq d-1\geq 0
of
\eta,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
, such that if
F\in S
is a form of degree
d\geq 1
with
s(F)\geq A(\eta,d)
, then the codimension of the singular locus in
S/(F)
is at least
\eta+1
, i.e.
S/(F)
satisfies the
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-property.

2.

For every
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists an ascending function
B_{\eta}=(B_{\eta,1},\cdots,B_{\eta,d}):{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
with the following property:

If
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}\subset S
is a graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector subspace with
\dim V=n
and dimension sequence
\delta=(\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{d})
, such that for every
i\in[d]
, we have
s_{\min}(V_{i})\geq B_{\eta,i}(\delta)
, then every sequence of
{\mathbb{K}}
-linearly independent forms in
V
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence.

3.

In part (2) above, it is sufficient to have
B_{\eta,i}(\delta)=A(\eta,i)+3(n-1)
, where
\delta=(\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{d})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
n=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{i}
.

Note that the bounds
A(\eta,d)
and
B_{\eta}
are independent of the field
{\mathbb{K}}
and the number of variables
N
in
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.
Theorem

5.7
is the main technical ingredient in the proof of Stillman’s conjecture in
[
3
]
, where the authors proved that the projective dimension of an ideal
I\subset S
admits a bound independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
and
N
.

The theorem above motivates our definition of strong Ananyan-Hochster algebras.
Informally, a strong Ananyan-Hochster algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subset S
is a
{\mathbb{K}}
-subalgebra generated by a sequence
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
of forms such that
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\right\}
is extremely strong.
In our setting, we will enforce the strength to be much higher than the Ananyan-Hochster functions
B_{\eta,i}
, which will ensure that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
remains an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence even after adjoining a constant number of forms to the subalgebra.
This robustness property with respect to augmentation of forms is crucial in our applications to Sylvester-Gallai configurations.

Definition 5.8

(Strong Ananyan-Hochster vector spaces)
.

Let
R=\oplus_{d\geq 0}R_{d}
be a finitely generated graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, generated by
R_{1}
.
For any function
B=(B_{1},\cdots,B_{d}):{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, we will say that a non-zero graded vector subspace
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}\subset R
, with dimension sequence
\delta
, is a
B
-strong AH vector space if
V_{i}
is
B_{i}(\delta)
-strong for all
i
, i.e.
s_{\min}(V_{i})\geq B_{i}(\delta)
.
The subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]\subset R
generated by a
B
-strong AH vector space
V
is called a
B
-strong AH algebra.

Note that if
V=(0)
, then
V
is
B
-strong for any function
B
, since the vector space
s_{\min}((0))=\infty
.
We note that
Theorem

5.7
implies that any homogeneous basis of a strong AH-vector space in
S
is a
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence if
B_{i}(\delta)
are sufficiently large.

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}\subset S
be a
B
-strong AH vector space for some
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Suppose
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for some
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then any sequence of
{\mathbb{K}}
-linearly independent forms in
V
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence. If
\eta\geq 3
, then
S/(V)
is a Cohen-Macaulay, unique factorization domain.

Proof.

Since
B_{i}(\delta)\geq B_{\eta,i}(\delta)
, we see that any sequence of
{\mathbb{K}}
-linearly independent forms in
V
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence by
Theorem

5.7
. If
\eta\geq 3
, then
S/(V)
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD by
Proposition

4.12
.
∎

For any
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, we define the translation function
t_{\mu}:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
as
t_{\mu}=(t_{\mu,1},\cdots,t_{\mu,d})
where the
i
-th component is defined by
t_{\mu,i}(\delta)=\delta_{i}+\mu_{i}
. In other words, for all
i\in[d]
we add
\mu_{i}
to the
i
-th component of
\delta
. For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let
t_{n}:=t_{(n,\cdots,n)}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
be an ascending function and
U\subset S
be a graded vector space in
S
with dimension sequence
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Let
R=S/(U)
and
V\subset R
be a graded vector subspace with dimension sequence
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
.

1.

Let
F\in S
be a form and
\overline{F}\in R
be the image of
F
in
R
. Then
s(\overline{F})\leq s(F)
. Furthermore, there exists a form
H_{1}\in U
, such that
s(\overline{F})\geq s(F-H_{1})-\dim(U)
.

2.

If
V
is
B
-strong in
R
, then for any homogeneous basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\in R
of
V
and any set of homogeneous lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\in S
, we have that
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
.

3.

Suppose
U
is
B\circ t_{\mu}
-strong in
S
and
V
is
B\circ t_{\delta}
-strong in
R
. Then, for any basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\in R
of
V
and any set of lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\in S
, we have that
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
.

4.

Suppose
B_{i}(\alpha+\beta)\geq B_{i}(\beta)+\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
i\in[d]
. Suppose that for any homogeneous basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
of
V
and any set of homogeneous lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\in S
, we have
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
. Then
V
is
B
-strong in
R
.

5.

Suppose
U
is
B\circ t_{n}
-strong for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in R_{1}
be linear forms. Then
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
is a prime sequence in
R
.

Proof.

1. Suppose
F\in S
has a
k
-collapse in
S
. Then we have that the image
\overline{F}
in
R
has an at most
k
-collapse in
R
. Thus
s(\overline{F})\leq s(F)
. Suppose
F
is of degree
i
and
\overline{F}=g_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+g_{k}h_{k}
is a
k
-collapse in
R
. Then we have
F=H+\widetilde{g}_{1}\widetilde{h}_{1}+\cdots+\widetilde{g}_{k}\widetilde{h}_{k}
where
H\in(U)
and
\widetilde{g}_{j},\widetilde{h}_{j}
are homogeneous lifts in
S
. We may write
H=H_{1}+H_{2}
, where
H_{1}\in U_{i}
and
H_{2}\in(U_{<i})
. Then
F-H_{1}=H_{2}+\widetilde{g}_{1}\widetilde{h}_{1}+\cdots+\widetilde{g}_{k}\widetilde{h}_{k}
is an at most
(\dim(U)+k)
-collapse of
F-H_{1}
. Hence, we have
\dim(U)+k\geq s(F-H_{1})+1
. Therefore,
k\geq s(F-H_{1})-\dim(U)+1
. Since
k
was arbitrary, we conclude that
s(\overline{F})\geq s(F-H_{1})-\dim(U)
.

2. Note that
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
has the same dimension sequence as
V
. For any non-zero
G\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
, we have that
s(G)\geq s(\overline{G})
by part
(1)
. Thus
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is also
B
-strong.

3. Let
W=U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
. If
G\in U_{i}
is a non-zero form, then
G
is
B_{i}(\delta+\mu)
-strong. Let
G\in W_{i}\setminus U_{i}
. We may write
G=H+\sum\alpha_{j}\widetilde{F_{j}}
where
H\in U_{i}
and
\deg(\widetilde{F}_{j})=i
. Then the image
\overline{G}=\sum\alpha_{j}F_{j}\in V
is non-zero in
R
, as
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
are linearly independent in
R
. Then by part
(2)
, we have that
G
is
B_{i}(\delta+\mu)
-strong. Hence
W
is
B
-strong.

4. Let
F\in V_{i}
be a non-zero homogeneous element and
\widetilde{F}\in S_{i}
be a lift. We may construct a basis of
V
by extending
F
and choose a set of lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}
extending
\widetilde{F}_{1}=\widetilde{F}
. By part
(1)
, we have
s(F)\geq s(\widetilde{F}-H_{1})-\dim(U)
, for some
H_{1}\in U_{i}
. Now we have
s(\widetilde{F}-H_{1})\geq B_{i}(\delta+\mu)\geq B_{i}(\mu)+\dim(U)
. Hence
s(F)\geq B_{i}(\mu)
and thus
V
is
B
-strong in
R
.

5. Let
\widetilde{a}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{a}_{n}\in S_{1}
be homogeneous lifts of
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
to
S
. Since
U
is
B\circ t_{n}
-strong in
S
and
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
is infinitely strong, we know that the vector space
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{a}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{a}_{n}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
by part (4). Hence, by
Corollary

5.9
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{a}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{a}_{n}\right\}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence, and in particular a prime sequence in
S
. Therefore, by
Proposition

4.12
, we conclude that
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
is a prime sequence in
R
.
∎

5.3
Lifted strength

Suppose that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\in S
are forms such that
V=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
, where
B
satisfies the Ananyan-Hochster bound given by
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(3,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
. Then by
Corollary

5.9
, we know that
S/(V)
is a UFD. We would like to obtain a similar statement where
V
is a strong vector space in a finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra
R
. However, the analogous statement is not necessarily true even if
V
is infinitely strong and
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD, as shown in the following example.

Example 5.11
.

Let
F=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{5}^{2}
and
R=S/(F)
. Then
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD. Consider the images
x_{1},x_{2}\in R
. Then
V=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{x_{1},x_{2}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
R
for any function
B:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
. However,
R/(V)=S/(x_{3}^{2}+x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2})
is not a UFD.

The above example shows that a direct generalization of
Theorem

5.7
or
Corollary

5.9
is not true for UFDs in general. However, note that if
R=S/(x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{7}^{2})
, then
R/(x_{1},x_{2})
is a UFD. In this case
x_{1},x_{2},x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{7}^{2}
is a sequence strong enough so that the quotient of
S
is a UFD. Motivated by this observation, we define the notion of lifted strength. We show that if
R
is a finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra which is Cohen-Macaulay and a UFD, and
V
is a lifted strong vector space, then the quotient
R/(V)
is also a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.

Definition 5.12

(Lifted strength)
.

Let
U\subset S
be a graded vector space and
R=S/(U)
.
Let
F\in R_{d}
be a non-zero form.
We define the lifted strength of
F
with respect to
U
as

\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,F):=\min\{s_{\min}(U_{d}+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}\right\})\}

where
\widetilde{F}
varies over all forms in
S_{d}
such that the image of
\widetilde{F}
in
R
is
F
.
Given a set of forms
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\in R_{d}
, we define

\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})=\min\{s_{\min}(U_{d}+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\})\},

where
\widetilde{F}_{i}
varies over all forms in
S_{d}
such that the image of
\widetilde{F}_{i}
in
R
is
F_{i}
.
Given a non-zero vector space
V\subset R_{d}
, we define

\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,V)=\min\{\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U_{d},F_{1},\cdots,F_{m})\},

where
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
vary over all possible bases of
V
.
We say that
V\subset R_{d}
is
k
-lifted strong with respect to
U
if
\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,V)\geq k
.
For simplicity, we omit
U
from the notation and write
\widetilde{s}_{\min}(V)
when
U
is clear from the context.

Let
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}\subset R
be a graded vector space.
For any function,
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
we will say that
V
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
, if
V_{i}
is
B_{i}(\dim(U_{i})+\dim(V_{i}))
-lifted strong, i.e.
\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,V_{i})\geq B_{i}(\dim(U_{i})+\dim(V_{i}))
for all
i\in[d]
.
In other words,
V
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
, if the vector space
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
, for any homogeneous basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\in R
of
V
and any set of homogeneous lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\in S
.

Remark 5.13
.

Throughout this remark, we will have
R:=S/(U)
.

1.

If
U=0
, then
V\subset S
is
B
-lifted strong iff
V
is
B
-strong.

2.

The two notions
B
-lifted strong and
B
-strong in
R
are not equivalent in general.
For example, let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},y_{1},\cdots,y_{2m}]
and
U=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\right\}
.
Let
F=y_{1}y_{2}+\cdots+y_{2m-1}y_{2m}
and
V=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F\right\}\subset R
.
Then
s(F)=m-1
and
V
is
(m-1)
-strong in
R
.
Now if
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
is a function such that
B_{2}(n,1)>B_{2}(0,1)=m-1
, then
V
is
B
-strong in
R
but it is not
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
.
We will show in
Proposition

5.14
, that under suitable conditions on the function
B
, we have that
B
-lifted strong implies
B
-strong in
R
.

3.

If
0\neq U\subset S
has dimension sequence
\delta
and
V\subset R
is a
B
-lifted strong vector space with dimension sequence
\mu
, then we must have that
U_{i}
is
B_{i}(\delta+\mu)
-strong for all
i\in[d]
.
Hence, a
B
-lifted strong vector space of dimension sequence
\mu
can exist in
R
only if
U
itself was
B(\delta+\mu)
-strong in
S
to begin with.
In particular, if
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for some
\eta\geq 3
, by
Corollary

5.9
, any sequence of
{\mathbb{K}}
-linearly independent forms in
U
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
.
Thus,
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.
In other words, if there exists a
B
-lifted strong vector space
V\subset R
, then
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
be an ascending function such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for some
\eta\geq 3
. Let
U\subset S
be a graded vector space in
S
with dimension sequence
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Let
R=S/(U)
and
V\subset R
be a graded vector subspace with dimension sequence
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
.

1.

We have
s_{\min}(V_{i})\leq\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,V_{i})
, for all
i\in[d]

2.

If
V
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
, then
V
is
B
-strong in
R
.

3.

If there exists a homogeneous basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
of
V
and a set of homogeneous lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\in S
, such that
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
2B
-strong in
S
, then
V
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
.

4.

If
V\subset R
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
, then any sequence of
{\mathbb{K}}
-linearly independent homogenous elements of
V
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence (and also a prime sequence in
R
), and
R/(V)
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.

Proof.

1. Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
of
V_{i}
be a homogeneous basis and a set of homogeneous lifts
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\in S
.
For any non-zero form
F\in U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
, we have
s(\overline{F})\leq s(F)
by
Proposition

5.10
.
Thus we have
s_{\min}(V)\leq\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,V)
.

2. Note that
B_{i}(\alpha+\beta)\geq B_{i}(\beta)+\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
i\in[d]
. Thus by
Proposition

5.10
part
(4)
, we have
V
is
B
-strong in
R
.

3. Let
G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}
be another homogeneous basis of
V
and
\widetilde{G}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{G}_{m}\in S
a set of homogeneous lifts. For any non-zero form
G\in U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{G}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{G}_{m}\right\}
of degree
i
, we have that
G=F+H
, where
F\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
and
H\in(U)
are homogeneous of degree
i
. We may write
H=H_{1}+H_{2}
, where
H_{1}\in U_{i}
and
H_{2}\in(U_{<i})
are homogeneous of degree
i
. Therefore, if
G
has a
k
-collapse, then
F+H_{1}
has an at most
(\dim(U)+k)
-collapse. Now
F+H_{1}\in U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
, which is a
2B
-strong vector space of dimension sequence
\delta+\mu
. Hence,
\dim(U)+k\geq 2B_{i}(\delta+\mu)+1\geq B_{i}(\delta+\mu)+\dim(U)+1
. Therefore we have
k\geq B_{i}(\delta+\mu)+1
. So we have
\widetilde{s}_{\min}(U,V_{i})\geq B_{i}(\delta+\mu)
for all
i\in[d]
and hence
V
is
B
-lifted strong.

4. If
V\subset R
is
B
-lifted strong, then
W:=U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S
for any homogeneous basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
of
V
.
Therefore, by
Corollary

5.9
, any homogeneous basis of
W
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
.
In particular, any homogeneous basis of
W
, extending
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
.
By
Proposition

4.12
,
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R=S/(U)
.
By
Proposition

4.12
and
\eta\geq 3
,
S/(W)
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.
Hence
R/(V)=S/(W)
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD.
∎

5.4
Strengthening and Robustness

We will show that given a graded vector space
U
, we can always find a strong AH-vector space
V
such that
{\mathbb{K}}[U]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
. Furthermore, we can construct
V
in a way such that any element of
U
that was
sufficiently strong
is an element of
V
. We follow the proof of
[
3
, Theorem B]
to construct such strong AH-algebras below.

Lemma 5.15

(Strengthening of Algebras)
.

For any
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a function
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow N^{d}
,
there exist functions
C_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
h_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, depending on
B
, such that the following holds:

Given a graded vector space
U=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}U_{i}\subset S
with dimension sequence
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, there exists a
B
-strong AH vector space
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}
such that

1.

{\mathbb{K}}[U]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
,

2.

for all
i\in[d]
, we have
\dim(V_{i})\leq C_{B,i}(\delta)
, where
C_{B,i}
denotes the
i
-th component of
C_{B}=(C_{B,1},\cdots,C_{B,d}):{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
.

Furthermore, suppose
H=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}H_{i}\subset U
is a graded subspace such that
s_{\min}(H_{i})\geq h_{B,i}(\delta)
for all
i\in[d]
.
Then there exists a
B
-strong AH vector space
V
satisfying (1) and (2) above such that
H\subset V
.

Proof.

For any graded vector space
U=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}U_{i}
with dimension sequence
\delta=(\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{d})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, we define an iterative process below.
We will construct
V
as an outcome of this iterative process.

1.

Set
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}:=U
, with
U_{i}=V_{i}
for all
i\in[d]
.

2.

While
V
is not
B
-strong:

•

Let
\ell:=\max\{i\in[d]|s_{\min}(V_{i})<B_{i}(\delta)\}
.

•

Pick
P\in V_{\ell}\setminus(0)
such that
s(P)=s-1<B_{i}(\delta)
. Choose a basis
\mathcal{F}
of
V_{\ell}
that contains
P
. Let
P=f_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+f_{s}g_{s}
be a
s
-collapse.

•

Set
V_{\ell}\leftarrow\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\setminus\{P\}\right\}
and
\oplus_{i<\ell}V_{i}\leftarrow\oplus_{i<\ell}V_{i}+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{s},g_{1},\cdots,g_{s}\right\}
as a graded vector space .

•

Set
V\leftarrow\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}
.

Note that after each iteration of the while loop we preserve the condition
{\mathbb{K}}[U]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
. Consider the reverse lexicographic ordering on the dimension sequences
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Note that the reverse lexicographic ordering is a well-ordering. We will inductively define the functions
C_{B,i}(\delta)
and prove by induction on
\delta
that the iterative process terminates after finitely many steps with the desired bound on dimension. If
d=1
, then
U
is
B
-strong for any function
B
. Therefore the iterative process ends at the first step with
V=U
and we may take
C_{B,1}(\delta)=\delta=\dim(U)
in this case. Thus the statement holds for any
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and any dimension sequence of the form
\delta=(\delta_{1},0,\cdots,0)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
with
C_{B,i}(\delta)=\delta_{i}
for all
i\in[d]
.

Let
d\geq 2
. Suppose that the iterative process terminates for all
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
, and
\dim(V)
is bounded by a function
C_{B,i}(\delta^{\prime})
defined for all
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
.
If
U
is
B
-strong, the iterative process stops at the first step and we may take
V=U
.
If
U
is not
B
-strong, then let
\ell\in[d]
be as in the definition of the while loop above.
Then after one iteration of the while loop we see that
\dim(V_{>\ell})
remains unchanged,
\dim(V_{\ell})
drops by
1
and
\dim(V_{<\ell})
increases by at most
2B_{\ell}(\delta)
.
Thus, after the first iteration of the while loop we end up with a vector space
V
with dimension sequence
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
and
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta^{\prime}_{i}<\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{i}+2B_{\ell}(\delta)
.
Therefore, by induction the iterative process stops after finitely many steps.
Note that
\dim(V_{d})
never increases throughout the process, so it is enough to take
C_{B,d}(\delta)=\delta_{d}
.
For
i<d
, we define
C_{B,i}(\delta)=\max\{\delta_{i},C_{B,i}(\delta^{\prime})\}
where
\delta^{\prime}
varies over the set of
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta^{\prime}_{i}<\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{i}+2B_{\max}(\delta)
with
B_{\max}(\delta)=\max\{B_{i}(\delta)|i\in[d]\}
.

We will define the function
h_{B}
inductively and show that if
s_{\min}(H_{i})\geq h_{B,i}(\delta)
then we may choose
P
and
\mathcal{F}
at each step of the iterative process such that the condition
H\subset V
is preserved. Suppose
d=1
, then
U=V
and we have
H\subset V=U
. So we may take
h_{B,1}(\delta)=B_{1}(\delta)
in this case.
Suppose
d\geq 2
and
\delta=(\delta_{1},0\cdots,0)
. Then again
H\subset U=V
and we may take
h_{B,i}(\delta_{1},0,\cdots,0)=B_{i}(\delta)
for all
i
. In general, we define
h_{B,i}(\delta)=\max\{B_{i}(\delta),h_{B,i}(\delta^{\prime})\}
where
\delta^{\prime}
varies over the set of
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta^{\prime}_{i}<\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{i}+2B_{\max}(\delta)
.

Let
d\geq 2
. Suppose that for any
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
and a subspace
H\subset U
with
s_{\min}(H_{i})\geq h_{B,i}(\delta^{\prime})
, we can run the iterative process such that at each step we preserve the condition
H\subset V
, and the process terminates with
H\subset V
. Let
U
be a graded vector space of dimension sequence
\delta
and
H\subset U
a graded subspace such that
s_{\min}(H_{i})\geq h_{B,i}(\delta)
. We fix a basis
\mathcal{F}_{H}
of
H
. If
U
is
B
-strong then we have
H\subset U=V
. If
U
is not
B
-strong, let
\ell\in[d]
be as in the definition of the while loop above. Note that
s_{\min}(H_{\ell})\geq h_{B,\ell}(\delta)\geq B_{\ell}(\delta)
. Therefore, we must have
P\not\in H_{\ell}
in the iterative process above. Thus we may choose a basis
\mathcal{F}
of
V_{\ell}
, containing
P
such that it also contains the basis
\mathcal{F}_{H}
of
H
. Then, at the end of this iteration of the while loop, we end up with a vector space
V
with dimension sequence
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
and
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta^{\prime}_{i}<\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{i}+2B_{\max}(\delta)
. We see that
H_{i}\subset V_{i}
for all
i\in[d]
, by the choice of the basis
\mathcal{F}
. Now,
s_{\min}(H_{i})\geq h_{B,i}(\delta)\geq h_{B,i}(\delta^{\prime})
for all
i\in[d]
. Therefore, by induction, we may run the iterative process such that it terminates with a vector space
V
with
H\subset V
.
∎

Corollary 5.16

(Robustness of strong algebras)
.

Let
B,G:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Suppose that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq h_{G,i}(\delta+\mu)
for all
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
i\in[d]
, where
h_{G}:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
is the function defined in
Lemma

5.15
. Let
U\subset S
be a
B
-strong AH vector space and
W\subset S
is a graded vector space with dimension sequences
\delta
and
\mu
respectively. Then there exists a
G
-strong AH vector space
V
such that

1.

{\mathbb{K}}[U+W]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
,

2.

U\subset V
,

3.

for all
i\in[d]
,
\dim(V_{i})\leq C_{G,i}(\delta+\mu)
, where
C_{G}:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
is the function defined in
Lemma

5.15
.

Proof.

Consider the graded vector space
U^{\prime}=U+W
. Then we have that
\delta^{\prime}_{i}:=\dim(U^{\prime}_{i})\leq\delta_{i}+\mu_{i}
for all
i\in[d]
. Since
U
is
B
-strong, we have
s_{\min}(U_{i})\geq B_{i}(\delta)
for all
i
. Now
U\subset U^{\prime}
is a graded subspace such that
s_{\min}(U_{i})\geq B_{i}(\delta)\geq h_{G,i}(\delta+\mu)\geq h_{G,i}(\delta^{\prime})
. Let
V
be the
G
-strong AH vector space constructed by applying
Lemma

5.15
to the vector space
U^{\prime}
along with the subspace
H=U
. Then, by
Lemma

5.15
, we know that
{\mathbb{K}}[U^{\prime}]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
and
\dim(V_{i})\leq C_{G,i}(\delta^{\prime})\leq C_{G,i}(\delta+\mu)
. Furthermore, we have
U\subset V
, since
s_{\min}(U_{i})\geq h_{G,i}(\delta^{\prime})
for all
i\in[d]
.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Let
U\subset S
be a graded vector space with dimension sequence
\delta_{U}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and let
R=S/(U)
. Let
V\subset R
is a graded vector space with dimension sequence
\delta_{V}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
. Suppose
V
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{k}
-lifted strong with respect to
U
. Let
P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}\in R_{\leq d}
be homogeneous elements. Then there exists a graded vector space
V^{\prime}\subset R_{\leq d}
such that:

1.

V^{\prime}
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
.

2.

P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}\in{\mathbb{K}}[V^{\prime}]
.

3.

V\subset V^{\prime}
.

4.

for all
i\in[d]
, we have
\dim(V^{\prime}_{i})\leq C_{2B,i}(t_{k}(\delta_{U}+\delta_{V}))-\delta_{U,i}
.

Proof.

Let
m=\delta_{V}
and
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}
be homogeneous lifts of a homogeneous basis of
V
. Let
\widetilde{P}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{P}_{k}\in S
be homogeneous lifts of
P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}
. Note that
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{k}
-strong in
S
, as
V
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{k}
-lifted strong in
R
. By applying
Corollary

5.16
to the vector space
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m},\widetilde{P}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{P}_{k}\right\}
, we may construct a
2B
-strong vector space
V^{\prime\prime}\subset S
such that
V^{\prime\prime}
contains
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{m}\right\}
and
\widetilde{P}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{P}_{k}\in{\mathbb{K}}[V^{\prime\prime}]
. Let
V^{\prime}\subset R
be the image of
V^{\prime\prime}
in
R
. Then
V\subset V^{\prime}
and
P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}\in{\mathbb{K}}[V^{\prime}]
. By
Proposition

5.14
, we know that
V^{\prime}
is
B
-lifted strong in
R
. Furthermore, we know that
\dim(V^{\prime\prime}_{i})\leq C_{2B,i}(t_{k}(\delta_{U}+\delta_{V}))
. Therefore, we have
\dim(V^{\prime}_{i})\leq C_{2B,i}(t_{k}(\delta_{U}+\delta_{V}))-\delta_{U,i}
.
∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
\eta\geq 3
. Let
U\subset S_{\leq d}
be a graded vector space and let
R=S/(U)
. Let
V\subset R_{\leq d}
be a graded vector space. Suppose that
V
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{1}
-lifted strong with respect to
U
, where
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
