is such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
i\in[d]
. Let
F,G,H\in R_{\leq d}
be homogeneous elements of positive degree. Suppose
F,G\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
and
F,G
do not have any common factors in
R
. If
H\not\in(V)
then
F,G,H
is a regular sequence in
R
.

Proof.

Let
V^{\prime}\subset R
be the
B
-lifted strong graded vector space constructed by applying
Corollary

5.17
to
V
and
H
. In particular,
V\subset V^{\prime}
and
H\in{\mathbb{K}}[V^{\prime}]
. Note that, by
Remark

5.13
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
U
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
and that
R
is a graded UFD. Furthermore, by
Proposition

5.14
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
V
or
V^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence (and also a prime sequence) in
R
.

Note that
F,G
is a regular sequence in
R
, by
Lemma

4.5
. Since
R
is Cohen-Macaulay by
Corollary

5.9
, we know that
(F,G)
does not have any embedded primes in
R
. Since
H\not\in(V)
, we conclude that
H
is a non-zero divisor in
R/(F,G)
, by
Corollary

4.23
. Therefore,
F,G,H
is a regular sequence in
R
.

∎

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
d,e,\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
1\leq d\leq e
and
\eta\geq 3
. Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a
h_{2B}\circ t_{2}
-strong vector space in
S
, where
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
is such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
i\in[e]
. Consider the unique factorization domain
R=S/(U)
. Let
P,Q,P_{1},\cdots,P_{m}\in R_{\leq d}
be homogeneous elements of positive degree where
m\geq 2^{d^{2}}
and
\gcd(P,Q)=\gcd(P,P_{i})=\gcd(Q,P_{i})=1
for all
i\in[m]
. If
Q\in\rad(P,P_{i})
for all
i\in[m]
, then we must have
\rad{(P,P_{i})}=\rad(P,P_{k})
for two distinct
i,k\in[m]
.

Proof.

Note that
P,Q
and
P,P_{i}
are regular sequences in
R
.
We have
\rad(P,Q)\subset\rad(P,P_{i})
for all
i\in[m]
, since
Q\in\rad(P,P_{i})
. Let
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{1},\cdots\mathfrak{p}_{\ell}\}
and
\mathcal{S}_{i}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{i1},\cdots,\mathfrak{p}_{i\ell_{i}}\}
be the set of minimal primes over
(P,Q)
and
(P,P_{i})
in
R
, respectively. We have
\bigcap\mathfrak{p}_{j}=\rad(P,Q)\subset\rad(P,P_{i})=\bigcap_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{ij}
. Therefore, by
[
5
, Proposition 1.11]
, we must have that for all
i,j
, there exists
k
such that
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{ij}
. Since we know that
\mathrm{ht}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})=\mathrm{ht}(\mathfrak{p}_{ij})=2
for all
i,j
, we must have that for all
i,j
, there exists
k
such that
\mathfrak{p}_{k}=\mathfrak{p}_{ij}
, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\mathcal{S}
for all
i\in[m]
. Therefore it is enough to show that there exist at most
d^{2}
minimal primes of
(P,Q)
.
Indeed, suppose
|\mathcal{S}|\leq d^{2}
, then there are at most
2^{d^{2}}-1
number of distinct choices for the set of minimal primes
\mathcal{S}_{i}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{ij}\}
of the ideals
(P,P_{i})
.
By the pigeonhole principle, there exist distinct
i,k\in[m]
such that
\mathcal{S}_{i}=\mathcal{S}_{k}
.
Thus,
\rad(P,P_{i})=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\mathfrak{q}=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\mathfrak{p}=\rad(P,P_{k})
.

Now we will show that
|\mathcal{S}|\leq d^{2}
. Since
U
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{2}
-strong in
S
, we note that the vector space
(0)\subset R
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{2}
-lifted strong. Hence, by
Corollary

5.17
, there exists a
B
-lifted strong vector space
W\subset R
such that
P,Q\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
. Let
A:={\mathbb{K}}[W]
. Note that
Proposition

5.14
, any homogeneous basis of
W
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence (and also a prime sequence) in
R
. By
Proposition

4.20
, we know that the minimal primes over
(P,Q)
in
A
are in one-to-one correspondence with the minimal primes over
(P,Q)
in
R
. Now
A
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring and
P,Q
are of degree at most
d
in
A
. Note that
P,Q
do not have any common factors in
A
, since they do not have any common factors in
R
. Also,
(P,Q)\neq A
, since
P,Q\in(W)
as they are homogeneous. Therefore, by
Lemma

4.5
, we have that
P,Q
is a regular sequence in
A
. Therefore, by
Lemma

4.6
, we conclude that the number of minimal primes over
(P,Q)
in
A
is at most
d^{2}
.

∎

6
General Quotients

As discussed in the introduction, our inductive strategy for proving
Theorem

1.8
is to reduce the degree of the SG-configuration by applying a sequence of quotient homomorphisms
R\rightarrow R[z]/(W)
, where
W
is a sufficiently strong vector space. In this section, we describe general quotients and prove the useful properties we need from them in order to reduce the degree of radical Sylvester-Gallai configurations.
The quotients defined here are generalizations of the projection maps used in
[
56
,
51
,
49
,
31
]
.

Throughout this section, we fix positive integers
d,\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
\eta\geq 3
, and
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
denotes an ascending function such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
i\in[d]
, where
A(\eta,i)
is the function provided by
Theorem

5.7
.

Definition 6.1

(Graded Quotients)
.

Let
U=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}U_{i}\subset S
be a graded vector space of dimension sequence
\delta
in
S
and
R:=S/(U)
be the quotient ring.
Let
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{d}V_{i}\subset R
be a graded subspace of dimension sequence
\mu
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
be a homogeneous basis for
V
and
z
be a new variable.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, let
V_{\alpha}:=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{1}-\alpha_{1}z^{\deg(F_{1})},\dots,F_{n}-\alpha_{n}z^{\deg(F_{n})}\right\}
and
I_{\alpha}\subset R[z]
be the homogeneous ideal
I_{\alpha}=(V_{\alpha})
.
We define the graded quotient map
\varphi_{V,\alpha}
as the quotient homomorphism of finitely generated graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras given by

\varphi_{V,\alpha}:R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}.

For simplicity we will often drop the subscripts
V
or
\alpha
, and write
\varphi_{\alpha}
or
\varphi
for our quotient map when there is no ambiguity about the vector space
V
or the vector
\alpha
.

Remark 6.2
.

In the above definition we abuse notation and define
V_{\alpha}
with a fixed basis in mind.
This abuse of notation is only to simplify our definition of the quotients we will use, and the choice of basis is not very important, since
[
3
]
shows that any basis of
V_{\alpha}
forms an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
sequence.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
V\subset R
is
B
-lifted strong with respect to
U
. Then
R[z]
and
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
are quotients of
S[z]
by
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequences, for any choice of
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
In particular, they are Cohen-Macaulay UFDs.

Proof.

Note that by
Remark

5.13
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
U
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
. Therefore, if
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t}
is a homogeneous basis of
U
in
S
, then
G_{1},\cdots,G_{t},z
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S[z]
. Let
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}\in S
be homogeneous lifts of a homogeneous basis of
V
. Then
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}\right\}
is
B
-strong. Let
H_{i}=\widetilde{F}_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{\deg(F_{i})}\in S[z]
. Then by
Proposition

5.6
, we have that
W:=U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{H_{1},\cdots,H_{n}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S[z]
. Thus, any basis of
W
consists of an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
sequence in
S[z]
. Then by
Corollary

5.9
, we know that
R=S/(U)
,
R[z]=S[z]/(U)
and
R[z]/I_{\alpha}\simeq S[z]/(W)
are all Cohen-Macaulay UFD.
∎

General quotients
. We say that a property
\mathcal{P}
holds for a
general

\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, if there exists a non-empty open subset
\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
such that the property
\mathcal{P}
holds for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{X}
. Here
\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
is open with respect to the Zariski topology.
Hence
\mathcal{X}
is the complement of the zero set of finitely many polynomial functions on
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

In particular, given a graded vector space
V\subset R
as in
Definition

6.1
,
we will say that a property
\mathcal{P}
holds for a
general quotient

\varphi_{\alpha}
, if there exists a non-empty open subset
\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
such that the property holds for all
\varphi_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\mathcal{X}
. The general choice of the element
\alpha
defining a general homomorphism
\varphi_{\alpha}
allows us to say that such homomorphisms will avoid any finite set of polynomial constraints. As shown in
[
56
,
51
,
49
]
in the case of general projections, these maps preserve several important properties of polynomials.
In this section we generalize such properties to the setting of graded quotients.

Suppose
V\subset R
is a
B
-strong vector space. Then the subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring
{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
, where
n=\dim(V)
. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, consider the quotient homomorphism
\varphi_{\alpha}:R\rightarrow R[z]/(V_{\alpha})
as defined above. The restriction of
\varphi_{\alpha}
to the subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
is given by
y_{i}\rightarrow\alpha_{i}z^{d_{i}}
under the isomorphism with
{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
. Therefore, we first consider such non-homogeneous quotients of a polynomial ring.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
and
z
be a new variable. Fix positive integers
d_{1},\cdots,d_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, let
I_{\alpha}=(x_{1}-\alpha_{1}z^{d_{1}},\cdots,x_{n}-\alpha_{n}z^{d_{n}})
. Let
\varphi_{\alpha}:S[z]\rightarrow S[z]/I_{\alpha}
be the quotient ring homomorphism.

1.

The ideal
I_{\alpha}
is prime in
S[z]
, and the composition morphism
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\hookrightarrow S[z]\rightarrow S[z]/I_{\alpha}
is injective.

2.

If
F\in{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
is a non-zero polynomial, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\neq 0
in
S[z]/I_{\alpha}
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

3.

Let
F\in S\setminus{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
in
S[z]/I_{\alpha}
, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

4.

If
F\in S
is a non-zero polynomial, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\neq 0
in
S[z]/I_{\alpha}
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

5.

If
F,G\in S
have no common factor, then
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

6.

If
F\in S
is square-free.
Then, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, the multiple factors of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
must be in
{\mathbb{K}}[z]
.

Proof.

1. Consider the ring homomorphism
\vartheta:S[z]\rightarrow{\mathbb{K}}[x_{m+1},\cdots,x_{N},z]
defined by
x_{i}\mapsto\alpha_{i}z^{d_{i}}
for
i\in[m]
, and
x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
for
m+1\leq i\leq N
and
z\mapsto z
.
Note that
\vartheta
is surjective and
\mathrm{ker}(\varphi)=I_{\alpha}
. Therefore
I_{\alpha}
is a prime ideal and
S[z]/I_{\alpha}\simeq{\mathbb{K}}[x_{m+1},\cdots,x_{N},z]
.

Suppose
F\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
such that
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)=0
. Then
F\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]\cap I_{\alpha}
in
S[z]
.
Since
I_{\alpha}\subset(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},z)
, we note that if
g\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
is a polynomial with non-zero constant term, then
g\not\in I_{\alpha}
.
If
F\neq 0
, let
F=z^{r}g(z)
, where
r
is the highest power of
z
diving
F
, and
g(0)\neq 0
.
Therefore, we have that
z^{r}\in I_{\alpha}
, as
I_{\alpha}
is a prime ideal.
Hence,
z\in I_{\alpha}
and thus
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},z)\subset I_{\alpha}
, which implies
\mathrm{ht}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},z)=n+1
.
However,
I_{\alpha}
is generated by
n
polynomials, and thus
\mathrm{ht}(I_{\alpha})\leq n
.
This is a contradiction.

2. We have
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)=F(\alpha_{1}z^{d_{1}},\cdots,\alpha_{n}z^{d_{n}})
is a polynomial in
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\subset S[z]/I_{\alpha}
.
If
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)=0
in
{\mathbb{K}}[z]
, then by setting
z=1
, we obtain
F(\alpha)=0
.
Therefore if
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\setminus V(F)
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\neq 0
.

3. We may write
F
as a polynomial in
x_{m+1},\cdots,x_{N}
with coefficients in
A:={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
.
Since
F\not\in A
, we know that there is a non-zero polynomial
g(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in A
such that
g
is the coefficient of some monomial
\Pi_{j=m+1}^{N}x_{j}^{e_{j}}
in
F
.
By part (2), we know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(g)
is a non-zero polynomial in
{\mathbb{K}}[z]
, for general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
Therefore, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, the coefficient of
\Pi_{j=m+1}^{N}x_{j}^{e_{j}}
in
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
is
\varphi_{\alpha}(g)\neq 0
. Hence
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

4. Follows immediately from parts (2) and (3) above.

5. If
F,G
have no common factor in
S
, then
\mathrm{Res}_{x_{i}}(F,G)\neq 0
for all
x_{i}
. By, part (4), we know that for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we have
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathrm{Res}_{x_{i}}(F,G))\neq 0
for all
i
.
Note that
S[z]/I_{\alpha}={\mathbb{K}}[x_{m+1},\cdots,x_{N},z]
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
Now,
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathrm{Res}_{x_{i}}(F,G))=\mathrm{Res}_{x_{i}}(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))
for
m+1\leq i\leq N
.
Therefore,
\mathrm{Res}_{x_{i}}(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))\neq 0
for
m+1\leq i\leq N
.
Hence,
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
cannot have any common factor depending on
x_{i}
in
{\mathbb{K}}[x_{m+1},\cdots,x_{N},z]
. Hence,
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
.

6. Since
F
is square-free, we know that
F
and
\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}
do not have a common factor for any
i\in[N]
.
Note that we have
\varphi_{\alpha}(\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}})=\frac{\partial\varphi_{\alpha}(F)}{\partial x_{i}}
for
m+1\leq i\leq N
.
If
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
has a multiple factor that is not in
{\mathbb{K}}[z]
, then there exists
k\in[m+1,N]
such that
\frac{\partial\varphi_{\alpha}(F)}{\partial x_{k}}
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
have a common factor that depends on
x_{k}
.
However, by part (5), we know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{k}})
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
do not have a common factor depending on
x_{k}
, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
V\subset R
be a
B
-lifted strong vector space and
\varphi_{\alpha}:R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
be a graded quotient as defined in
Definition

6.1
.

1.

The ideal
I_{\alpha}
is a prime ideal in
R[z]
and the composition
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\hookrightarrow R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
is injective.

2.

If
F\in R\setminus\{0\}
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\neq 0
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

3.

If
F\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]\subset R
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
be the homogeneous basis of
V
used in the quotient map
\varphi_{\alpha}
. By
Proposition

5.14
, we know that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R
.
Thus,
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n},z
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R[z]
. We may extend
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
to a homogeneous system of parameters
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
of
R
.
Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]
,
G_{i}=F_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{\deg(F_{i})}\in A[z]
for

i\in[n]
and
J_{\alpha}=(G_{1},\cdots,G_{n})\subset A[z]
.
Let
\vartheta_{\alpha}:A[z]\rightarrow A[z]/J_{\alpha}
be the quotient map.
We have that
A\subset R
and
A[z]\subset R[z]
are module-finite free extensions, by
Theorem

4.10
.
Now we prove the desired statements below.

1. Note that
R[z]
is the quotient of
S[z]
by an ideal generated by an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence, by
Proposition

6.3
.
Note that,
J_{\alpha}
is a prime ideal in
A[z]
, by
Lemma

6.4
.
Therefore, by
Proposition

4.20
,
I_{\alpha}:=J_{\alpha}R[z]
is prime in
R[z]
and
I_{\alpha}\cap A[z]=J_{\alpha}
in
A[z]
. In particular,
A[z]/J_{\alpha}\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
is injective.
By
Lemma

6.4
,
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\hookrightarrow A[z]\rightarrow A[z]/J_{\alpha}
is injective. Therefore,
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\hookrightarrow R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
is injective.

2. Suppose
\mathrm{rank}(R)=r
as a free
A
-module.
By tensoring with
A[z]
, we obtain that
A[z]\subset R[z]
is a free extension of rank
r
.
Therefore,
R[z]\xrightarrow{\sim}A[z]^{\oplus r}
as
A[z]
-modules.
Hence,

R[z]/I_{\alpha}\simeq R[z]\otimes_{A[z]}(A[z]/J_{\alpha})
is also a free
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
-module of rank
r
. Therefore, we have

R[z]/I_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\sim}(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
. Let
F\in R\subset R[z]
have components
(f_{1},\cdots,f_{r})\in(A[z])^{\oplus r}
under this isomorphism. Note that
\varphi_{\alpha}:R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
commutes with the quotient map
\vartheta_{\alpha}^{\oplus r}:(A[z])^{\oplus r}\rightarrow(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
via the isomorphisms above, i.e. the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[z]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[z]/I_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[z])^{\oplus r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\vartheta_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}}

If
F\neq 0
, then
f_{i}\neq 0
for some
i\in[r]
. Now, by
Lemma

6.4
, we know that for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, the image
\vartheta_{\alpha}(f_{i})\neq 0
. Since
\vartheta_{\alpha}(f_{i})\neq 0
, we see that
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\neq 0
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, by the commutative diagram above.

3. Note that, by
Proposition

4.11
, we may assume that the inclusion
A[z]\subset R[z]
is an isomorphsim onto one of the direct summands of
A[z]^{\oplus r}
via the isomorphism
R[z]\simeq A[z]^{\oplus r}=A[z]\cdot e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus A[z]\cdot e_{r}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that it is an isomorphism with the first direct summand
A[z]\cdot e_{1}
. Tensoring the isomorphism with
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
, we see that the inclusion
A[z]/J_{\alpha}\subset R[z]/I_{\alpha}
is an injection onto the first direct summand of
(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
. Let
F\in R\subset R[z]
have components
(f_{1},\cdots,f_{r})\in(A[z])^{\oplus r}
under this isomorphism, as above. If
F\in R
and
F\not\in A
, then we must have that
f_{i}\neq 0
for some
i>1
. Therefore, we must have that
\vartheta_{\alpha}(f_{i})\neq 0
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, by
Lemma

6.4
. Therefore,
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\not\in(A[z]/J_{\alpha})\cdot e_{1}\subset(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
. Now, note that
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\subset A[z]\subset R[z]
, therefore in the quotient, we have
{\mathbb{K}}[z]\subset(A[z]/J_{\alpha})\cdot e_{1}
. Therefore,
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
∎

The next proposition is a generalization of
[
51
, Claim 2.23]
. Given
F,G\in R
, we show that under a general quotient
\varphi_{\alpha}
, the common factors of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
can only be elements of
{\mathbb{K}}[z]
. In the proof, we would like to construct a strong subalgebra of
R
containing
F,G
and the vector space
V
, where the generators of
V
are preserved. Therefore we need to assume that
V
is sufficiently strong to begin with, so that we can apply
Corollary

5.16
. Recall that for any
a\in{\mathbb{N}}
the translation function
t_{a}:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
is given by
t_{a}(\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{d})=(\delta_{1}+a,\cdots,\delta_{d}+a)
and
h_{B}
is the function defined in
Lemma

5.15
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
G:{\mathbb{N}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
be a function such that
G_{i}(\delta)\geq h_{B,i}\circ t_{2}(\delta)
for all
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
.
Let
V\subset R_{\leq d}
be a
G
-lifted strong vector space and
\varphi_{\alpha}:R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
be a general quotient as defined in
Definition

6.1
.
Let
F,G\in R_{\leq d}
be such that they have no common factor.
Then,

1.

\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]

2.

If
F,G
are homogeneous, then

\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))=z^{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
In particular, we have
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(zF),\varphi_{\alpha}(zG))=z^{k+1}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Furthermore, if
F,G\not\in K[V]\subset R
then
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
,
\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
are linearly independent.

3.

If
F\in R
is a square-free form, then

\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
does not have multiple factors other than
z^{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

1. Recall that
R=S/(U)
.
Let
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}
be homogeneous lifts of a homogeneous basis of
V
.
Let
\widetilde{F},\widetilde{G}\in S
be homogeneous lifts of
F,G
.
Note that
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}\right\}
is
G
-strong in
S
.
By applying
Corollary

5.16
to the vector space
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{F},\widetilde{G}\right\}
, we may construct a
B
-strong vector space
\widetilde{W}\subset S
such that
\widetilde{W}
contains
U+\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}\right\}
and
\widetilde{F},\widetilde{G}\in{\mathbb{K}}[\widetilde{W}]
.
Let
W\subset R
be the image of
\widetilde{W}
in
R
.
Then
V\subset W
and
F,G\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
.
Note that any homogeneous basis of
\widetilde{W}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
, by
Corollary

5.9
.
Hence we know that
W
is generated by an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R
, by
Proposition

4.12
.
Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[W]\subset R
. Let
G_{i}=F_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{\deg{F_{i}}}
and
J_{\alpha}=(G_{1},\cdots,G_{n})\subset A[z]
.
Let
\vartheta_{\alpha}:A[z]\rightarrow A[z]/J_{\alpha}
.
Note that
F,G
do not have a common factor in
A
.
Then, by
Lemma

6.4
, we know that for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we have
\gcd(\vartheta_{\alpha}(F),\vartheta_{\alpha}(G))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
.
Since
A[z]
is generated by an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R[z]
, defining
I_{\alpha}:=J_{\alpha}R[z]
, we note that
I_{\alpha}\cap A=J_{\alpha}
, by
Proposition

4.20
.
Then we have
A[z]/J_{\alpha}\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
is injective and that
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
can be identified with the image of
A[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
.
Hence we have,
\vartheta_{\alpha}(F)=\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
and
\vartheta_{\alpha}(G)=\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
. We will show that

\gcd(\vartheta_{\alpha}(F),\vartheta_{\alpha}(G))
in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
is equal to
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))
in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
.

Let
\widetilde{G}_{i}=\widetilde{F}_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{\deg(F_{i})}
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a homogeneous basis of
\widetilde{W}
in
S
that contains
\widetilde{F}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{F}_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be the sequence of forms obtained by replacing
\widetilde{F}_{i}
with
\widetilde{G}_{i}
for all
i
. Note that, by
Proposition

5.6
, we have that
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\right\}
is
B
-strong in
S[z]
and hence an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S[z]
, by
Corollary

5.9
. Note that the image of
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\right\}
in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
, generates the image of
A[z]
.
Therefore, we see that
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
is identified with a subalgebra generated by an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
. Therefore,
Lemma

4.19
implies that any common factor of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
must be a in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
. Therefore,
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))=\gcd(\vartheta_{\alpha}(F),\vartheta_{\alpha}(G))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

2. By
Proposition

A.2
, we see that if
h\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]\subset R[z]/I_{\alpha}
and
h
is a factor of
F
, then
h
is homogeneous. Thus we must have
h=z^{r}
for some
r
. Similarly for
G
. Therefore, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we have that
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))=z^{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by part (1). If
F,G\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
, then by
Proposition

6.5
, we know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G)\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
. If
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
were linearly dependent, then we must have
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G)\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
which is a contradiction.

3. Since
F\in A={\mathbb{K}}[W]
as above, and
F
is square-free in
R
, we know that
F
is square-free in
A
.
Let
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)=z^{k}f_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots f_{r}^{e_{r}}
be an irreducible factorization in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
.
Since
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\in A[z]/J_{\alpha}
, we know that
f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\in A[z]/J_{\alpha}
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)=z^{k}f_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots f_{r}^{e_{r}}
is an irreducible factorization in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
, by
Lemma

4.19
.
By applying
Lemma

6.4
to
A
, we know that the multiple factors of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
must be in
{\mathbb{K}}[z]
, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
Since
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
is a homogeneous element of
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
, we conclude that any multiple factor of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
must be a homogeneous element of
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
, and hence of the form
z^{\ell}
for some
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Since
z
does not divide
f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}
in
A[z]/J_{\alpha}
, we conclude that
e_{i}=1
for all
i\in[r]
.
Hence, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we see that the only multiple factor
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
is
z^{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Given two elements
F,G\in R
, the results above show that under a general quotient
\varphi_{\alpha}
, we have that
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G)
satisfy certain properties such as non-vanishing and
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
etc. In other words, given any such property
\mathcal{P}
, there exists a non-empty open subset
\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
(depending on
F,G
) such that
\mathcal{P}
holds for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}\}\subset R
be a finite set and
\mathcal{P}
a property as above. We may take the finite intersection of the non-empty open subsets
\mathcal{X}_{ij}
corresponding to pairs of forms
F_{i},F_{j}
to assume that for a general quotient
\varphi_{\alpha}
, all elements of
\mathcal{F}
satisfy the property
\mathcal{P}
such as non-vanishing and
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F_{i}),\varphi_{\alpha}(F_{j}))\in{\mathbb{K}}[z]
etc. Therefore we may assume that the results proved in this section apply to elements of any finite set
\mathcal{F}
and
general quotients
\varphi_{\alpha}
.

6.1
Lifting from general quotients

As part of our inductive strategy, we would like to show that if a general quotient
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})
of a SG-configuration
\mathcal{F}
has small vector space dimension then
\mathcal{F}
must also have small dimension. In this section we prove that we can indeed lift the bounds on dimensions under a general quotients. We prove our lifting result for strong quotients of polynomial rings and generalize previous results on quadratics
[
51
, Claim 2.26]
and for forms in a polynomial ring
[
49
, Proposition 2.11]
.

Let
V\subset R
be a
B
-strong vector space. Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
be a homogeneous basis of
V
. Then the graded subalgebra
{\mathbb{K}}[V]
is isomorphic to the polynomial ring
{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
via the isomorphism
y_{i}\mapsto F_{i}
. Note that if
{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
is the standard polynomial ring with
\deg(y_{i})=1
, then the isomorphism above is not a homomorphism of graded rings. However, if we consider
{\mathbb{K}}[y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}]
with the grading
\deg(y_{i})=\deg(F_{i})
, then we have an isomorphism of graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebras. Therefore, in the results below we use the grading described above to compute the dimensions of the graded factors and obtain the dimension bounds.

Lemma 6.8
.

Fix positive integers
d,d_{1},\cdots,d_{N}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
be the polynomial ring with the grading
\deg(x_{i})=d_{i}
and
z
be a new variable with
\deg(z)=1
.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, let
I_{\alpha}=(x_{1}-\alpha_{1}z^{d_{1}},\cdots,x_{n}-\alpha_{n}z^{d_{n}})
in
S[z]
.
Let
\varphi_{\alpha}:S[z]\rightarrow S[z]/I_{\alpha}
be the quotient ring homomorphism.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subset S_{\leq d}
be a finite set of homogeneous polynomials of positive degree.
Suppose there exists
D\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})\right\})\leq D
for general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
Then we have

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq d^{2}(1+d)^{2n+2}D.

Proof.

Note that it is enough prove that
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\cap S_{\ell}\right\})\leq\ell(1+\ell)^{2n+2}D
for all
\ell\in[d]
.
Therefore, without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathcal{F}\subset S_{d}
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\cdots,F_{t}\}\subset S_{d}
.
We will show that
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq d(1+d)^{2n+2}D
.
Consider the graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-subalgebras given by
R={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
and
A={\mathbb{K}}[x_{n+1},\cdots,x_{N}]
.
For any
k\geq 1
, let
r_{k}
denote the number of distinct monomials of degree
k
in
R
.
Note that
r_{k}\leq(1+k)^{n}\leq(1+d)^{n}
for all
k\in[d]
.
Let
M^{(k)}_{1},\cdots,M^{(k)}_{r_{k}}
denote these monomials that span
R_{k}
.

Consider
F_{i}
as a polynomial in
A[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
.
Let
F_{i}^{(0)}\in A
be the degree zero part of
F_{i}
as a polynomial in
A[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
. For
k\geq 1
, let
F^{(k)}_{ij}\in A
denote the coefficient of the degree
k
monomial
M^{(k)}_{j}
in
F_{i}
. Note that if
F^{(k)}_{ij}\neq 0
, then it is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
d-k
in
A
. Let
V_{d}=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{i}^{(0)}|i\in[t]\right\}
and for
k\in[d-1]
we let
V_{d-k}=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F^{(k)}_{ij}|i\in[t],j\in[r_{k}]\right\}
. Let

V=V_{1}+\cdots+V_{d-1}+V_{d}
. Then we have
\mathcal{F}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V,x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
. We will show that
\dim(V_{d-k})\leq r_{k}D
for all
k\in[d-1]
and
\dim(V_{d})\leq D
.

Fix
k\in[d-1]
. For simplicity, let
F_{ij}
denote the forms
F^{(k)}_{ij}
and
M_{j}
denote the monomials
M^{(k)}_{j}
as defined above.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{t}
, we have
\varphi_{\alpha}(F_{i})\in A[z]
and the coefficient of
z^{k}
in
\varphi_{\alpha}(F_{i})
is given by
M_{1}(\alpha)F_{i1}+\cdots+M_{r_{k}}(\alpha)F_{ir_{k}}
. For a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{t}
, we know that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi(\mathcal{F})\right\}\leq D
. Therefore the dimension of the span of the coefficients of
z^{k}
in all the
\varphi(F_{i})
is at most
D
, i.e. we have
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{M_{1}(\alpha)F_{i1}+\cdots+M_{r_{k}}(\alpha)F_{ir_{k}}|i\in[t]\right\}\leq D
. By
Lemma

A.4
, we may choose
r_{k}
number of general points
\alpha^{(1)},\cdots,\alpha^{(r_{k})}\in{\mathbb{K}}^{r_{k}}
, such that the matrix
\mathcal{M}
defined by
\mathcal{M}_{ij}=M_{i}(\alpha^{(j)})
is invertible. Then we have
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{ij}|i\in[t],j\in[r_{k}]\right\}\leq r_{k}D
. Furthermore, since
F_{i}^{(0)}\in A
, we know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(F_{i}^{(0)})=F_{i}^{(0)}
and
\dim(V_{d})\leq\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(F_{i}^{(0)})|i\in[t]\right\})\leq D
.

Let
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
and
k\in[d-1]
.
By the above argument, we know that the coefficients
\{F^{(k)}_{ij}\}
of the degree
k
monomials
M^{(k)}_{1},\cdots,M^{(k)}_{r_{k}}
in
F_{i}
’s span a vector space of dimension at most
r_{k}D
.
Hence, the span of
\{M_{\ell}F_{ij}|i\in[m],j,\ell\in[r_{k}]\}
is of dimension at most
r_{k}^{2}D
. For
k=d
, the coefficients of the degree
d
monomials
M^{(d)}_{1},\cdots,M^{(d)}_{r_{d}}
in
F_{i}
are constants. For
k=0
, we know that span of the parts that are independent of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
has dimension given by
\dim(V_{d})\leq D
. Therefore,
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq D+\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}r_{k}^{2}D+r_{d}\leq d(1+d)^{2n+2}D
, since
r_{k}\leq(1+d)^{n}
for all
k\in[d]
.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
d,e\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
1\leq d\leq e
. Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space generated by forms
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}
. Let
R=S/(U)
. Let
V\subset R_{\leq e}
be a
B
-lifted strong vector space with basis
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}\in R
. Let
\varphi_{\alpha}:R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
be a graded quotient as defined in
Definition

6.1
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subset R_{\leq d}
be a finite set of homogeneous elements.
Suppose that there exists
D\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})\right\}\leq D
for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.
Then

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq d^{2}(1+d)^{2n+2}D\cdot\Pi_{i=1}^{t}\deg(H_{i})\cdot\Pi_{j=1}^{n}\deg(F_{j}).

Proof.

Note that
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
and hence
R
is a Cohen-Macaulay UFD, by
Remark

5.13
.
Also,
R[z]
and
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
are quotients of
S[z]
by
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequences and hence by Cohen-Macaulay UFDs by
Proposition

6.3
.
By
Proposition

5.14
, we know that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R
.
Thus,
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n},z
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
R[z]
.
We may extend
F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}
to a homogeneous system of parameters
F_{1},\cdots,F_{N-t}
of
R
such that
\deg(F_{i})=1
for
n+1\leq i\leq N-t
.
Let
A={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{N-t}]
,
G_{i}=F_{i}-\alpha_{i}z^{\deg(F_{i})}\in A[z]
for
i\in[n]
and
J_{\alpha}=(G_{1},\cdots,G_{n})\subset A[z]
.
Let
\vartheta_{\alpha}:A[z]\rightarrow A[z]/J_{\alpha}
be the quotient map.
We have that
A\subset R
is a module-finite free extension by
Theorem

4.10
.
Furthermore, the rank of
R
as a free
A
-module is
r:=\mathrm{rank}(R)=\Pi_{i=1}^{t}\deg(H_{i})\cdot\Pi_{j=1}^{n}\deg(F_{j})
by
Proposition

4.13
. By tensoring, we obtain that
A[z]\subset R[z]
and
A[z]/J_{\alpha}\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
are module-finite free extensions of rank
r
. Therefore, we have

R[z]/I_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\sim}(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
. Let
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\subset R[z]
have components
(f_{i1},\cdots,f_{ir})\in(A[z])^{\oplus r}
under this isomorphism. Note that we must have
f_{ij}
are homogeneous and
\deg(f_{ij})\leq\deg(F_{i})
in
A[z]
, by
Proposition

A.3
. We have that
\varphi_{\alpha}:R[z]\rightarrow R[z]/I_{\alpha}
commutes with the quotient map
\vartheta_{\alpha}^{\oplus r}:(A[z])^{\oplus r}\rightarrow(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
via the isomorphisms above, i.e. the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[z]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[z]/I_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[z])^{\oplus r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\vartheta_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}}

Now we know that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})\right\}\leq D
in
R[z]/I_{\alpha}
. Therefore, for any
j\in[r]
, we have
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\vartheta_{\alpha}(f_{ij})|i\in[m]\right\}\leq D
in the
j
-th component of
(A[z]/J_{\alpha})^{\oplus r}
. Note that
A={\mathbb{K}}[F_{1},\cdots,F_{n}]\subset R
is a graded polynomial ring.
By applying
Lemma

6.8
, we conclude that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{f_{ij}|i\in[m]\right\}\leq d^{2}(1+d)^{2n+2}D
. Therefore, we have

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq d^{2}(1+d)^{2n+2}Dr=d^{2}(1+d)^{2n+2}D\cdot\Pi_{i=1}^{t}\deg(H_{i})\cdot\Pi_{j=1}^{n}\deg(F_{j}).

∎

6.2
General Quotients and Sylvester-Gallai Configurations

We show that radical SG-configurations are preserved under general quotients.
Moreover, if a
{d}\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration is contained in an ideal generated by a strong vector space, then we can reduce the degree of the configuration.

Definition 6.10

(Reduction of a form)
.

Let
R
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra which is a UFD.
For any
F\in R
we define a reduction of
F
to be a generator of the principal ideal
\rad(F)
in
R
.
In other words if
F=f_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots f_{r}^{e_{r}}
is an irreducible factorization of
F
, then
f_{1}\cdots f_{r}
is a reduction of
F
.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subset R
be any subset consisting of elements of positive degree.
We define
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{red}}
to be a set of pairwise non-associate elements in
R
such that for any
F\in\mathcal{F}
we have that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{red}}
contains a reduction of
F
.

Remark 6.11
.

(a) By abuse of notation we will denote any choice of a reduction of
F
as
F_{\textnormal{red}}
.
Note that
F_{\textnormal{red}}
is well-defined up to multiplication by elements of
{\mathbb{K}}
.

(b) Let
\mathcal{F}\subset R
be a finite set of homogeneous elements.
Suppose there is a homogeneous element
z\in R_{1}
such that for all
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
we have
\gcd(F,G)=z^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
. Then
|\mathcal{F}_{\textnormal{red}}|=|\mathcal{F}|-|\mathcal{F}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[z]|+1
.

For the rest of the section, we fix positive integers
d,e,\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
1\leq d\leq e
and
\eta\geq 3
.
Let
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
denote an ascending function such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
i\in[e]
, where
A(\eta,i)
is the function provided by
Theorem

5.7
.
For any
a\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
t_{a}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be the translation function defined as
t_{a}(\delta)=(\delta_{1}+a,\cdots,\delta_{e}+a)
.
Let
C_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
and
h_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be the functions provided by
Lemma

5.15
.

Throughout this section we will use the following notations. Let
S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
and
y
a new variable.
Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space with dimension sequence
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
, generated by forms
H_{1},\cdots,H_{t}
and let
R=S/(U)
. Let
z\in R_{1}
. Let
V\subset R_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space of dimension
n+1
containing
z
. We choose a basis
z,G_{1},\cdots,G_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq e}
and for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n+1}
, we let
I_{\alpha}=(V_{\alpha})=(z-\alpha_{1}y,G_{n}-\alpha_{1}y^{\deg(G_{1})},\cdots,G_{n}-\alpha_{n}y^{\deg(G_{n})})
.

Proposition 6.12
.

With the notations as above, suppose that
V\subset R_{\leq e}
is
(h_{B}\circ t_{2})
-lifted strong vector space.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subset R
be a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
Let
\varphi_{\alpha}:R[y]\rightarrow R[y]/I_{\alpha}
be the graded quotient. Then we have

1.

the set
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\mathrm{red}}\subset R[y]/I_{\alpha}
is a
({d},y,R[y]/I_{\alpha})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration for general
\varphi_{\alpha}
.

2.

Suppose that for all
zP\in\mathcal{F}\cap R_{d+1}
we have
P\in(V)
. Then we have that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a
({d-1},y,R[y]/I_{\alpha})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration for general
\varphi_{\alpha}
.

3.

Suppose there exists
D\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that we have a global bound
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\mathrm{red}}\right\}\leq D
, for general
\varphi_{\alpha}
. Then

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq(d+1)^{3}(d+2)^{2n+2}(D+1)\cdot\Pi_{i=1}^{t}\deg(H_{i})\cdot\Pi_{j=1}^{n}\deg(G_{j}).

In particular, we have

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq(d+3)^{3n+6}e^{t}D=(d+3)^{3\dim(V)+6}e^{\dim(U)}D.

Proof.

1. Note that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
is a finite set of square-free homogeneous elements of degree at most
d+1
in
R[y]/I_{\alpha}
. Since
z\in V
, we have that
\varphi_{\alpha}(z)=\beta y
for some non-zero
\beta\in{\mathbb{K}}
. We know that
V
is also
B
-lifted strong, since
h_{B}\circ t_{2}\geq B
. Since
\mathcal{F}
is a finite set, we may assume that for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we have
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\neq 0
for any
F\in\mathcal{F}
, by
Proposition

6.5
. We may assume that
y\in(\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}))_{\textnormal{red}}
, by changing the representative of
\varphi_{\alpha}(z)
in the reduction. Now we prove that the defining conditions for a
({d},y,R[y]/I_{\alpha})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration hold.

(a)
For any two elements
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
we know that
\gcd(F,G)=z
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(z)=\beta y
. Therefore, by
Proposition

6.6
we have
\gcd(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))=y^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
. In particular, for any
F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\in\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
we have that
\gcd(F^{\prime},G^{\prime})=y
.

(b) For any two distinct
F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\in\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
, there exists distinct
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
such that
F^{\prime}=(\varphi_{\alpha}(F))_{\textnormal{red}}
and
G^{\prime}=(\varphi_{\alpha}(G))_{\textnormal{red}}
. Now, there exists
H\in\rad(F,G)\cap\mathcal{F}
such that
H\neq F,G
, as
\mathcal{F}
is a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration. Then,
\varphi_{\alpha}(H)\in\rad(\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(G))
, since
\varphi_{\alpha}
is a ring homomorphism. Now we have
H^{\prime}=(\varphi_{\alpha}(H))_{\textnormal{red}}\in\rad(F^{\prime},G^{\prime})\cap\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
. If
F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\neq y
, then
F,G\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
. If
H\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
, then we have
H^{\prime}=y\neq F^{\prime},G^{\prime}
. By
Proposition

6.6
, we know that the only common factor of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F),\varphi_{\alpha}(H)
is
y
. If
H\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
, then we have
H^{\prime}\neq y
, by
Proposition

6.5
. Therefore we conclude that
H^{\prime}=(\varphi_{\alpha}(H))_{\textnormal{red}}\neq(\varphi_{\alpha}(F))_{\textnormal{red}}=F^{\prime}
. Similarly,
H^{\prime}\neq G^{\prime}
. Therefore, we have that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
is a
({d},y,R[y]/I_{\alpha})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.

2. Suppose that for all
zP\in\mathcal{F}\cap R_{d+1}
, we have
P\in(V)=(z,G_{2},\cdots,G_{n})
.
Note that
\varphi_{\alpha}(G_{i})=\alpha_{i}y^{\deg(G_{i})}
in
R[y]/I_{\alpha}
.
Since
\mathcal{F}\subset(z)
by definition, we see that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})\subset(y^{2})
as
\varphi_{\alpha}(z)=\beta y
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(G_{i})\in(y)
for all
i
. Therefore, we have
\deg(\varphi_{\alpha}(F)_{\textnormal{red}})\leq d
for any
F\in\mathcal{F}
. Since
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
is a
({d},y,R[y]/I_{\alpha})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, by part (1). We conclude that since
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}
is a
({d-1},y,R[y]/I_{\alpha})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.

3. Recall that
\mathcal{F}\subset R_{\leq d+1}
and we have
\mathcal{F}\subset(z)
. By definition of a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, we know that
\mathcal{F}
consists of square-free homogeneous elements. For a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we know that for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
, the only multiple factor of
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)
is of the form
y^{r}
, by
Proposition

6.6
. Note that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})\cap{\mathbb{K}}[y]\subset\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{y,y^{2},\cdots,y^{d+1}\right\}
. On the other hand, for any
F\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[y]
, we have
\varphi_{\alpha}(F)=y^{r}P
where
P
is square free and
y\not\mid P
. Thus

\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(F)\setminus{\mathbb{K}}[y]\right\}\subset\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{y^{r}P\mid r\in[d],y\not\mid P,yP\in(\varphi_{\alpha}(F))_{\textnormal{red}}\right\}.

Therefore, for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, we have
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})\right\})\leq d+1+dD<(d+1)(D+1)
, as
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F})_{\textnormal{red}}\right\})\leq D
. Hence, by applying
Proposition

6.9
to
\mathcal{F}\subset R_{\leq d+1}
, we have

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq(d+1)^{3}(d+2)^{2n+2}(D+1)\cdot\Pi_{i=1}^{t}\deg(H_{i})\cdot\Pi_{j=1}^{n}\deg(G_{j}).

∎

In the proof of
Theorem

1.8
, we will apply general quotients successively to eventually reduce the degree of the SG-configuration. In
Corollary

6.13
, we generalize
Proposition

6.12
to compositions of general quotients and show that we can indeed lift bounds on dimensions of SG-configurations.

Composition of general quotients.
Recall that
1\leq d\leq e\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
R=S/(U)
, where
U\subset R_{\leq e}
is a graded vector space.
Let
z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell}
be new variables and
n_{0},\cdots,n_{\ell-1}
be positive integers. Suppose we have a sequence of graded quotients and Sylvester-Gallai configurations defined inductively as follows.

1.

Let
R^{(0)}=R
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{(0)}
be a
({d},z_{0},R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration for some
z_{0}\in R
.

2.

For some
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
, suppose that we have defined
R^{(k)}
and a
({d},z_{k},R^{(k)})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}
. We define
\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}
iteratively as follows:

(a)

Suppose that
W^{(k)}\subset R^{(k)}_{\leq e}
is a
h_{B}\circ t_{2}
-lifted strong graded vector space such that
z_{k}\in W^{(k)}
and
\dim(W^{(k)})\leq n_{k}
.

(b)

We define
R^{(k+1)}=R^{(k)}[z_{k+1}]/(W^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}})
and
\varphi_{\alpha^{(k)}}:R^{(k)}[z_{k+1}]\rightarrow R^{(k+1)}
as the graded quotient defined by a general
\alpha^{(k)}\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W^{(k)})}
, for which the conclusions of
Proposition

6.12
hold.

(c)

We define
\mathcal{F}^{(k+1)}=(\varphi_{\alpha^{(k)}})_{\textnormal{red}}\subset R^{(k+1)}
, which is a
({d},z_{k+1},R^{(k+1)})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration by
Proposition

6.12
.

Note that we assume the existence of a vector space
W^{(k)}\subset R^{(k)}
which is
h_{B}\circ t_{2}
-lifted strong. In particular
U\subset S
must be sufficiently strong to begin with. In the following result, we show that if we have a bound on the dimension of
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
for compositions of general quotients as above, then we can lift it to a bound on the dimension of
\mathcal{F}^{(0)}
.

Corollary 6.13
.

Let
1\leq\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. With the notations as above, for all
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
, we may write

R^{(k+1)}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k+1}]/(U^{(k)}),

where
U^{(k)}
is a vector space such that
\dim(U^{(k)})=\dim(U)+\dim(W^{(0)})+\cdots+\dim(U^{(k)})
.
Furthermore, suppose there exists
D\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})_{\mathrm{red}}\right\}\leq D
for all choices of general
\alpha^{(0)},\cdots,\alpha^{(\ell-1)}
as above.
Then,

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{(0)}\right\}\leq(d+3)^{6\ell+3(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}n_{k})}\cdot e^{(\ell\dim(U)+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-k-1)n_{k})}\cdot D.

Proof.

Given
{\mathbf{a}}=(\alpha^{(0)},\cdots,\alpha^{(\ell-1)})
and
W^{(0)}_{\alpha^{(0)}},\cdots,W^{(\ell-1)}_{\alpha^{(\ell-1)}}
as above, we let
\widetilde{W}^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}}\subset S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}]
be the vector space spanned by homogeneous lifts of a homogeneous basis of
W^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}}\subset R^{(k)}
, for all
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
. We define
U^{(k)}_{{\mathbf{a}}}=U+\widetilde{W}^{(0)}_{\alpha^{(0)}}+\cdots+\widetilde{W}^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}}
in
S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k+1}]
. Note that we have
R^{(k+1)}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k+1}]/(U^{(k)}_{{\mathbf{a}}})
for all
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
. By
Proposition

5.6
, we know that
\dim(W^{(k)}=\dim(W^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}})
for all
k
. Now we have
\dim(W^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}})=\dim(\widetilde{W}^{(k)}_{\alpha^{(k)}})
for all
k
. Hence we have

\dim(U^{(k)}_{{\mathbf{a}}})=\dim(U)+\dim(W^{(0)})+\cdots+\dim(U^{(k)})\leq\dim(U)+n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k}

for all
k
. Therefore we may take
U^{(k)}=U^{(k)}_{{\mathbf{a}}}
.

Now we prove the dimension bound by induction on
\ell
. If
\ell=1
, then by applying
Proposition

6.12
to the general quotient
\varphi_{\alpha^{(1)}}
, we obtain that

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{(0)}\right\}\leq(d+3)^{6+3n_{0}}\cdot e^{\dim(U)}\cdot D.

Therefore we may assume that
\ell\geq 2
and that the statement holds for all
k\leq\ell-1
. By applying
Proposition

6.12
to
R^{(\ell-1)}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}]/(U^{(\ell-2)}_{{\mathbf{a}}})
and the general quotient
\varphi_{\alpha^{(\ell-1)}}
we obtain that

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{(\ell-1)}\right\}\leq D^{\prime}:=(d+3)^{6+3n_{\ell-1}}\cdot e^{\dim(U)+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}n_{k}}\cdot D.

Since the statement holds for
k=\ell-1
, we obtain by induction that

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{(0)}\right\}\leq(d+3)^{6(\ell-1)+3(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}n_{k})}\cdot e^{((\ell-1)\dim(U)+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-3}(\ell-k-2)n_{k})}\cdot D^{\prime}.

Therefore we have

\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{(0)}\right\}\leq(d+3)^{6\ell+3(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}n_{k})}\cdot e^{(\ell\dim(U)+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-k-1)n_{k})}\cdot D.

∎

7
Main Result

In this section, we prove the radical Sylvester-Gallai theorem for forms of degree at most
d
.
For the rest of this section, we will denote our
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}=\{z,zF_{1},\cdots,zF_{m}\}
, where
m:=|\mathcal{F}|-1
and use the convention from
Remark

3.8
to denote for any
0\leq j\leq d
:

\mathcal{F}_{j}:=\{F\in R_{j}\mid zF\in\mathcal{F}\}\text{ and }m_{j}:=|\mathcal{F}_{j}|.

Also, for any
j>1
we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{<j}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathcal{F}_{i}
. Moreover, for an element
F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
, we define the following sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{span}(F)

\displaystyle:=\{G\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\ \mid\ |\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,G\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}_{d}|\geq 3\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)

\displaystyle:=\{P\in\mathcal{F}_{<d}\ \mid\ z,P,F\text{ regular sequence in $R$, and }(F,P)=\rad(F,P)\}

Note that for
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
, we have
|\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,G\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}_{d}|\geq 3
iff
|\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{zF,zG\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}|\geq 3
.

Proposition 7.1
.

With the definitions as above, the following holds.

1.

Any two elements
P,Q\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{F}_{i}
do not have any common factors in
R
.

2.

Suppose
F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
and
P\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
. Then there exists
G\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
such that
G\in(F,P)
.

Proof.

1. Recall that
\mathcal{F}
is a set of non-associate square-free homogeneous elements and
\gcd(F_{i},F_{j})=z
for any
i\neq j
. Therefore, if
P,Q\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{F}_{i}
, i.e.
zP,zQ\in\mathcal{F}
, then
P,Q
have no common factors.

2. We have
zF,zP\in\mathcal{F}
. By definition of
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configurations, there exists
zG\in\mathcal{F}
such that
zG\in\rad(zF,zP)
and
zG\neq zF,zP
. As
P\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
, we have that
z,P,F
is a regular sequence. By
Lemma

4.5
we have that
G\in\rad(F,P)
. As
P\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
, we have
G\in(F,P)=\rad(F,P)
. Since
\deg(P)<d=\deg(F)
by assumption, we see that
\deg(G)=d
, as
G,P
do not have any common factors by part (1).
∎

We define the set
\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)

\displaystyle:=\{G\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\ \mid\ \exists\ P\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)\text{ s.t. }G\in(F,P)\}

By
Proposition

7.1
, we know that if
\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
is non-empty, then
\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)
is also non-empty.

7.1
Preparatory lemmata

We fix positive integers
d,e,\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
1\leq d\leq e
and
\eta\geq 3
.
We let
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
denote an ascending function such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
i\in[e]
. Here
A(\eta,i)
is the function given by
Theorem

5.7
.
For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
t_{n}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be the translation by
n
function defined as
t_{n}(\delta)=(\delta_{1}+n,\cdots,\delta_{d}+n)
.
Let
C_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
and
h_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be the functions defined in
Lemma

5.15
.
We first prove the linear case of our main theorem below.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space.
Suppose
U
is
B\circ t_{2}
-strong.
Consider the graded unique factorization domain
R:=S/(U)
.
Let
z\in R_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{z,za_{1},\cdots,za_{m}\}\subset R_{\leq 2}
be a
({1},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
Then
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq 26
.

Proof.

We may assume that
m\geq 4
. Consider the
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space
R_{1}
and the set
\mathcal{G}=\{z,a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}
. We will show that
\mathcal{G}
is a
(1,1/3)
-linear Sylvester-Gallai configuration in
R_{1}
. Indeed, let
a_{i},a_{j}\in\mathcal{G}
where
i\neq j
. Since
U
is
B\circ t_{2}
-strong in
S
, we know that
(a_{i},a_{j})
is a prime ideal in
R
by
Proposition

5.10
. If
z,a_{i},a_{j}
is not a regular sequence in
R
, then
z
is a zero-divisor in
R/(a_{i},a_{j})
and hence we have
z\in(a_{i},a_{j})
. As
R
is a graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, we conclude that
z\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{a_{i},a_{j}\right\}
. Now suppose that
z,a_{i},a_{j}
is a regular sequence in
R
. Then, there exists
za_{k}\in\rad(za_{i},za_{j})
for some
k\neq i,j
. Hence, we have
a_{k}\in\rad(a_{i},a_{j})
by
Lemma

4.5
. Since
(a_{i},a_{j})
is prime in
R
and
a_{i},a_{j},a_{k}\in R_{1}
, we must have that
a_{k}\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{a_{i},a_{j}\right\}
. Note that
|\mathcal{G}|=m+1
and
m-1\geq\frac{2}{3}m
as
m\geq 4
. Hence, for all
a_{i}
there exist at least
\frac{2}{3}m
number of
a_{j}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
|\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{a_{i},a_{j}\right\}\cap\mathcal{G}|\geq 3
. Therefore,
\mathcal{G}
is a
(1,\frac{1}{3})
-linear Sylvester-Gallai configuration in
R_{1}
by
Proposition

3.6
. Therefore
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{G}\right\})\leq 25
by
Proposition

3.5
. Therefore,
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{za_{1},\cdots,za_{m}\right\})\leq 25
and
\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq 26
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a
B
-strong graded vector space in
S
.
Consider the graded UFD
R=S/(U)
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
For any
F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
, we have

|\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)|\leq 2^{d^{2}}\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)|\leq 2^{d^{2}}\cdot m_{d}.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)\subset\mathcal{F}_{d}
, it is enough to prove the first inequality. We may assume that
\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
is non-empty, otherwise the inequality is trivially satisfied. Let
\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\}
.
Note that
\deg(P_{i})<d=\deg(F)
by definition.
If
(F,P_{i})=(F,P_{j})
for some
i,j\in[k]
, then
P_{i}\in(F,P_{j})
.
As
\deg(P_{i})<d
, we must have
P_{i}\in(P_{j})
.
Hence we must have
i=j
, since
P_{i},P_{j}
do not have any common factors for
i\neq j
, by
Proposition

7.1
.
Therefore, we have that
(F,P_{i})\neq(F,P_{j})
for all
i\neq j
.

By
Proposition

7.1
, we know that for every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
, there exists at least one
G\in\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)
such that
G\in(F,P_{i})
. Let us fix such a choice of
G_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)
for each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
. If
|\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)|>2^{d^{2}}\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)|
, then by the pigeonhole principle, there exist at least
2^{d^{2}}
number of
P_{i}
’s in
\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
such that the corresponding
G_{i}
is the same element, say
G\in\mathcal{F}_{rad}(F)
. Therefore, there exist at least
2^{d^{2}}
number of
P_{i}
’s in
\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
such that
G\in(F,P_{i})=\rad(F,P_{i})
. By
Lemma

5.19
, there exist at least two distinct
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
such that
\rad(F,P_{i})=\rad(F,P_{j})
. Hence there exist
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)
such that
(F,P_{i})=(F,P_{j})
for some
i\neq j
. This is a contradiction as
(F,P_{i})\neq(F,P_{j})
for all
i\neq j
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space and let
R=S/(U)
.
Let
z\in R_{1}
and
W\subset R_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space.
Suppose that
W
is
h_{2B}\circ t_{1}
-lifted strong with respect to
U
and we have
z\in W
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.

1.

Suppose
|\mathcal{F}_{d}|=m_{d}>6d^{3}
. If
|\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|\geq\frac{2m_{d}}{3}
, then
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
.

2.

If
|\mathcal{F}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|>(d+1)m_{d}+d^{2}(2d-1)
, then
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]=:\{z,zP_{1},\ldots,zP_{k}\}
. By
Remark

5.13
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
U
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence in
S
and that
R
is a graded UFD. Furthermore, by
Proposition

5.14
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
W
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence (and also a prime sequence) in
R
. Note that, by
Lemma

4.19
, we must have
P_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
for all
i\in[k]
. Suppose there is
F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus(W)
.
Let
V\subset R
be the
B
-lifted strong graded vector space constructed by applying
Corollary

5.17
to
W
and
F
. In particular,
W\subset V
and
F\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
. By
Proposition

5.14
, we know that any homogeneous basis of
V
is an
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
-sequence (and also a prime sequence) in
R
. In particular,
W
and
V
have homogeneous bases containing
z
, that form a prime sequences in
R
. Note that
z,P_{i},F
is a regular sequence in
R
, by
Proposition

5.18
. By applying
Corollary

4.31
to
F
and the algebras
{\mathbb{K}}[W]\subset{\mathbb{K}}[V]
, we have that
(F,P_{i})
is not radical for at most
d^{2}(2d-1)
elements in
\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}\}
. Now we prove (1) and (2) below.

1. Suppose
\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]\geq\frac{2m_{d}}{3}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
P_{1},\cdots,P_{r}\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]
for some
r\geq\frac{2m_{d}}{3}
. Furthermore, we can say that
(F,P_{i})
is radical for all
i\leq r-d^{2}(2d-1)
.
Now, the SG condition implies that there is
zG_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{zP_{i},zF\}
such that
zG_{i}\in\rad(zP_{i},zF)
. Let us fix such a choice of
G_{i}
for each
P_{i}
where
i\in[r]
. Since
z,P_{i},F
is a regular sequence in
R
, we have that
G_{i}\in\rad(F,P_{i})
by
Lemma

4.5
.
As
(F,P_{i})
is radical for
1\leq i\leq r-d^{2}(2d-1)
, we have
G_{i}\in(F,P_{i})
for all such
i
. Since
\deg(F)=d
, we must have
\deg(G_{i})=d
, as
G_{i},P_{i}
do not have common factors by
Proposition

7.1
. In particular,
G_{i}\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,P_{i}\right\}\cap\mathcal{F}_{d}
. Note that since
F\not\in(W)
and
P_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
, we must have that
G_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus(W)
. If
G_{i}=G_{j}
for some
i\neq j\in[r]
, then we have
G_{i}=G_{j}\in(F,P_{i})\cap(F,P_{j})
. Since all of
G_{i},G_{j},F,P_{i},P_{j}\in R_{d}
, we must have that
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,P_{i}\right\}=\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F,P_{j}\right\}
. This is contradiction since
P_{i},P_{j}
do not have common factors and
F\not\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
, whereas
P_{i},P_{j}\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
.
Therefore,
G_{i}\neq G_{j}
for
1\leq i\neq j\leq r-d^{2}(2d-1)
. Note that
G_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus(W)
for all
i\in[r]
and
|\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus(W)|\leq|\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus{\mathbb{K}}[W]|\leq\frac{m_{d}}{3}
. Now we have

r-d^{2}(2d-1)>\frac{2m_{d}}{3}-2d^{3}>\frac{m_{d}}{3},

as
m_{d}>6d^{3}
. Hence, by the pigeonhole principle, there exists
1\leq i\neq j\leq r-d^{2}(2d-1)
such that
G_{i}=G_{j}
. This is a contradiction. Therefore, there does not exist
F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus(W)
and we have
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
.

2. Recall that
(\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{F}_{i})\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]=\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}\}
and
k>(d+1)m_{d}+d^{2}(2d-1)
. By the argument in the first paragraph, without loss of generality, we can say that
(F,P_{i})
is radical for all
i\leq k-d^{2}(2d-1)
.
Now, the SG condition implies that there is
zG_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{zP_{i},zF\}
such that
zG_{i}\in\rad(zP_{i},zF)
. Since
z,P_{i},F
is a regular sequence in
R
, we have that
G_{i}\in\rad(F,P_{i})
by
Lemma

4.5
.
As
(F,P_{i})
is radical for
1\leq i\leq k-d^{2}(2d-1)
, we have
G_{i}\in(F,P_{i})
for all such
i
.

Since
\deg(F)=d
, we must have
\deg(G_{i})=d
, as
G_{i},P_{i}
do not have common factors by
Proposition

7.1
. In other words,
G_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus\{F,P_{i}\}
for
1\leq i\leq k-d^{2}(2d-1)
. Note that
k-d^{2}(2d-1)>(d+1)m_{d}
and
|\mathcal{F}_{d}|=m_{d}
by assumption. Therefore, by the pigeonhole principle, there exists
G\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
such that
G\in\rad(F,P_{i})
for at least
(d+1)
number of
P_{i}
’s. Without loss of generality, suppose that
G\in\rad(F,P_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq(d+1)
. Since
G,F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
and
\rad(F,P_{i})=(F,P_{i})
, we may write
G=\alpha_{i}F+P_{i}A_{i}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
and
A_{i}\in R_{d-\deg(P_{i})}
. If
\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{j}
for some
i\neq j\leq(d+1)
, then we have that
(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})F=P_{j}A_{j}-P_{i}A_{i}\in(P_{i},P_{j})\subset(W)
. This is a contradiction, since
F\not\in(W)
. Therefore, we must have that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq(d+1)
. Therefore, we have
G-\alpha_{1}F=P_{i}A_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq(d+1)
. Now
\deg(G-\alpha_{1}F)=d
and
P_{i},P_{j}
do not have any common factors for
i\neq j
, by
Proposition

7.1
. Therefore, we have that
\Pi_{i=1}^{d+1}P_{i}
divides
G-\alpha_{1}F
, which is a contradiction. Hence we must have that
F\in(W)
and hence
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset(W)
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mu\in(0,1)
and
k\geq\lceil\frac{37d^{3}}{\mu}\rceil
.
Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space with dimension sequence
\delta_{U}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
.
Let
\widetilde{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be such that
\widetilde{B}\geq h_{2B}\circ t_{1}
. Suppose
U
is
h_{2\widetilde{B}}\circ t_{k}
-strong in
S
. Consider the graded unique factorization domain
R=S/(U)
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration. Suppose that
2d^{3}<m_{d}
. Then there exists a
\widetilde{B}
-lifted strong vector space
W\subset R_{\leq d}
such that the following holds:

1.

\dim(W_{i})\leq C_{2\widetilde{B},i}(t_{k}(\delta_{U}))

2.

z\in W

3.

|\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|>2d^{3}
.

4.

|\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap(W)|\geq(1-\mu)\cdot m_{d}
.

5.

Furthermore, if
2d^{3}<m_{d}<\varepsilon m
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{d^{3}2^{d^{2}+3}}
, then

|\mathcal{F}_{<d}\cap(W)|\geq(1-\varepsilon d^{3}2^{d^{2}+2})\cdot m\geq\frac{m}{2}.

Proof.

First we will show that we can construct a
\widetilde{B}
-lifted strong vector space
W\subset R
satisfying properties (1)-(4) above.
Note that the vector space
(0)\subset R
is
h_{2\widetilde{B}}\circ t_{k}
-lifted strong.
Let
r:=2d^{3}
and
\nu:=\dfrac{\mu}{2r}
.
Suppose
\mathcal{F}_{d}
is an
({r},{\nu})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration.
By
Proposition

3.5
,

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}_{d}\right\})\leq 2d^{3}+1+\frac{8}{\nu}=2d^{3}+1+\frac{32d^{3}}{\mu}<\frac{36d^{3}}{\mu}<k

Therefore we may apply
Corollary

5.17
to the vector space
(0)\subset R
and a basis of
\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}_{d},z\right\}
to obtain a
\widetilde{B}
-lifted strong vector space
W\subset R_{\leq d}
such that
z\in W
,
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[W]
and
\dim(W_{i})\leq C_{2\widetilde{B},i}(t_{k}(\delta_{U}))
. We also have
|\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|=|\mathcal{F}_{d}|>2d^{3}
.
This proves (1)-(4) in this case.

Hence we may assume that
\mathcal{F}_{d}
is not a
({r},{\nu})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration. Consider the set

\mathcal{H}=\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\mid|\mathcal{F}_{span}(F)|<2\nu m_{d}\}.

If
|\mathcal{H}|\leq r
, then
\mathcal{F}
is an
({r},{\nu})\textnormal{\!-linear-SG}
configuration, by
Proposition

3.6
.
Therefore we must have that
|\mathcal{H}|>r
. In particular, there exist
r+1
elements in
\mathcal{F}_{d}
, say
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r+1}
, such that
|\mathcal{F}_{span}(F_{i})|<2\nu m_{d}
for all
i\in[r+1]
.
Let
{\mathcal{B}}:=\mathcal{F}_{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{r+1}\mathcal{F}_{span}(F_{i})
. Suppose for some
G\in{\mathcal{B}}
and
i\in[r+1]
, we have that
z,F_{i},G
is a regular sequence and
(F_{i},G)
is a radical ideal. Now there exists
zH\in\mathcal{F}
such that
zH\in\rad(zF_{i},zG)
. Since
z,F_{i},G
is a regular sequence by assumption, we have that
H\in\rad(F_{i},G)=(F_{i},G)
. Hence we have
H\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
as
F_{i},G\in R_{d}
and thus
H\in\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{F_{i},G\right\}
.
This is a contradiction since
G\not\in\mathcal{F}_{span}(F_{i})
.
Therefore, for any
G\in{\mathcal{B}}
and
i\in[r+1]
, we must have that
z,F_{i},G
is not regular or
(F_{i},G)
not radical.

Since
r+2=2d^{3}+2<k
, we may apply
Corollary

5.17
to the vector space
(0)\subset R
and the set
z,F_{1},\cdots,F_{r+1}
to obtain a
\widetilde{B}
-lifted strong vector space
W\subset R_{\leq d}
such that
z,F_{1},\cdots,F_{r+1}\in{\mathbb{K}}[W]
and
\dim(W_{i})\leq C_{2\widetilde{B},i}(t_{k}(\delta_{U}))
.
Note that
|\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|\geq r+1>2d^{3}
. We will show that
{\mathcal{B}}\subset(W)
.

Fix
G\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Since
\widetilde{B}\geq h_{2B}\circ t_{1}
, we may apply
Corollary

5.17
to
W
and
G
to obtain a
B
-lifted strong vector space
V\subset R
such that
G\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
and
W\subset V
. Therefore we may apply
Corollary

4.31
to
G
and
W\subset V
in
R
. Thus, if
G\not\in(W)
, then
(G,F_{i})
is not radical for at most
d^{2}(2d-1)
number of elements in
{\mathbb{K}}[W]
by
Corollary

4.31
. Since
r>d^{2}(2d-1)
, we must have that
z,G,F_{i}
is not a regular sequence for some
i\in[r+1]
. This is a contradiction since
z,F_{i},G
is a regular sequence in
R
, by
Proposition

5.18
. Therefore we must have that
G\in(W)
and hence
{\mathcal{B}}\subset(W)
. Now we have

|{\mathcal{B}}|\geq m_{d}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\mathcal{F}_{span}(F_{i})|\geq m_{d}-r\cdot 2\nu m_{d}=(1-\mu)\cdot m_{d}.

Now we prove property (5).
Let
W\subset R
be a
\widetilde{B}
-lifted strong vector space satisfying (1)-(4) above.
In particular,
|\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|>2d^{3}=r
.
We may assume that
F_{1},\cdots,F_{r}\in\mathcal{F}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]
.
By
Lemma

7.3
, we know that
|\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)|\leq 2^{d^{2}}m_{d}
for all
F\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{<d}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F_{i})
.
Note that

|\mathcal{G}|\geq m-m_{d}-r2^{d^{2}}m_{d}\geq m-(1+2d^{3}2^{d^{2}})\varepsilon m\geq(1-\varepsilon d^{3}2^{d^{2}+2})\cdot m\geq\frac{m}{2},

since
|\mathcal{G}_{rad}(F)|\leq 2^{d^{2}}m_{d}
and
m_{d}<\varepsilon m
and
\varepsilon d^{3}2^{d^{2}+2}<\frac{1}{2}
.
We will show that
\mathcal{G}\subset(W)
.
Let
G\in{\mathcal{B}}
.
Since
\widetilde{B}\geq h_{2B}\circ t_{1}
, we may apply
Corollary

5.17
to
W
and
G
to obtain a
B
-lifted strong vector space
V\subset R
such that
G\in{\mathbb{K}}[V]
and
W\subset V
.
Therefore we may apply
Corollary

4.31
to
G
and
W\subset V
in
R
.
Thus, if
G\not\in(W)
, then
(G,F_{i})
is not radical for at most
d^{2}(2d-1)
number of elements in
{\mathbb{K}}[W]
by
Corollary

4.31
.
Since
r>d^{2}(2d-1)
, we must have that
z,G,F_{i}
is not a regular sequence for some
i\in[r]
.
This is a contradiction since
z,F_{i},G
is a regular sequence in
R
, by
Proposition

5.18
.
Therefore we must have that
G\in(W)
and hence
\mathcal{G}\subset(W)
.
∎

7.2
Proof of the main theorem

We are now ready to prove our main technical theorem on radical Sylvester-Gallai configurations.

7.2.1
Definitions of
\Lambda_{d,e}
,
\lambda_{d,e}
and auxiliary functions

We now introduce some auxiliary functions and notation that we will use in
Theorem

1.8
.

We fix positive integers
e\geq 2
and
\eta\geq 3
. We let
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
1\leq d\leq e
.
For all positive integers
d\leq e
, we will inductively define ascending functions
\Lambda_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
and
\lambda_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
, which shall appear in the statement of
Theorem

1.8
. The functions
\Lambda_{d},\lambda_{e}
will depend on both
d
and
e
. We will omit
e
from the notaion for simplicity.
For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
t_{a}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be the translation by
n
function defined as
t_{a}(\delta)=(\delta_{1}+a,\cdots,\delta_{e}+a)
.
We fix an ascending function
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
\delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
and
i\in[e]
, where
A(\eta,i)
are the functions in
Theorem

5.7
.
Let
C_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
and
h_{B}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
be the functions defined in
Lemma

5.15
.

Potential function
\phi
.

Let
U\subset S_{\leq e}
be a graded vector space in
S
such that
R=S/(U)
is an UFD.
Let
z\in R_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}
be a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration. We define the following potential function:

\phi(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}j\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{j}|.

Note that for any
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}
we have
0<\phi(\mathcal{F})\leq d|\mathcal{F}|
.
The potential function is a weighted measure of the size of the configuration
\mathcal{F}
.
In the proof of
Theorem

1.8
, we will show that the potential of a Sylvester-Gallai configuration drops under suitably chosen general quotients (defined in
Section

6
).
We will use this to reduce a
({d},z,R)\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration to a
({d-1},y,R^{\prime})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration where
R^{\prime}
is a general quotient of
R
.

The parameters
\varepsilon_{d}
and
k_{d}
.

Let
d\geq 2
.
We choose and fix a rational number
\varepsilon_{d}\in(0,1)
such that
\varepsilon_{d}<\frac{1}{d^{3}2^{d^{2}+3}}
.
Let
k_{d}=\left\lceil\frac{(5d)^{3}}{\varepsilon_{d}}\right\rceil
.
Note that
\varepsilon_{d}
and
k_{d}
satisfy the numerical hypotheses of
Lemma

7.5
with
\mu=\frac{\varepsilon_{d}}{3}
as
k_{d}\geq\lceil\frac{37d^{3}}{\mu}\rceil
.
We have
k_{d}>6d^{3}
by definition, and we note that
(1-8d(d+2)\varepsilon_{d})\geq\frac{1}{4}
.
We will use these facts in the proof of
Theorem

1.8
.

The functions
\Lambda_{d}
and
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
.

For all positive integers
2\leq d\leq e
, we will inductively define ascending functions
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
for
0\leq\ell\leq 8d+1
, and use the functions
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
to define the ascending function
\Lambda_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
.
Recall that
B:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
is an ascending function such that
B_{i}(\delta)\geq A(\eta,i)+3(\sum_{i}\delta_{i}-1)
for all
i\in[e]
, as defined above.

For
d=1
, we let
\Lambda_{1}=2B\circ t_{2}
. For
d\geq 2
, suppose that
\Lambda_{d-1}
has been defined.
We define
\Lambda_{d}
and
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
for
0\leq\ell\leq 8d+1
as follows.

Let
\Lambda^{(8d+1)}_{d}=h_{2\Lambda_{d-1}}\circ t_{k_{d}}
.
Suppose
\Lambda^{(\ell+1)}_{d}
has been defined for some
\ell\in[0,8d]
.
We inductively define
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}=h_{2\Lambda^{(\ell+1)}_{d}}\circ t_{k_{d}}
.
Given the definition of
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
for all
\ell\in[0,8d]
, we define
\Lambda_{d}=h_{2\Lambda^{(0)}_{d}}\circ t_{k_{d}}
.

Recall that
h_{G}\geq G
and
h_{G}\circ t_{k}\geq h_{G}
for any function
G:{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}^{e}
.
Since
\Lambda_{1}=h_{2B}\circ t_{2}
, we have
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}\geq h_{2B}\circ t_{2}
for all
d\leq e
and
\ell\in[0,8d+1]
by induction.
In the proof of
Theorem

1.8
, we will use the
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
functions to lower bound the strengths of iteratively constructed vector spaces.

The functions
\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{d}
and
\Gamma_{d-1}
.

For all
0\leq\ell\leq 8d+1
, we will define the functions
\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
and
\Gamma_{d-1}:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
inductively as follows:

\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{d}(n)=\max\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{e}C_{2\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d},i}(t_{k_{d}}(\delta))\ \ |\ \ \delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\text{ with }\sum_{i=1}^{e}\delta_{i}\leq n\right\}

\Gamma_{d-1}(n)=\max\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{e}C_{2\Lambda_{d-1},i}(t_{k_{d}}(\delta))\ \ |\ \ \delta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{e}\text{ with }\sum_{i=1}^{e}\delta_{i}\leq n\right\}.

Note that
\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{d}
and
\Gamma_{d-1}
are non-decreasing functions.
In the proof of
Theorem

1.8
, we will use the functions
\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{d}
and
\Gamma_{d-1}
to bound the dimensions of iteratively constructed vector spaces.

The functions
\lambda_{d}
.

Given
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
n_{0}=n+\Gamma^{(0)}_{d}(n)
and inductively define

n_{k}=\Gamma^{(k)}_{d}\left(n+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}n_{k}\right)

for all
k\leq 8d+1
and finally let
n^{\prime}=n+\Gamma_{d-1}(n+\sum_{k=0}^{8d+1}n_{k})
.
For all
d\geq 1
, we define a function
\lambda_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
as follows.

For
d=1
, let
\lambda_{1}=26
.
For
d\geq 2
, let
D(n)=\lambda_{d-1}(n+n_{0}+\cdots+n_{\ell-1}+n^{\prime})
and

\lambda_{d}(n)=(d+3)^{6(8d+2)+3(\sum_{k=0}^{8d+1}n_{k}+n^{\prime})}\cdot e^{((8d+2)n+\sum_{k=0}^{8d}(8d+1-k)n_{k}+n^{\prime})}\cdot D(n).

In
Theorem

1.8
, we show that the functions
\lambda_{d}
provide upper bounds on the dimension of the span of any radical Sylvester-Gallai configuration of degree
d
.

Remark 7.6
.

Note that the functions
\Lambda_{d},\Lambda_{d}^{(\ell)},\Gamma_{d}^{(\ell)},\Gamma_{d-1},\lambda_{d},\lambda_{d}^{(\ell)}
defined above depend on the integer
e
that we fixed in the beginning of this section. We omit
e
from the notation for simplicity. In the proof of
Theorem

1.8
below, we will fix the integer
e
and consider these functions for
d\in[1,e]
. We also note that all these functions defined above are ascending functions.

7.2.2
Main theorems

We begin with our main technical theorem, which proves bounds on radical Sylvester-Gallai configurations over quotients of the polynomial ring by sufficiently strong ideals.
We then show how the radical Sylvester-Gallai theorem over polynomial rings follows from our technical theorem.

See
1.8

Proof.

Fix an integer
e\geq 2
.
For all positive integers
d\leq e
, let
\Lambda_{d}
,
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
,
\lambda_{d}
,
\varepsilon_{d}
and
k_{d}
be defined as in
Section

7.2.1
. We let
\Lambda_{d,e}:=\Lambda_{d}
and
\lambda_{d,e}:=\lambda_{d}
. We will show that these functions satisfy the conclusion.

Suppose
d=1
. Since
\Lambda_{1}=h_{2B}\circ t_{2}
, we have that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}\leq\lambda_{1}(\dim(U))=26
by
Proposition

7.2
. If
d\geq 2
, we will construct a sequence of general quotients to eventually reduce
\mathcal{F}
to a
(d-1)
-radical Sylvester-Gallai configuration and then apply
Corollary

6.13
together with the inductive bound given by
\lambda_{d-1}
.

From now on we assume that
d\geq 2
.
For
0\leq\ell\leq 8d+1
, we define a graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra
R^{(\ell)}
, a
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
-lifted strong vector space
W^{(\ell)}\subset R^{(\ell)}
and a
({d},z_{\ell},R^{(\ell)})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
inductively as follows.

1.

Set
R^{(0)}=R
,
U^{(0)}=U\subset S
,
z_{0}=z
and
\mathcal{F}^{(0)}=\mathcal{F}
.

2.

Suppose that for some
\ell\in[0,8d]
, we have defined a quotient ring
R^{(\ell)}
, a
({d},z_{\ell},R^{(\ell)})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
with
z_{\ell}\in R^{(\ell)}_{1}
and
U^{(\ell-1)}\subset S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell}]
.

If
m^{(\ell)}_{d}:=|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}|>6d^{3}
:

2.1.

Apply
Lemma

7.5
with
\mu=\frac{\varepsilon_{d}}{3}
and the constant
k=k_{d}
, to obtain a
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
-lifted strong vector space
W^{(\ell)}\subset R^{(\ell)}
such that
z_{\ell}\in W^{(\ell)}
and
|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap(W^{(\ell)})|\geq(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}}{3})\cdot m^{(\ell)}_{d}
.

2.2.

Let
z_{\ell+1}
be a new variable. We let
R^{(\ell+1)}:=\frac{R^{(\ell)}[z_{\ell+1}]}{(W^{(\ell)}_{\alpha})}
and
\varphi_{\alpha}:R^{(\ell)}[z_{\ell+1}]\rightarrow R^{(\ell+1)}
be the quotient defined by a general
\alpha^{(\ell)}\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W^{(\ell)})}
for which the conclusions of
Proposition

6.12
hold.

2.3.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)}=(\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}))_{\textnormal{red}}
as defined in
Section

6.2
.

2.4.

Let
U^{(\ell+1)}\subset S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell+1}]
be the vector space given by
Corollary

6.13
, such that we have
R^{(\ell+1)}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}]/(U^{(\ell)})
.

Note that
\Lambda^{(\ell-1)}_{d}\geq h_{2\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}}\circ t_{k_{d}}
and
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}\geq h_{2B}\circ t_{1}
.
Therefore,
Lemma

7.5
applies in Step 2.1. and we get that
W^{(\ell)}
is
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
-lifted strong with respect to
U^{(\ell)}
.
By
Proposition

5.6
, we know that the vector space
W^{(\ell)}_{\alpha}
is also
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}
-lifted strong in
R[z_{\ell+1}]
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W^{(\ell)})}
.
Note that
\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d}>h_{B}\circ t_{2}
.
Therefore, by
Proposition

6.12
, we conclude that
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)}=(\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}))_{\textnormal{red}}
is a
({d},z_{\ell+1},R^{(\ell+1)})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W^{(\ell)})}
.
Note that the definitions of
R^{(\ell)}
and
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}
depend on the choice of the general
\alpha^{(\ell)}\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W^{(\ell)})}
.
We omit this from the notation for simplicity.

Now we will show that the size of
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}
significantly reduces every time we increase
\ell
.
In particular we have the following.

Claim:

m^{(\ell)}_{d}\leq 6d^{3}
for some
\ell\leq 8d+1
.

Proof of Claim.

Note that
m^{(\ell)}_{d}\geq m^{(\ell+1)}_{d}
for all
\ell
.
Suppose that
m^{(\ell)}_{d}>6d^{3}
for all
0\leq\ell\leq 8d+1
.
If
|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W^{(\ell)}]|\geq\frac{2m^{(\ell)}_{d}}{3}
for some
\ell\leq 8d
, then by
Lemma

7.4
, we have that
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\subset(W^{(\ell)})
.
Therefore, by
Proposition

6.12
, we see that
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)}
is a
({d-1},z_{\ell+1},R^{(\ell+1)})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
In particular,
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)}_{d}=\emptyset
and
m^{(\ell+1)}_{d}=0
.
Therefore we may assume that
|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W^{(\ell)}]|<\frac{2m^{(\ell)}_{d}}{3}
for all
0\leq\ell\leq 8d
.

Recall that
|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap(W^{(\ell)})|\geq(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{\ell}}{3})\cdot m^{(\ell)}_{d}
by the construction in step 2.1.
Since all elements of
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap(W^{(\ell)})
will be of lower degree after a general quotient and reduction, we have that
m^{(\ell+1)}_{d}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}}{3}m^{(\ell)}_{d}
.
Thus:

m^{(\ell+1)}:=|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)}|=|\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})_{\textnormal{red}}|\geq|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}|-|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W^{(\ell)}]|\geq\frac{m^{(\ell)}_{d}}{3}

as
|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W^{(\ell)}]|<\frac{2m^{(\ell)}_{d}}{3}
.
Therefore, using the inequalities above, we obtain

m^{(\ell+1)}_{d}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\ell+1}}{3}m^{(\ell)}_{d}\leq\varepsilon_{\ell+1}m^{(\ell+1)}

for all
0\leq\ell\leq 8d
. Therefore, by
Lemma

7.5
, we know that

|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)}_{<d}\cap(W^{(\ell+1)})|\geq\frac{m^{(\ell+1)}}{2}

for all
0\leq\ell\leq 8d
, and hence

|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{<d}\cap(W^{(\ell)})|\geq\frac{m^{(\ell)}}{2}

for all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
.
Therefore we have

\phi(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})-\phi(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell+1)})\geq\frac{m^{(\ell)}}{2}

for all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
.
Note that
\phi(\mathcal{F}^{(1)})\leq\phi(\mathcal{F}^{(0)})\leq dm^{(0)}=dm
.
If
m^{(\ell)}\geq\frac{m}{4}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
, then we have that
\phi(\mathcal{F}^{(8d+1)})=0
, which is a contradiction.
Hence, it is enough to prove that
m^{(\ell)}\geq\frac{m}{4}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
.

For all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
, we have

(d+2)\varepsilon_{d}m^{(\ell)}\geq(d+2)m^{(\ell)}_{d}>(d+1)m^{(\ell)}_{d}+d^{2}(2d-1)

since
m^{(\ell)}_{d}\leq\varepsilon_{d}m^{(\ell)}
and
m^{(\ell)}_{d}>2d^{3}
.
If for some
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
, we have
