|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W]|>(d+2)\varepsilon_{d}m^{(\ell)}
, then
\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\subset(W^{(\ell)})
by
Lemma

7.4
.
Thus we have
m^{(\ell+1)}_{d}=0
and
\ell+1\leq 8d+1
, which is a contradiction.
Therefore we must have that
|\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}\cap{\mathbb{K}}[W^{(\ell)}]|\leq(d+2)\varepsilon_{d}m^{(\ell)}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
.
Letting
\gamma=(d+2)\varepsilon_{d}
, we have

m^{(\ell+1)}\geq(1-\gamma)m^{(\ell)}\geq(1-\gamma)^{\ell}m^{(0)}\geq(1-\gamma)^{8d}m\geq(1-8d\gamma)m\geq\frac{m}{4}

for all
1\leq\ell\leq 8d
, where the last inequality holds since
(1-8d(d+2)\varepsilon_{d})\geq\frac{1}{4}
.
This completes the proof of the claim.
∎

Thus we may henceforth assume
m^{(\ell)}_{d}\leq 6d^{3}
for some
\ell\leq 8d+1
.
Let
\ell
be the smallest such number.
Note that
W^{(\ell-1)}\subset R^{(\ell-1)}
is
\Lambda^{(\ell-1)}_{d}
-lifted strong by construction and
\Lambda^{(\ell-1)}_{d}\geq h_{2\Lambda_{d-1}}\circ t_{k_{d}}
.
Therefore the vector space
(0)
is
h_{2\Lambda_{d-1}}\circ t_{k_{d}}
-lifted strong in
R^{(\ell)}
.
As
6d^{3}<k_{\ell}
, we may apply
Corollary

5.17
to obtain a
\Lambda_{d-1}
-lifted strong vector space
W\subset R^{(\ell)}_{\leq e}
such that
z_{\ell},\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)}_{d}\subset{\mathbb{K}}[W]
and
\dim(W_{i})\leq C_{2\Lambda_{d-1},i}(t_{k_{\ell}}(\delta_{U}+\delta_{W^{(\ell)}}))
for all
i\in[e]
.

Let
y
be a new variable. By
Proposition

5.6
, we know that the vector space
W_{\alpha}
is
\Lambda_{d-1}
-lifted strong in
R^{(\ell)}[y]
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W^{(\ell)})}
.
Note that
\Lambda_{d-1}>h_{B}\circ t_{2}
.
Let
R^{\prime}=R^{(\ell)}[y]/(W_{\alpha})
.
By
Proposition

6.12
, we conclude that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})_{\textnormal{red}}
is a
({d-1},y,R^{\prime})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration for a general
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\dim(W)}
.

By construction, we have
R^{(k)}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}]/(U^{(k-1)})
for all
1\leq k\leq\ell
, where
U^{(k-1)}
is given by
Corollary

6.13
.
By
Lemma

7.5
we have,
\dim(W^{(k)}_{i})\leq C_{2\Lambda^{(\ell)}_{d},i}(t_{k_{d}}(\delta_{U^{(k-1)}}))
.
Therefore

\dim(W^{(k)})=\sum_{i=1}^{e}\dim(W^{(k)}_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{e}C_{2\Lambda^{(k)}_{d},i}(t_{k_{d}}(\delta_{U^{(k-1)}}))\leq\Gamma^{(k)}_{d}(\dim(U^{(k-1)})).

By
Corollary

6.13
, we also have
\dim(U^{(k-1)})=\dim(U)+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\dim(W^{(j)})
.
Let
\dim(U)=n
,
n_{0}=n+\Gamma^{(0)}_{d}(n)
and define
n_{k}=n+\Gamma^{(k)}_{d}(n+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}n_{j})
.
By induction and using the inequality above, we have
\dim(W^{(k)})\leq n_{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq\ell
.
Similarly, we may write
R^{\prime}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell},y]/(U^{\prime})
for some
U^{\prime}
.
Then we have

\dim(W)\leq n^{\prime}:=n+\Gamma_{d-1}(n+\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}n_{j})\ \text{ and }\ \dim(U^{\prime})=\dim(U)+\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\dim(W^{(j)})+\dim(W).

We have shown above that for all
\alpha^{(0)},\cdots,\alpha^{(0)},\alpha
such that the conclusions of
Proposition

6.12
hold, we get that
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})_{\textnormal{red}}
is a
({d-1},y,R^{\prime})\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration.
Since
W_{\alpha}\subset R^{(\ell)}
is
\Lambda_{d-1}
-lifted strong, we know that
U^{\prime}\subset S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell},y]
is
\Lambda_{d-1}
-strong in
S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell},y]
.
Therefore by our induction hypothesis, we may apply the theorem to
R^{\prime}=S[z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell},y]/U^{\prime}
and the configuration
\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})_{\textnormal{red}}
and obtain

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\varphi_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{(\ell)})_{\textnormal{red}}\right\})\leq\lambda_{d-1}(\dim(U^{\prime}))\leq\lambda_{d-1}(n+n_{0}+\cdots+n_{\ell-1}+n^{\prime}).

Setting
D:=\lambda_{d-1}(n+n_{0}+\cdots+n_{\ell-1}+n^{\prime})
,
Corollary

6.13
, implies that

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq(d+3)^{6(\ell+1)+3(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}n_{k}+n^{\prime})}\cdot e^{((\ell+1)n+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-k)n_{k}+n^{\prime})}\cdot D.

Hence, by definition of
\lambda_{d}
, we conclude that

\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})\leq\lambda_{d}(n)=\lambda_{d}(\dim(U)).

∎

As a corollary of our technical theorem above, we obtain the radical Sylvester-Gallai theorem below, proving
[
35
, Conjecture 2]
.

See
1.2

Proof.

We define
\lambda(d)=\lambda_{d}(0)
, where
\lambda_{d}:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
is the function given by
Theorem

1.8
. If
\mathcal{F}:=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\}
, where
F_{i}\in S
, and
z
is a new variable (free over
S
),
Proposition

3.9
implies that the set
\mathcal{G}:=\{zF_{1},\ldots,zF_{m}\}
is a
({d},z,S[z])\textnormal{-radical-SG}
configuration, with
U=0
.
Since the zero vector space is arbitrarily strong, we have that
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

1.8
, with the vector space
U=(0)\subset S_{\leq d}
. Hence we have that
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{G}\right\}\leq\lambda(d)
.
As
\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\}=\dim\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{G}\right\}
, we are done.
∎

Using the Stillman uniformity principle, we obtain the following direct consequence of
Theorem

1.2
.

See
1.3

Proof.

Note that by
Theorem

1.2
, we may assume that
I
is generated by at most
\lambda(d)
forms of degree at most
d
. Therefore, the Stillman uniformity type results
[
23
, Theorem 1.1, Proposition 4.6]
imply that each of the ideal invariants of
I
are uniformly bounded by functions of
d
only, independent of
{\mathbb{K}}
and
N
.
∎

Appendix A
Auxiliary algebraic results

We note a few elementary results below which will be used in the previous section of the paper.

We note the relationship between transcendence degree and vector space dimension.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset S={\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}]
be a finite set of forms. Then
\mathrm{trdeg}_{{\mathbb{K}}}(\mathcal{F})\leq\dim(\textnormal{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\left\{\mathcal{F}\right\})
.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
R
be a graded integral domain. Let
F\in R
be a homogeneous element. If
g\in R
and
g
divides
F
, then
g
is homogeneous. In particular, if
R
is a UFD, then all irreducible factors of
F
are homogeneous.

Proof.

Suppose
F=gh
for some
h\in R
. Let
g=g_{d}+g_{d-1}+\cdots+g_{r}
and
h=h_{e}+h_{e-1}+\cdots+h_{s}
be the decomposition into homogeneous parts of
g
and
h
, where
g_{d},g_{r},h_{e},h_{s}\neq 0
, and
g_{r},h_{s}
are the least degree non-zero homogeneous parts of
g
and
h
respectively. Suppose that
g
is not homogeneous, i.e.
d>r
. Since
F
is homogeneous, we must have
g_{d}h_{e}=0
or
g_{r}h_{s}=0
. This is a contradiction as
R
is a domain.
∎

Proposition A.3
.

Let
R
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{N}}
-graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra with
R_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
. Let
A\subset R
be a finitely generated graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-subalgebra. Suppose
A\subset R
is a module-finite free extension of rank
r
and
R
is generated by homogeneous elements
u_{1},\cdots,u_{r}\in R
as a free
A
-module. Consider the
A
-module isomorphism
\phi:R\xrightarrow{\sim}A\cdot e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus A\cdot e_{r}
given by
\phi(u_{i})=e_{i}
. Let
F\in R_{d}
be a homogeneous element of degree
d
. Let
\phi(F)=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{r})\in A^{\oplus r}
. Then
f_{i}
are homogeneous and
\deg(f_{i})\leq d
in
A
and
R
.

Proof.

Suppose
\deg(u_{j})=s_{j}
and
d_{j}=d-s_{j}
. Let
f_{id_{j}}
be the degree
d_{j}
homogeneous part of
f_{i}
in
A
and
f_{i,\neq d_{j}}=f_{i}-f_{id_{j}}
. Now we have
F=f_{1}u_{1}+\cdots+f_{r}u_{r}
in
R
. Since
F
is homogeneous of degree
d
, we must have
F=f_{1d_{1}}u_{1}+\cdots+f_{rd_{r}}u_{r}
and
f_{1,\neq d_{1}}u_{1}+\cdots+f_{r,\neq d_{r}}u_{r}=0
. Since
u_{1},\cdots,u_{r}
generate
R
as a free
A
-module, we must have that
f_{i,\neq d_{j}}=0
for all
j\in[r]
. In particular,
f_{i}=f_{i,d_{j}}
is homogeneous of degree
d_{j}\leq d
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}:=\{M_{i}|i\in[r]\}
be a set of monomials in
S
of degree at most
d
.
Let
x^{(j)}=(x^{(j)}_{1},\cdots,x^{(j)}_{n})
be new variables for
j\in[r]
.
If
M
is the
r\times r
matrix with
(i,j)
-th entry given by
M_{ij}=M_{i}(x^{(j)})
, then
\det(M)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let
M_{i}={\mathbf{x}}^{{\mathbf{u}}_{i}}
, where
{\mathbf{u}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
is the exponent vector of
M_{i}
.
Since
\deg(M_{i})\leq d
, we have
\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{ik}\leq d
.
Let
D=2d+1
and consider the vector
{\mathbf{e}}:=(1,D,D^{2},\ldots,D^{n-1})
.
Then the inner products
\mu_{i}:=\langle{\mathbf{e}},{\mathbf{u}}_{i}\rangle
are such that

\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(u_{ik}-u_{jk})D^{k-1}\neq 0

whenever
i\neq j
.
Hence, after relabeling, we can assume that
\mu_{1}>\mu_{2}>\cdots>\mu_{r}
.
In particular, we can write
\mu_{i}=\lambda_{i}+(r-i)
for some decreasing sequence of integers
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r}
.

Thus, if we consider the natural ring homomorphism given by
\varphi(x^{(j)}_{k})=y_{j}^{D^{k-1}}
, where
y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}
are new variables, we obtain that
\varphi(M)
is a generalized Vandermonde matrix corresponding to the partition
\lambda:=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
, and therefore we have that

\varphi(\det(M))=\det(\varphi(M))=s_{\lambda}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})\cdot\prod_{i<j}(y_{i}-y_{j})

which implies
\det(M)\neq 0
.
∎
